HARVARD COLLEGE LIBRARY AN ANONYMOUS GIFT 

JAN 5 1933 Printed in the United States of America

MR. PHILLIP TOBE, MONTEFIORE HOSPITAL, NEW YORK CITY. 

Dear Phil:

You have been down into the abyss. Life has made you a martyr. Yet you sing her hymn of praise. Having suffered, you are one of the wisest of men.

From across the bottom of the abyss I greet you—you, Phil, genius of savoir vivre, friend, Dionysian.

And I dedicate to you these pages whereon is written my own poor hymn of joy. Sincerely,

D. V. C.

CHAPTER I 

I was born at the close of the nineteenth century on a farm which bordered the St. Lawrence River. The fact might be elaborated by the addition of such circumstances as: that four generations of my ancestors were American born; that God only knows how many generations preceded them in Holland, England, Germany, Scotland and the early cradles of eighteenth century Europe; that my mother was attended at the time of my birth by a Dr. Adams who wore a black beard; and that the water for my first bath was heated on the old wood-burning cookstove in the kitchen.

I came into this life at the time that my mother was approaching forty. My father was affecting a walrus mustache and playing bass-horn in the village band. I do not doubt that the period during which the attentions of the family were largely concentrated on me was very short indeed, for I was the fourth child in a family which achieved the fecundity of six children. I was neither wicked nor angelic enough to outshine the rest of them. "You had," said my mother, “a nice disposition . . . and 

I don't know when I ever seen such big blue eyes." Which information never particularly startled me because I was very willing to admit the first and frequently annoyed

with reminders of the latter on innumerable occasions after the beginnings of my first self-consciousness. That annoyance must remain my only claim to prophetic vision, for its presence foretold, in those tender years, the appearance and reputation of naïveté which was forever to follow me on account of those eyes which were blue and so uncommonly big; an annoyance, too, which was anything but funny during those periods when one was positive of his own perfect sophistication . . . and others, inexplicably, were not.

My first recollection is of an episode which I can hardly offer as evidence of my good disposition; though the incidents which preceded it, and those which followed soon after, were, past doubt, splendid examples of the justice which a boy of four may render the amenities of social relations. Unfortunate, indeed, it is, then, that I should remember of all things the passion which possessed me that afternoon when my cousin Bessie waved a cookie in front of me which she had just purloined from the kitchen. I seized it and ran and, being a practical person, jammed as much of it as I could into my mouth before she overtook me. And then, at the moment that she repossessed it, I ceased being a defendant and became-most vociferouslya plaintiff who had been abused beyond all that it was human to bear. I pleaded my case with frenzy, vocally, contortionally with my back on the ground and my heels in the air, with shrieks and kicks and all such manner of acting as I deemed would most forcibly convince my mother that I was on the verge of being murdered. But when my mother ran out of the kitchen and took me in her arms I was at a loss to explain either the nature of my torture or the justice of my cause. And thus faced with the new discomfort of embarrassment I straightway told my mother 2

that Bessie had taken my cookie. And with what shame

I have always recalled that incident! Enough, anyway, to shake my belief and confound my faith in all the Freudian theories of concealing memory to the contrary.

CHAPTER II 

It seemed as though the family had been talking about moving to Potsdam "since time whereof the memory of men runneth not to the contrary." In the winter, when the snow was deep and the pump had frequently to be thawed out with a teakettle full of hot water, Mother had said again and again: "Well, thank the Lord, if we ever live in Potsdam, a person won't have to bundle themselves up as though they were goin' to the North Pole just to get a pail of fresh water!" And she would put on some stormrubbers, throw a shawl about her and bustle out to the pump with the teakettle and a pail. These incidents were not so frequent, since they occurred only when "the men" were not at the house. And usually before they would leave for the barn or the woods, the house was furnished with all that might be needed until they returned. 

My mother would not think of letting me, at seven, struggle with a teakettle full of scalding water and a big iron pump. I could shovel snow, carry in wood, crack nuts, and occasionally throw down hay for the horses, but from all traffickings with scalding water I was forbidden, because my mother had once known of a baby which had been scalded to death by falling into its mother's mop-pail. 

It was in the spring that my father spoke most colorfully of moving to Potsdam-when the weather was slow to break and very changeable, and the ground was slushy and muddy under foot, and there was little for him to do except putter around the barns or fuss around the house. 5

"I'll bet by another year," he would say, "I won't be wasting my time puttering around here with nothing to do, waiting for the weather to break." And then he would bluster about the house like a chicken with its head cut off. 

When my oldest brother Mathew would come back for a holiday from Potsdam, where he was attending the Normal School, it seemed as though the whole family spoke of nothing else at the supper-table except moving to Potsdam. It was to be the beginning of wealth, happiness, education, and comfort for all of us-to say nothing of the greater spiritual benefit which would come from being able to attend church more frequently. 

Grandfather's was the only dissenting voice. He liked the farm and was satisfied to stay there with occasional visits to Potsdam. "Amos," he would say to my father, “I'd like to know how you calculate to git along in the village with a lot o' yo' ones. You'll find it mighty different, havin' to pay out money for food and wood and tryin' to git along on carpenter's wages, than raisin' most everythin' you need right here. The farm's the only place to raise a family anyway." But Grandfather's words about such matters were not given much consideration by Father. And even at that age my mother had already whispered to me the reason— 

Grandfather drank!! It might have been because of his hard experiences in the Civil War, but that did not quite excuse him. Moreover, Papa had to scold him every once in a while for swearing in front of the children. And it was well known among all of us how "too disgusting" was his habit of chewing tobacco.

But, most of all, Mother wanted to move to Potsdam, because it would mean a better education for the children. 

She hoped one day that one of us would become a Methodist minister and she had named us all against that

event. Mathew was now sixteen and was finishing his first year at the Potsdam Normal. Mark would be thirteen in the fall and ready to go to high school. These circumstances, together with the facts that Ruth, who had just become ten, had already missed many days at the country school because the weather had been so bad that she couldn't get there; and that I had created in the family a disquieting skepticism concerning the virtues of the country school by coming home one day with the assurance that thunder was not made by God but by the bumping together of clouds—a phenomenon of natural philosophy of which my teacher had that very day told me-made it, indeed, seem necessary that we live in Potsdam. 2 My father was a quick, energetic, and indefatigable man who had, during the years of toil, emerged from the burdens of an ill-equipped and heavily mortgaged farm, and a sizeable and increasing family to support, to that state of restive confidence which strained for more ambitious fields. Having "raised the mortgage" on the farm, improved the place to a condition of excellence which rivaled the best properties in the neighborhood, and sent his oldest son off to Normal School, he was convinced-more or less justly -that he could accomplish greater things if he moved into the village. In his youth he had apprenticed himself as a carpenter and he never tired of telling of the high opinion. which his former employer had of his abilities as a craftsman. "Well," he would say, "I think this is a pretty good illustration of what he thought of me. He sent me to take charge of one of his very particular jobs once where there were men working who were twice my age. And he told

me that if I had any complaints to make about any of the men I could either bring them to him or fire them on the spot-just as I chose." His eyes would sparkle with pride and my mother would look at him with that worshipful look which through years of upsets and difficulties she was never quite able to lose. To me, Papa then represented a stern example of almost unattainable virtue. He was one to be respected. 

There had been no silver spoons in the life of my father.

When he was still a young boy he had to leave school and work on a farm in order to support his mother. His own father had returned from the Civil War, restless, wandering, and undependable-contributing to the family's welfare only during those fickle and sparse intervals between his excursions to Ogdensburg and Montreal in search of vinous adventures. And because of the example of my grandfather, I suppose, my father became unusually seriousminded and responsible. At twenty, in addition to supporting my grandmother, he had saved a little money, learned to play the bass-horn, acquired a certain facility in singing barytone, mastered the ponderous verbosities of the Wesleyan interpretation of the Holy Scriptures, and earned a reputation among the God-fearing people of his native community as a hard-working, splendid young man. But at twenty, too, he had begun to feel the first stirrings of that same restless spirit which animated the peregrinations of my grandfather. He went to Colorado, riding all the way in day-coaches and spending the nights at cheap hotels in order to see the country. In those closing years of the 

Seventies he settled in Boulder and became a waiter in a hotel dining-room. Before a year was up, and he was to return back home to his native St. Lawrence, he had been promoted to steward and had emerged from the wicked

temptations of the fast life in a western hotel of that period unscathed by alcohol, tobacco, loose women or bad language.

So it was not on account of a strangeness to town life or an ignorance of experience which made my father so eager to get off the farm and to try his luck in the village. 

He was now forty years old. His years on the farm had been successful. Quite naturally he wanted to be on the move to a domain of greater opportunity. But beyond that natural and expected explanation of his yearning to move to Potsdam there was, I suspect, an even greater urge that ever-disturbing and stimulating memory of the circumstance that he had once, however temporarily, been the boss over a gang of carpenters in the thrilling enterprise of building a house. And that momentary feeling of importance, of power, of the sense of creation, once tasted, had left him forever after with the pain of an appetite unsated and unforgotten. So now, after all those years since 

1893, when he had moved to the farm that he might be sure of food and shelter for his family, he was going back with the unfaltering determination to take up where he had left off this business of building which had so insistently lured him. 3 And thus that fall we moved to Potsdam. The Holland family of seven eager souls emerging from the cradle of its rural beginnings. . . . Father Amos, Mother Etta, Mathew,

Mark, Ruth, Peter and John . . . with our formidable 

Biblical nomenclature, our rigorous Wesleyan discipline, and our two favorite horses, Sally and Frank.

CHAPTER III 

I SHALL never be sure that it was due to the imperfections of my critical faculties that I then thought Potsdam to be the most well-favored and lovely town in the whole world. 

Certainly in that connection at least I shall always be inclined to favor those theorists who maintain that the unstudied criticism of youth is the only judgment that is truly sincere and thus worth a whoop.

Tall elm trees and shady maples, friendly unpaved streets and flagstone sidewalks! Houses with uncrowded yards of lawns and hedges, orchards and gardens surrounding them! Peaceful brownstone churches modestly retiring behind their garments of ivy! The dignified Normal School with its mansard roof and the park of majestic trees which made its campus! Market Street, with its easy-going merchants and modest shop-windows, and its colorful hotel with the balcony on the second story where the band used to play on Saturday nights during the summer! What a crowd of delights and perfections come tumbling through my mind!

To disentangle from these happily mixed threads of my memory that single, delicate strand of my first impressions upon coming into Potsdam! How impossible! It was late,

I know, of an autumn evening and I was riding on top of a wagonload of cordwood with my oldest brother. The hours which we had spent in traversing the sixteen miles from the farm had seemed interminable to me. For the load was heavy, and there were plenty of hills, and the 

II

horses had never gone faster than a good walk. So when

I first saw the spire of the Catholic church I was overjoyed with the thought that we were at last to get into town and I was to see for the first time the house in which we were going to live. That house about which I had heard so much—with pipes running through it which furnished water without going outdoors, and a thing called a bathtub in which one could almost go swimming.

On the outskirts of the town I saw some boys playing football, and I watched, fascinated by the novelty and fun of the game. I was so excited about everything that I finally had to talk. "What are they doing?" I asked Mathew. "Playing football," he said, simply. He had long ago grown rather tired of my unceasing questions. 

Nevertheless I had to go on talking. "Football! Football!" I said under my breath. And then presently exclaimed: "Gee, I would like to play football!" "Thought you said you wanted to learn to play the trombone," commented Mathew with a tone in his voice which implied that I couldn't do both. 

Then, having a problem to solve as to why I couldn't do both, I remained silent for several minutes. Presently the bell in the Catholic church thundered out five heavy strokes. I straightway began to ask more questions. Then suddenly, without a word of warning, my brother turned the horses into the driveway of the grayest-looking house on the street. 

We were home!

I know that I was piercingly disappointed over the drabness of the house's exterior-especially after having passed so many bright and good-looking homes on the street along which we had just been riding; but that disappointment 12

was quickly forgotten in the excitement of actually arriv ing and seeing the rest of the family in the new surroundings.

I jumped down from the wagon and ran to the kitchen door. And there was my mother, whom I hadn't seen for seven whole hours, taking fresh loaves of bread out of the oven and hustling about the kitchen as though nothing could upset her from her beloved ritual, be it new houses, new towns, the topsy-turviness of moving or whatever else. (And nothing ever could.) I plunged my face into the center of her warm gingham apron and threw my arms about her waist, for she was a familiar sight in this new world of unfamiliar disorder. 

The first thing she said was: "How's my darlin'? I'll bet you're hungry." Then she led me over to the sink, showed me how magically one could get hot water just by turning a faucet. "Now wash your hands and I'll give you a nice piece of fresh-baked bread and butter. That'll stay your stomach and won't spoil your supper," she said.

Armed with a piece of bread and butter, I began to explore the house. In the beginning I noticed that the ceilings of the rooms were ever so much higher than those on the farm. Next I discovered the front door, with its vari-colored panes of glass, and for many moments I was fascinated with the changing appearance of the world outside when viewed through the red and the blue and the colorless glass. I decided at length that such a wonderful door partly made up for the lack of both clapboards and paint on the outside of the house.

But the greatest wonder awaited me upstairs. At the end of the hall there was a little room which contained a huge, long, painted tub with faucets. I tried them with great excitement. Finally I came to a bowl with a seat on it. 13

Its rustic equivalent had possessed enough similarity to make me suspect its purpose. A long chain with a handle on it dangled temptingly in front of me. I seized it and gave a pull. Immediately there was a loud, strange, and frightening noise. I fled in terror, still clutching my piece of bread, downstairs into the kitchen. "There's a flood! There's a flood!" I tried to explain. 

My brother Mathew dashed back up the stairs with me to investigate. To my great surprise, the thing was again as quiet and calm as I had first seen it. My brother laughed uproariously, pulled the chain again and demonstrated the working of my first water-closet. Thus occurred the birth of a growing and affected nonchalance to all the mechanical wonders of civilization which I was ever after to encounter.

Downstairs again, I peered out the front windows overlooking the street. Directly opposite was a large white house with a big screened porch on the side and a smooth driveway running down the edge of its well-kept lawn. 

Along the driveway there was a boy about my size riding a strange-looking machine which I learned later was called a tricycle. I compared the appearance of the boy with myself: I with my well-worn overalls and faded shirt and he with his dark-blue suit and polished shoes. I was immoderately impressed with his ease in riding back and forth on the tricycle. I began to feel envious and uncomfortable. I didn't realize then that this boy had never ridden on a horse's back, nor managed a hay-rake all alone, as I had. I could only think of how superior he must feel with his fine suit and his ability to ride a tricycle. 

Unwilling to face the discomfort of that comparison any longer, I turned away from the window and decided 14

that I would go out to the barn. Once out the back door, everything seemed much better. There was a big orchard in the back yard and the trees were loaded with apples. 

The barn was new and very nice-looking. I walked into it proudly and gazed with satisfaction at the fine-looking horses that had brought us all the way from the farm. I thrilled with pride when I peeked under a huge tarpaulin in the carriage-house and saw a brand-new buggy which my father had just purchased.

But still I had to ask my mother why our house didn't look like the others. "Well, now, darlin'," she said, "if you're not too impatient, it will look like the others." She added with a determined smile, "And I wouldn't be at all surprised if it looked a plagued sight better one of these days.” 2 That night we ate supper in the kitchen, because the dining-room things were not unpacked. There were seven of us crowded around the fall-leaf table: Papa, Mama, Mathew, Mark, Ruth, John and I. After Papa had said grace very impressively, as befitted our first meal in the new house, we all ate hungrily and talked with excitement. There were endless comparisons of the new life which was hardly a day old, with that which we had been accustomed to for seven years on the farm. 

It was astonishing-the difference and wonder of everything. Fancy-looking carriages were passing by on the street; lights were appearing in the neighbors' windows; people were walking by even after dark, with the bright street-light down at the corner shining so brilliantly. It 15

was all so fascinating that I didn't want to go to bed. But 

Mama kept saying: "Come now, darlin', you're all tired out. And there'll be another day tomorrow."

It was a long time after the light was blown out before 

I could get to sleep. 

Oh, first and golden September of the twentieth century, where has the magic of your transient days forever fled! 16

CHAPTER IV 

On the Sunday following our arrival in Potsdam we all went to church: an imposing procession with Ruth and me in front, Mathew and Mark following, and Father and 

Mother with three-year-old Johnny hanging onto their hands.

Our arrival was, of course, the signal for curious glances, surreptitious smiles and approving nods from the more Christian members of the congregation. But when I turned around on going into the pew and saw my father again standing so straight and stiff in his Sunday clothes, white tie, and tall collar, I felt less embarrassed at the big auditorium and the large crowd of people looking at us. He was so important-looking.

Presently, after the first excitement, I began to realize that the one thing that did most to impress me of the size, the grandeur and the wonder of the place was the sonorous, majestic music from the pipe-organ! I had never before heard anything larger than the gaudily carved and decorated harmonium in the country church. This instrument seemed so huge and all-powerful, so magnificently noisy that it appeared in its harsher moments to be a good representation of the voice of God. I liked it; it made church service a little more capable of being borne. In those days anything which could do that was a welcome boon, for I had at that time come to look upon church services as the punishment and penance for my sins during the previous week. It seemed to me that when anything took 17

place which might possibly mitigate the maddening tediousness and torturing confinement of those services, the occasion was a trustworthy sign that God had relaxed a moment from his forbidding severity and was actually getting to be a little bit jolly and kind. 

I had to blunder along believing that “Our Father, Who art in Heaven" was, after all, just a super and omnipotent edition from which my own father was derived-austere, anthropomorphic, terribly just and exacting. The fact that 

He had such inscrutable sermons and prayers said in His name and demanded such painful restrictions of one's activities on Sundays, and that He had such a big, dull, black-covered book written about Him, were characteristics comparable to my father's insistences that I get up so early in the morning, that I should not wade in mud puddles with my shoes and stockings on, that I work around the house when I wanted to go out and play, and that I should sit up so straight and eat so deliberately at the table. I always wanted to curry favor with both of them, but my efforts were seldom successful, due perhaps to the circumstance that those very, very good deeds which I so ostentatiously performed were so often succeeded by violations of Good Behavior. That is, if by an extra errand or some self-denial I happened to come to the end of the week with a penny which I had not spent, I would occasionally and with much to-do put it in the Sunday School collection together with the one which my father customarily gave me for that purpose. Certainly the Lord knew about it because He, I knew, saw everything which I did or thought. As for my father, there was no doubt about his knowledge of the Christianly sacrifice. I took great pains to see to that. 18

But what happened? If that very afternoon I was caught playing baseball in a field distant enough, I thought, from the house to escape the attention of my father, it seemed to matter to him not at all that I should have performed such an unselfish and virtuous act that very morning; nor did my silent prayers to the Lord on the way home have the slightest apparent effect. For, despite all, upon reaching home I was inevitably led out to the barn and there made to feel both the wrath of God and my father by free applications of that corrective which my grandfather used to call-so poetically-"beech-oil." (Beech-oil was a general term applied to whippings by beech-switches, mapleswitches, horsewhips, or convenient straps.)

The misery of such occasions was transient, especially if it happened that the same evening after church serviceas it often did-some of the Normal School girls who boarded with us would come down into the parlor to play the piano and sing. For Marian Cleve was among thema dark-haired, contralto-voiced creature who seemed to me then to be very sympathetic and angelic. She would whisper to me with her low, soft voice that she was very sorry to hear that I had got a whipping. And when my father's back was turned and he was tremendously preoccupied with singing bass to the shrieking trebles, she would wink at me a little, and give me a quick little hug. Her gesture did more than a little to convince me at a tender age that if young men were to receive indulgences or dispensations from any of the Heavenly Hosts such dispensations must come without question from those angelic members thereof who "did up" their hair and spoke with contralto voices. 19

Indeed, there were other compensations to the harsher aspects of my religious and spiritual tutelage. In those days there were no movies, and to the Town-Hall-and-Opera- House, there came very few plays. I was allowed to attend fewer still. Moreover, there was no social life to speak of except that which centered around the church. So it was both necessary and inevitable that the more frivolous features of such social life as I did have should come under its guardianship. There were monthly suppers and holiday parties, church bazaars and magnificent Christmas celebrations, Junior League meetings and Easter entertainments. 

I remember most vividly those glorious Christmas celebrations. For weeks before Christmas I would go to the church basement with my sister Ruth on several afternoons a week. We went to rehearse my part in a Christmas play or pageant, help with decorations, make ornaments for the Christmas tree, but always to play around and have a good time. If one were late or even absent due to the momentarily more important engagements of a snowball fight or a hockey game, there were no dire punishments. The girls might try to tease one, but even that was not an altogether unpleasant experience. It was always so wonderful on those cold afternoons when the snow was already two feet deep, and more was falling, to go into the warm and lighted basement of the church, and be there in the midst of the phantasmagoria of laughter, talking, young people rehearsing, and the general industry of “getting ready for Christmas." It was good to have an audience before which one could boast of one's most recent achievements of skill and bravery on the skating rink or atop the enemies' snowball fort, and to see the girls with their braids of hair undone, 20

dressed like fairies, angels or children of long ago. Then too, if I were sly enough, I could steal most thrilling glimpses of that ever-mysterious region of guarded whiteness and pink bare skin which on more formal occasions the girls concealed so carefully. One always had the excellent pretext of asking a particular girl to come away into some corner alone with him so that he might practice his lines with her as a prompter. When the details of that particular subterfuge were disposed of, there was of course no good reason why she should not remain there with him. for a while talking about very private little things, feeling very excited and funny about it. . . . ...

Yes, the church had compensations for the harsher aspects of its religious and spiritual guidance. For around 

Christmas time even the Bible stories seemed to become interesting and colorful. Oh, stars of the East and Bethlehem mangers and Christmas carols and Peace on Earth, Good 

Will to Men! What beautiful, incomparable romance! My heart was gayer than usual. The very snowflakes were full of excitement. Chimneys and fireplaces became objects of affection. I was full of good deeds! Ah, those gorgeous illusions of youth and Yuletide! 

On Christmas Eve there are oysters for supper! The smell of balsam boughs, candy-making, cookie-baking and freshly popped popcorn permeates the house. There is an air of magic and mystery in every surreptitious wink and whispered conversation. Strange excursions are made to bedrooms, where doors are locked, and paper rattles cautiously. There have been last-minute trips to Weston's store, with its frosty windows only partially hiding the fascinating array of glistening skates, of silvery horns, and brightcolored games, its counters loaded with steam-engines and crocinole sets, rocking-horses and drums, its walls lined 21

with wonderful red sleds and magnificent toboggans. The sleigh-bells ring with a merrier jingle; the gay snow crystals sparkle with midnight brilliance; cutters whizz by on the jolly excursions to church entertainments and home celebrations. The very frost of the air tingles with excitement. 

Later, on the cruel road of truths and disillusionmentthat forbidding path of categories and ruthless dialectics— I learned, of course, how transient and trivial were the tangible circumstances of those glorious flights of imagination. But I can never forget the romance of those passages.

They have remained the permanent and significant superstructure of a foundation of flim-flams! The unrealities have turned out to be the only realities! From a perversion misnamed "religion" the dross of stupidity and dogma has found its place, and the pure gold of fancies remains. 

Those pregnant evidences of a will to see things as they ought to be, rather than as they are, have triumphed, as art survives the hands which make it. 3 But it must not be supposed that I was very imaginative, or reflective, or given to any but the most ordinary mental activities in my youth. Then indeed my life was made up of action and physical sensation. If precocity visited me at all it spoke only to my body and let my brain go hang. At twelve my body had grown so rapidly, and had thus outdistanced all but the clumsier forms of coordination, that it earned for me the name of "big lummox." I was generally most useful in all those forms of activity which required size and strength.

In the Junior League of our church there were about nine boys who ranged in age from twelve to fourteen. 22

And during the winter we made up a formidable hockey team, with substitutes, manager and all. By "Hobson's choice" I was goal-keeper. And in that position in those days both power and magnitude were an asset, for many of the assaults upon the goal were made in the manner of football "rushes" and a goal-keeper was really the last line of defense.

The captain of our team was Johnny Frobisher, my closest companion and nearest neighbor. Years later he became one of Dartmouth's greatest athletes and has never got over it. There was a large vacant lot beside his house which when flooded in winter became an excellent skating rink. 

We practised there, played our games, compared hockey sticks and showed off before the girls. Sometimes we ventured down to the river and skated there with the much older fellows from the Normal School and the Clarkson "Tech". When they played we would watch their hockey games closely, trying to learn all the fine points of passing and carrying the puck through opponents' most desperate defense. All this interest bore fruit. Johnny learned to play center exceptionally well and acquired that deft little whip to his hockey stick which gained us many a goal. While I won a reputation as goal-keeper which made me content with the job.

When spring came around we had a baseball team. We called ourselves the "Nine Young Athletes". Though there was only one other team in town which we could play, we were greatly concerned with regular practise, identical uniforms, and official scoring. The other team was made up of Catholic boys, and the rivalry between us was always intense. I became the pitcher, my lanky strength giving me ability to throw a faster ball than any of the others. 

Speed at that age was the thing that counted. As I remem- 23

ber, we won most of our games. That same kind memory reminds me that on the occasions we lost we were usually without some of our best players, who had been called to the painful tasks of beating carpets, sawing wood, or running on eternally long errands.

On some of those occasions, indeed, I was myself called; my horrible task being to reduce the mounting piles of wood behind our house into lengths to fit the kitchen stove. 

It was at the instance of my first call that I deserted my job in order to pitch the last two innings of an allimportant game. I received very generous applications of the painful "beech-oil" that evening after supper. I considered that my father should have given me half-awhipping because I had, after all, done half of my work. 

And because he was deaf to my pleas in that direction, my sense of the injustice made me determine a revenge. 4 It was my job to go out to the barn after supper to throw some bedding under the horses. I saw my opportunity. 

The evening after the whipping I did my chore as usual, but before I left the barn I loosened the halter-shank of my father's favorite bay mare, Sally, and unhooked the rear door of the stable. Several times on the short path back to the house I was on the verge of returning to repair my wickedness, but the very painful recollections of that whipping restrained me. At length I got back to the kitchen trembling with excitement, and a fearful sense of sin. Desperately I tried all evening to forget my deed, but the harder I struggled, the more insistently it tortured me. In bed I tossed in hectic alternations of defiance and remorse. 

The next morning was Sunday. I stayed in bed as long 24

as possible, worn out from the agony of the night's unrest, listening, listening for my brother Mathew's steps as he returned from the barn. At last the commotion downstairs announced the terrible discovery. Sally had been stolen! 

Or had run away!! I buried my head under the covers and shivered. Finally, not being able to stand it any longer, I got up with feverish resolve and went to the bathroom. 

I was splashing cold water on my face in preparation for facing Papa and telling him the truth, when Mathew came in crying the "news.” "Was the stable door hooked when you bedded the horses last night?" he asked.

I feigned the utmost surprise and consternation. "Why, as far as I remember," I stammered, "it was closed and hooked. . . . I'm almost sure it was in fact, I'd almost bet anything that it was. . . ." • · "It's mighty funny," he said, and he turned around to go downstairs. I followed him with the towel in my hands.

I encountered my father in the kitchen with the display of utmost innocence. Five minutes before, I had boldly decided to tell him the truth and face the worst whipping that I had ever had in my life. But having unexpectedly told Mathew a lie, I now unaccountably decided to stick to it. 

My father did not suspect me. No doubt throughout that day, upon the few occasions that he noticed me, he interpreted my tortured face as an indication of how bad I felt that Sally had run away. Early that forenoon instead of going to church he hitched up the other horse and drove around the country looking for Sally. Meanwhile the fear that the mare would never be found was added to the misery of my remorse. For days I suffered my first great wretchedness. When Sally was found on a farm some six 25

miles out of Potsdam I knew my first relief. But for many months the consciousness of my deed returned to torture me. After many years it came back frequently to remind me that the worst act of my youth—at least according to any of the successive codes of ethics which I have ever embraced-had gone unpunished by my father, who was always so meticulous and rigid in his adherence to the caveat of Solomon. This was the only occasion, however, that I can remember that my father ever missed punishing me for any infraction of his very severe code. Yet throughout all these years there is no remorse or punishment that stands more vividly painful in my mind than that which my conscience inflicted upon me at the time. 26

CHAPTER V 

WHILE during all those pleasant years between seven and fourteen I was predominantly a creature of action and feeling (as I continued to be for another seven years thereafter) there must have been some slight increment to my mental development. Only the evidence of academic milestones show it. From second grade to the first year of high school one does learn the fundamental operations of arithmetic, to say nothing of the discovery of America and the difference between a strait and a peninsula. 

But from that period there come to me now only a few vivid memories of any mental ferment, and one has to do with economic values. When my father arrived in Potsdam he was, to my mind, a man derived from God and quite capable of doing anything as long as he deemed it righteous. His confidence in his ability and righteousness had never been questioned by me. But during those first two winters in Potsdam his employment as a carpenter was quite sporadic, a circumstance which aroused enough feeling of alarm in my mother so that she could not conceal her fear from me. Thus there was born in me that first vague uncertainty concerning the security of life! I know that my mother had an almost blind confidence in my father. But I now realize also that she always had an acute and painful sense of the precariousness of any situation, whether it was the danger of our going out in the snow without our overshoes on or the chance which my father took in moving his family from the safety of the farm to 27

the uncertainties of life in Potsdam. It was due to the unfortunate acuteness of that sense of hers that I experienced my first vague acquaintance with that hideous monster, "Economic Insecurity," which like a hound of hell has dogged me ever since, in my most elevated enthusiasms and melancholy apathies, in moments of ecstasy, and in hours of horrible pain. Doubtless it has mixed benefit with its curse, as all the trials of man must do. Still nowhere on my journey towards urbanity have I been able to view it with any more affability than I must always feel towards cruelty or greed.

So during those pleasant years between seven and fourteen there was certainly one mental ferment, however bitter the wine it produced. 2 As springtime of the second year came around a cornucopia of good news and startling prospects was emptied into our midst. Excitement pervaded the household. 

For weeks my father had been bringing home long rolls of blueprints which, after supper, he spread upon the dining-room table and pored over until long after I had gone to bed. When he had first brought them home and opened them so enthusiastically, I had asked him what they were. Being told that they were plans for the new Methodist church my interest in them had ceased, for I had heard so much both at Sunday School and at home about "the new Methodist church," and the seemingly endless activity in raising money for it, that I long ago had grown quite indifferent to anything concerning it. 

One night there was a most extraordinarily eager glow on my father's face as he came home from work and strode 28

into the kitchen. Indeed, there was an aura of elation about him that was inescapably contagious. "Well, Mama," he said triumphantly, "I wouldn't be at all surprised if your husband built the M. E. Church."

My mother stared at him for a moment with an expression of awe and incredibility. "My soul a' body, Amos!" she finally exclaimed. "You don't mean it!" "That's just what I do mean!" exulted my father. And he grabbed her around the waist and began a most un- Methodist-like dance around the kitchen. ... 

My mother shrieked, "Amos! Amos! Good land o' livin'! 

What are you doin'! You'll have me on the floor in a minute if you don't look out!" She scolded him with a smile on her face that would have melted even the heart of a successful banker.

I remember that one of my strongest impressions then was of the unwonted and undignified behavior of my father. It seemed to me that he was violating the very foundations of his character, his austere God-like perfection, by thus revealing such human traits. The emotion of joy over anything except doing God's duty seemed to place him farther down toward my level of sinfulness; and though it faintly suggested that someday I might like him, it also decreased my respect. Later as I came to see more of these human traits of his, so incorrigible had been my early impression of him as a harsh agent of divinity who could not be deeply affected by the joys and sorrows of the world of flesh, that each such display, which would have been lovable and hoped-for in any one else, appeared obscene or ridiculous in him, an evidence of degeneration. 

Indeed, so incurable was the taint of that childhood impression that when I began to learn the fundamentals of 29

sex and to place two and two together and thus realize how I happened to be born, I could not help viewing him with disgust. Sex, I had learned, was the original, most shameful sin. My father, who had always stood before me as being without sin, had indulged in it! How horrible! 

How much more terrible I, sinful enough in my own right, must be, having such a father! Against that thought I would weigh such facts as: that he did not smoke or drink or use the name of the Lord in vain; that he attended church regularly and made many efforts to bring other people to God.

Later, however, when I made the discovery that sex, properly restrained and sanctified by marriage in the church, was not only righteous but a thing entirely-though inexplicably-different from all other manifestations of it, I felt less badly. But it took many years to completely eliminate that particular disgust for my father. 

So it was that my father's dancing around the kitchen with my mother made me feel embarrassed for him, although I laughed, and although the import of the news which he brought was of such an exciting nature, even to my unsophisticated youth, that it might normally have been expected to command my mental attention to the exclusion of everything else. For however indifferent I had become to the recurring conversations, campaigns, and promises concerning the proposed "New Methodist

Church," that very indifference was but an indication of the tremendous and even vital importance of the project of building it-just as one's usual indifference to the roof over one's head, the air one breathes or the ideals one cherishes is a form of acknowledgment of their critical consequence. 30

That good news which foretold the building of the new Methodist church by my father should be set down as among the memorabilia of those days in Potsdam. My father, having come to the town simply as a farmer who had acquired a little knowledge of carpentry at some vague period during his past, had studied the plans (which had been made by a New York architect), figured on them, submitted his figures, and had been awarded the contract. 

It later developed that though most of the men on the building committee had called him "a farmer who knows nothing about superintending the erection of a fiftythousand-dollar marble church," they were influenced to give him the contract: first, because the architect had said that his bid showed more knowledge of the distributed costs of the project; second, because his figure was the lowest; and third, because he was a member of the church. 

Early that summer the old church was torn down. For weeks it was a fascinating sight to watch. When part of the steeple was removed, the big heavy bell, which I had been told weighed several tons, was revealed. I could not imagine how it could possibly be lowered to the ground. 

But one afternoon, when I had gone down to the job to take my father a pail of lemonade (which my mother was still very fond of making for him on hot days as she had done on the farm during the weeks of haying and harvesting) I encountered a large crowd of curious observers who, apparently as interested as I was in the problem of getting the bell down, stood there staring at the visible solution of that problem. As the makeshift crane which my father had devised gently lowered that heavy mass of iron to the ground, I felt suddenly born in me a new pride in my father which was so unprecedentedly mov- 31

ing and wonderful that I wanted to rush up and throw my arms around him and talk to him as I would to one of my closest friends.

Shortly after Father had begun work on the church he decided to make those improvements to our house, whose absence had so long made it shabby and conspicuous as well as an uncomfortable reminder to me that perhaps our family wasn't as well-off, or as clever, or as nice, as our neighbors. That summer my oldest brother, Mathew, with the help of a carpenter, clapboarded the house, built a large piazza across the front and down one side, painted the entire exterior of the house white with a green border, and laid hardwood floors. During the evenings, later on in the season, my father built an elaborate cherry grill between the parlor and the living-room and put up a new set of hardwood stairs in the reception hall. 

When all the work was completed it seemed to me that there was no other house in Potsdam quite so fine. Many years later when I returned and saw that house again I suffered one of those great shocks which attend one's constant state of bewilderment as they return to familiar scenes in quest of their youth. That ill-proportioned, gingerbready house of the very early twentieth century! Yet what a fairyland palace in my youth!

Even in the little community of Potsdam, which was far less ruthless than the rest of the world in gaging the worth and importance of a person by the glitter and value of their material possessions, that new house did a great deal to relieve my first pangs of inequality and inferiority. 

One day in the charity wards of City Hospital I had unending hours and ceaseless provocations for thinking over and over again of the unequal distribution of material benefits. The creatures around me, who must depend upon 32

charity for the meager sustenance which kept them alive, challenged by their contrast a yacht club of millionaires. just across the river from our hospital. I asked myself if it were not certain insidious and inescapable environmental influences of our youth which induce us to feel that we are somehow better than some one else if we own more than they do. Because I had felt years before that I was a much better person when my father had contrived to make our house finer than any I had ever lived in before, I had become inoculated with that pride which is the virus of all vicious strife, all tyranny, all wars.

Now that the house was finished and we were all so proud of it, my mother decided that Ruth and I could have a Hallowe'en party. We had never had a party before, and it seemed that the new house not only made such an affair plausible, but made it necessary. If one has just acquired something that is far more handsome than anything he has ever had before, most of the pleasure to be derived from it is from showing it off to his friends-for both their admiration and their envy.

By that third fall in Potsdam my sister and I had become acquainted with several boys and girls who did not belong to the Methodist church. In many cases the very freshness of their acquaintance--the intriguing lure which envelops people about whose lives we know so much less than we do of our close friends-made us eager to become more familiar with them. So we invited a great many of them to our party.

There was a considerable debate with Mama and Papa about Mary Crowley, Ruth's friend, because she was a Catholic. But she came. Mama and Papa were especially careful to be nice to her and to show her all of the very finest Christian virtues that were in the catalog of polite- 33

ness. She, with her warm Irish talent for making every one love her, and her copper hair and blue eyes, became the very flame of the festivity around which the rest of us glowed with a reflected warmth.

The enchantment of that party lasted for weeks, for it was at least during that length of time that Marian Premier spoke to me and treated me with an air of an intimate understanding when our eyes or our occupations brought us together in the fourth grade classroom of No. 8 School. 

And I was certain that my older brothers must feel very pleased with me for having attained to such familiar relations with a proud girl whose father was one of the wealthiest merchants in the town. Therefore I was particularly chagrined and resentful when one day, after a rough and heated session of football in the schoolyard, sitting down close to her to help her with some arithmetic, she said, "I hate boys who don't keep their hands clean and their hair combed!" It seemed to me that she implied that any boy who was as haughty as she was could play football without soiling his hands or mussing his hair. The implication wounded me so deeply that I could not even reply; but in order to conceal my feelings, I did my utmost to pay no attention to her words, finishing the example which I was doing for her and shamefacedly excusing myself by saying that I had to study my spelling lesson.

That characteristic in me as it grew and manifested itself more widely gained for me the alternate aspersion of cowardice or a soft-hearted disposition. Later, upon analysis, it seemed to me to be mainly an unpopular form of vindictiveness in a world which defines courage as an eagerness to be pugnacious about any affront or criticism. 

In childish accord with the environment of which I was a 34

part, I began to feel I was a coward and, after similar displays so lacking in boldness, would determine that on the next occasion I would have a ready retort of such a scathing nature as to stamp me for all time as a creature with extraordinary courage. But when those next occasions arrived my real nature was as unprovoked to daring demonstrations of violence either in speech or action as any normal person's life is uninfluenced by New Year's resolutions. Because of this continued failure upon my part to develop that cocky quality which I classified as courage, my vindictive impulse inevitably sought an outlet in other directions.

I tried to make it as obvious as possible to Marian Premier that I was not so ill received by other girls, despite my dirty hands, and I strove to show the school bullies, Puggy

Hawkins and Elmer Burns, that although it was not easy to pick a fight with me, it was more difficult to hit one of my pitches in baseball, and just as hard to get a hockey puck through my goal.

In all of us common mortals there is desire to vindicate ourselves in one way or another. We yearn to make our ideas of life prevail. If one person with an inborn distaste for angry assault-whether it goes under the name of timidity or soft-heartedness-seeks by skill and ridicule, by gentleness, by tolerance or by the dramatization of his own example to allure his envious acquaintances or impartial critics into an acceptance of his opinions, he is bound to rationalize his method; just as my own mind was inevitably driven into that (then unnamed) process when 

Marian wounded my feelings in that fourth grade classroom and I did not retaliate in the conventional manner of a boy nine years old. 35

It may have been that the excitement and the tangible, physical pleasures of Thanksgiving, following so soon after my first Hallowe'en party and the end of my first romance, did much to mitigate my yearning for a renewal of that slight but precious relationship with Marian. In any event appetite, for that day at least, triumphed over tender passion. Life somehow went on. 

That Thanksgiving was an event that might well have made any of the Holland family forget everything except its own good fortune, and the abundant blessings of the Lord. From precarious existence of its first two years in 

Potsdam, it had suddenly been transported into a life of material prosperity which seemed to border upon opulence. 

The improvements on the house and its new furnishings were still a fresh source of pride and well-being. Old friends and relations could now be entertained with the lavishness of middle-class Methodism. Mathew had been made leader of the Normal School orchestra. Papa had been asked to organize an orchestra in the Sunday School. 

Mama had been cooking for days and Ruth had made several pounds of homemade candy. There were twentytwo people for Thanksgiving dinner. The immense table was set in the grandest fashion, with silver standing upright in the grotesque manner of Indian tepees, napkins elegantly curled in the water glasses, jams and jellies in innumerable glass dishes whose endless variety of bizarre patterns were a sight to behold, and bowls of oranges which, even in opulence, were a Saturday night luxury. 

Last, but by no means least, came the turkey, with its lesser accompaniments of a goose, several chickens and a huge roast of pork! The unaccustomed splendor of the 36

Holland family! It was like Dick Whittington being made 

Lord Mayor of London . . . like the woodsman's son suddenly discovering that he was a prince! Papa stood at the end of the long table beaming triumphantly. Then there was a sudden silence and his face became passionately serious. His head bowed-into the impressive hush his bass voice entered reverently. "Father," he said, "will you say grace." And then that even more eloquent bass of my grandfather began its sonorous supplication, “Almighty God,

Father of all blessings, Giver of all gifts, we thank Thee. 

We thank Thee for the gift of life which once again allows us to gather together in thanksgiving for Thy glorious bounty. We humbly thank Thee for Thy constant grace and for the unnumbered material benefits that Thou hast seen fit to heap upon us. And wilt Thou now bless us once more in our Thanksgiving. Bless this food to our use and our lives to Thy service, in Christ's name we ask it. Amen."

There is a timid, uncertain raising of heads, a gradual break in the reverent hiatus and then a deluge of laughter and exclamations, gayety and compliments.

Indeed, so great was the abundance, so loud were the thanks, so magnificent the festivity of that Thanksgiving, that in the spirit of it all there was a suggestion of exaggeration; as though, underneath, this unaccustomed grandeur betrayed the uncertainty of such benefits-the precarious nature of wealth. To a farmer a year of prosperity is no guarantee that it is to be followed by another, yet he answers the fickle bountifulness of Nature with a corresponding lavishness; and we of the Holland family were essentially farmers.

As unregardful as most farmers are of the future, my father was even more so. When he had money he never 37

thought about the future except in the most optimistic way. 

In all his life, whenever good fortune smiled upon him he never for one moment thought that it would cease doing so. It may have been a sincere faith in the assurance that "The Lord will provide." I think that the fundamental cause of his optimism was even deeper. I am sure that he never ceased having faith in himself. He never became resigned to the capriciousness of life. He was always confident that some day he would overcome all its economic uncertainties. At least he never admitted a doubt. He was a consummate bluffer, but one got the impression that he believed in his bluffs. Yet on account of his abnormal faith in himself he distrusted others to an extraordinary degree. 

The whole curious process came to him naturally from the inane tenets of his religion. A religion that holds man to be born in sin and naturally evil, that considers most all of the spontaneous pleasures of life to be worldly and wicked, that demands of him that he believe that these, and other equally bizarre falsehoods, are the divine truth in the face of all evidence to the contrary, cannot, of course, avoid developing in its followers the most fantastic notions as to what is real and what is false. It must encourage in them the most exaggerated hypocrisy.

My father's lack of any precautions concerning the future was based not so much upon a sunny optimism as it was upon the grim confidence that he and the Methodist 

Church could not be wrong.

His life was peculiar in the additional respect that it contained no very, very close companions. No men except relatives ever came to the house to be entertained, or to spend the evening in friendly conversation. He never seemed to possess any genuine affection for any of the men whom he knew. And yet long before I had ever ... 38

delved into the illuminating pseudo-science of psychology I had seen a twinkle in my father's eye as he surreptitiously scanned the trim figure of a pretty woman. I had observed his guarded grins whenever he listened to certain accounts of my grandfather's rakish and libidinous adventures. And

I had noticed the expression of satisfaction that came over his face whenever he took the medicinal-and alcoholic -Peruna. From these and many other observations I had evolved the faint and querulous speculation that perhaps his austerity and impossible righteousness were maintained at the expense of a violent and constant struggle with his better nature. 

At that memorable Thanksgiving dinner my grandfather got into a discussion of religion with my father. Such a circumstance was not, of course, unusual. It always seemed inevitable that my father should get into religious discussions on all more or less formal occasions, especially when there were a lot of people in to dinner. It was the one subject which, once begun, never seemed to end, which grew more interesting and heated as it went on despite the pleading protestations of my mother. Father could be more prepossessed and intractable about it than in any other debate because he could more plausibly and gracefully reduce all issues to quotations from the Bible, from which infallible position there could be no appeal by any sane controversialist.

Grandfather, however, was not sane, by the standards of Methodist alienists. When he talked about religion his words were always inclined to crackle with humor. "What 

I'd like to know," said my grandfather, "is—if God made 39

the world, who made God?" Papa, irritated, smiled with 

Christian tolerance. "Before time was, I am,'" he said simply. "That's very easy for anyone who wants to understand it," he went on to explain. “. . . God has always existed." But though Father was convinced of the infallibility of his explanations he nevertheless seemed unwontedly irritated and uncomfortable with Grandfather. Nothing that he could ever find to say could quite cancel the plausibility of Grandfather's skepticism. It was so disturbingly concerned with common sense and so annoyingly sacrilegious!

Grandfather was "evil" in such a variety of ways. Following his return from the Civil War he had never been able to settle down on the farm again and support his family, but had gone from one adventure to another until the time arrived when the Government gave him a pension. From that auspicious moment he became a frank and settled gentleman of leisure, condescending to work only on such occasions as he felt spontaneously impelled "to do something." He made axe-helves, did blacksmithing, or put in a garden, but never worked from any sense of duty. This dereliction, together with his other excursions from Wesleyan morals-drinking, smoking, swearing, and adultery-was, of course, prima facie evidence with my father that whatever he said about religion was, at the very outset, born of corruption. 

The depth of Grandfather's corruption seemed to measure for my older brothers-and certainly for me-the greater part of his interest and charm; for, devil though he must be, he was an entertaining devil. Tall, slim, straight as an arrow, with his Vandyke beard and high forehead, he had the bearing of a cavalier and the sardonic tongue of a disbeliever. With his alternating moods of 40

gruff asperity and jolly facetiousness he could inspire both love and respect. There were moments when I considered that to approach him any closer than a radius of ten feet would be to violate his dignity and solitude unpardonably. 

There were others when I sat on his lap as he showed me how to whittle expertly with my jack-knife, or wriggled in his arms while he tickled me with the tip of his beard.

He taught me how to make my first bow and arrows and thus enabled me to outshoot any of the rest of the boys in the neighborhood, both in distance and accuracy. Sometimes on Sunday afternoons he would take me with him. on his walks in the woods hunting and gathering suitable branches of briar and thorn-apple trees for canes-so unusual and pleasant a diversion on a Sabbath for me that my eagerness to have it repeated aroused my father's suspicions and resulted in a denial of that privilege, which encouraged Grandfather to quote Bob Ingersoll a little more frequently and bombastically than usual, and to send me to the store more often for his plugs of chewing tobacco. 

Although I was inclined to accept at that time Papa's interpretation of such actions as a malicious spite on the part of Grandfather, I later came to realize that such an attitude sprang from the generous desire of Grandfather to show me the converse of ecclesiastical restraint. And the enjoyment he derived no doubt was precisely the same as that which accrued to Socrates from "corrupting" the youth of Athens.

My exposure to Grandfather's philosophy and my pleasure in his indulgence was tempered with distrust-a distrust which always accompanies the gradual acquisition of any knowledge which violates a code already held, no matter how unpleasant that code may be. 

As my grandfather discussed religion with Papa, at 41

that memorable Thanksgiving dinner, and as my older brothers showed an inclination to support his disbeliefs, I could not decide whether I wanted those disbeliefs to triumph over my father's religion or whether I desired that my father should annihilate all of them with one sweeping, infallible quotation from the Bible which would settle the discussion for all time in favor of religion. All of us hesitate on the threshold of a new truth, hoping that somehow we may enjoy it surreptitiously without the necessity of its supplanting a pre-conceived collection of ideas. 

Yet without this hesitation, without this loyalty to opinions whose acquisition has been attended by its own difficulties, how much more chaotic life would be, how excessively mercurial! For inertia is as necessary a law of our mental life as it is of our physical. 42

CHAPTER VI 

THE Sessions family was a very old lineage in Potsdam. 

They had come there in the early days with the Clarksons, shortly after Gouverneur Morris had surveyed the earliest townships of St. Lawrence County. Throughout the years they had secured large tracts of land, built a sawmill, gone into the lumber business, and established paper mills. With all their projects they made a great deal of money. 

The most distinguished branch of the family lived on Elm Street. I passed their magnificent Georgian house every day when I went to church or school. They were the first people in town to have electric lights and a telephone in their house, and late winter afternoons as I returned from skating or sliding down hill I could see through the side windows of the stately colonial doorway the lovely color and elegance of their parlor. There, I thought, life must be lived with splendor and loveliness beyond my imagination. In the summer the Sessions' huge barn (they called it simply a stable), which seemed to have been finished with almost as much care and elaborateness as the house, and matched it with its brilliant yellow clapboards and white trim, was always a fascinating exhibition place of shiny carriages and glisteningly groomed horses. 

One day, coming home from school with Ruth during that first year in Potsdam, I had noticed a very lovely girl walking along on the opposite side of the street who presently went into the Sessions house. "There goes Doria 

Sessions!" said my sister quietly, with an effort to conceal 43

her admiration and awe of a person who lived in such a grand mansion. I turned to gaze more frankly at the vision who had occupied most of my surreptitious attention all the way up the street.

As the years went by I saw this girl more frequently. 

Indeed, whenever Johnny Frobisher or Earl Purves or any of the rest of our gang used to be together and saw any of the Sessions, it was always the habit to call attention to them by saying: "There goes Mrs. Sessions," or "There goes Gerald Sessions," or "There goes Doria Sessions!" Our voices were always full of respect. To all of us they represented our first impressive acquaintance with "very rich people." The rest of the boys had known (knowing a person, meaning to us then simply to know who they were) them longer than I had (and some of them even spoke to them), but they had never reached the point of familiarity which enabled them to take any of the Sessions family for granted. While I, from the first time that I saw Doria Sessions, could never hear her name mentioned, to say nothing of seeing her, without feeling that sad pang of melancholy, that bitter-sweet yearning, which so fatally tempers the joy of a passion for something or somebody which it is clearly impossible to touch. She represented to me the unattainable princess of the fairy tales which I read so eagerly. In all of my imaginative reveries of heroism and love she was the one to whom I ultimately came after my greatest triumphs, kneeling before her with adoration and humility.

When my sister finally came to know her and to speak about her more or less familiarly around the house, Ruth herself magically acquired a new and prodigious importance in my estimation. My sister talked with Doria Sessions! She walked with her!! Perhaps she even touched 44

her!!!... For long periods I meditated in silence upon that intimacy, wondering how her voice would sound in conversation, how it would feel to stand close to her; if she ate, laughed, went to the bathroom, and slept like ordinary mortals who did not possess her divinity. Like a desire that is so strong in us, so overwhelming, that it temporarily precludes our making any effort to fulfill it, my passion to discover everything, the most intimate details and habits, the most esoteric thoughts, wishes and appetites, of Doria Sessions, was so overwrought, so impetuous, so paralyzing, that I could not bring myself to show the sheerest interest in what my sister said. I failed to ask her even the most casual questions. 

One day I saw her walking up the street with my sister, and because they had passed the Sessions house and were going on toward ours, I decided that I would not go home; 

I stopped at Johnny Frobisher's, sent his younger brother to get my skates and hockey stick, and spent the rest of the afternoon avoiding our house as though it were an inviolable sanctuary. When it approached the time that I should be getting home in order to get my chores done before Father was through with work, I saw over the top of the cedar hedge which partially concealed the Frobisher ice-pond from the street my sister walking with Doria downtown. I skated more violently and conspicuously than ever, so that if they should happen to look in my direction they might be convinced that I was wholly preoccupied with playing, and had no time for girls. But just as I had concluded that they were going by without noticing me, my sister called from across the street and told me that Mama was expecting me home immediately. At supper that night I outwardly displayed a profound irritation when my brother Mathew began to twit me about 45

how much I had missed by not being home, saying that Doria was a very beautiful girl, very intelligent and had lovely manners-which I might well observe with considerable profit. I glowed with excitement and elation as I realized that the rest of the family were able-despite the impossibility of their ever attaining to my acuteness and appreciation—to see in this divine angel some of the virtues which were already so familiar to me; a familiarity not achieved from experience, of course, but by that inevitable projection into her of all the finest qualities that my imagination had been able to conceive. 2 As the winter wore on there came an awakening to a fresh initiation of life, mingling strange sensations of surprise and pain, shame and pride, with a startling awareness of a new attribute of manhood. For a few days I was sick in bed with the grippe. As I lay with the weakness of fever arresting momentarily the unceasing round of my normal physical activity—and stimulating, by that curious pathological phenomenon of compensation, an unwonted flow of mental animation-I began to think of the things I had done which I ought not to have done, and of those things undone which I ought to have done. For the first time in my life I consciously and spontaneously desired to be good and I silently prayed to Jesus to help me in this new resolve to live a life as much like His as was humanly possible. And in a flash there came to me a feeling of virtue that elevated my whole being into a delicious state of dazed relaxation: free from worries, anxieties, youthful avariciousness and selfish desires.

I resolved to be a friend to every one and to help them 46

all I could. I would tell Papa of how I had left Sally's halter shank untied and the stable door unhooked, and of the day that I purposely ran the bucksaw into a nail in order to make it dull so that I might not have to cut any more wood. I would come home from school every afternoon before I started to play and see if there were any chores that I could do for Mama. I would study harder in school and become the head of my class. I would speak kindly again to Marian Premier and help her with her arithmetic problems. An ineffable feeling of righteousness came over me as I lay there in my bed, and the prospect of doing even the most difficult tasks in the future suddenly appeared amazingly easy. In my imagination I pictured myself becoming so good, so Christlike, so generous and helpful that Reverend Miller would quickly recognize the change which had taken place and would soon be asking me to help him in the work of the church. My father would smile at me and realize how unnecessary the use of the horsewhip would be in the future.

I had entered the portals of puberty and in a passing hour of fever I was certain that my spirit had at last delivered itself freely and wholly into the sacred bosom of Christianity.

Incidents, the recollection of which in my previous existence had brought the most uncomfortable pangs of remorse and uneasiness, I now reviewed with the peaceful equanimity of a person "saved": occasions when I had charged peanuts at the grocery store without my mother's permission; the time when I had my bicycle tire repaired at the hardware store and told Mr. Needham that Papa had sent me; the fifty-cent baseball I had charged at 

Weston's and boastingly told my companions that my uncle had given to me; the nickels I had withheld from 47

the Sunday School collection in order to buy icecream sodas. Now I saw them as unfortunate evidences of the innate sinfulness of a poor, dark life which had not seen the light. 

When my mother came into the room bringing me some bouillon and an orange for lunch, I smiled at her with the radiance of my new-discovered spirituality. I piously asked her if when she came upstairs again she would not bring me my arithmetic book and a copy of the Bible. 

A look of bewilderment came over her face, when from her half-bent position, leaning over the bed to straighten out the sheets, she stared at me, immobile and silent. Suddenly the quaintness and bathos of my request came pouring down upon me like a deluge of icy water upon the deluded security of a late-sleeper's warm somnolence. To set out on the road of righteousness was not so difficult in solitude as in the pitying glare of my own mother's uncomprehension. Downright wickedness might be subdued, but embarrassment was another matter. Indeed, at that very moment, in order to disabuse my mother of the notion that I had unaccountably become extremely virtuous-for the thought of her cherishing such a notion had suddenly become very embarrassing to me--I went on to explain to her that the reason I wanted the books was that I was curious to discover how far my classes in school and the Junior League had got ahead of me during my illness.

After my mother had gone out of the room, I was left alone with the dismal consciousness of an abysmal deception. In the agony of my dejection the perspiration poured from my fevered body and I was once more brought rudely down to earth from the dazed and dreamy heights of my brief spiritual elevation. When my mother brought 48

me the books a half-hour later I opened my arithmetic and went through a perfunctory, half-hearted, five-minute attempt to study and then innocently put it aside and picked up my Bible, whose pages I vainly explored for the next half-hour in a lackadaisical attempt to regain the beatified state from which I had just fallen. Presently losing all consciousness of my original quest, I turned to the most purple passages of the Old Testament and was soon lost in a maze of erotic revery, which ultimately led me to a tender, ineffable contemplation of Doria Sessions as I laid the Bible aside and sank into a tenuous, bodiless lethargy.

In those early hours of acquaintance with the strange and subtle phenomena of puberty-tested as they were with the added ordeal of fever-that curious, intangible, and chemical distillation of my changing metabolism (known in religious parlance as the Spirit) went groping for an anodyne with which to soothe the pain of life's frustrations, uncertainties and melancholy humors. Suspending itself for a moment in the lulling assurances and restful security of religion, the predominant paganism of its formula was asserted and precipitated the whole solution into the pleasant, though turbulent, waters of love. 

But it must not be supposed that at thirteen this inevitable predilection of my nature had any cerebral significance to me other than to incur, during those frequent periods of introspection that afflict one's adolescence, a constant bedevilment at the instance of what I considered sincerely and devoutly "my better nature." So inescapable, so insistent were the ubiquitous influences which went to make up and support this "better nature," that the triumphs of my innate predilections, far from being attended by any mental growth or frank and justly deserved ela- 49

tion, in my conscious mind were meanly corrupted by a sense of sin. If I felt one thing, my mind was made to think another, so powerfully had the incessant protestations of Puritanism perverted my organism to the very antithesis of normality. Instead of the normal and healthy recognition that all evidences of truth, all trustworthiness, all pure conscience, originate from the senses, I had been poisoned with the illusion that the senses were the very root of all evil. And when I succumbed to those senses it was always in that state of exhilaration which momentarily-though unconsciously, of course-allowed me to feel such excursions from grace as beyond good and evil. 

It was in an excess of an aspect of love felt for Doria Sessions-imagined, and severely forbidden me by all the respectable elements of the society in which I then livedthat I first encountered a happiness so enduring, so reliable, so encouraging, that it transcended, like a just and beautiful compensation, the trials of adolescence, giving birth to an inchoate, unrealized wisdom, whose substance was not to appear upon the surface of my consciousness until years later, when in the immovable fastness of an arthritic's solitude, my mind discovered what my emotions had long felt. 3 The accumulation of events, ideas, days, hours, experiences and physical changes culminated, one day in this tender period of my thirteenth year, in the sweeping, vivid climax of a realization that I would some day be taking my place in the serious activities of the world. In the future (quite distant, to be sure) I would become, according to the course I now elected to follow, a builder like 50

my father, or an engineer like the fellows from the Tech who went around the town with their fascinating transits during nice days in the spring. They seemed to be incredibly wise in being able to tell the distance across the Racquette River without having to measure it with a tapemeasure. I might be a minister like the Reverend Miller, with his immaculate long black coat and carefully trimmed mustache, or a physician like the distinguished-looking Doctor Fuller, who seemed possessed of enough abstruse and magical knowledge to set him far above all the ordinary run of human beings.

It was at this time that the Normal girls became more wary about letting me sit on their laps and while treating me with more respect would frequently ask, "What are you going to be when you get to be a man?" Mother would say, if she happened to be nearby, that she hoped out of four sons at least one would be a minister. While Mama was talking about it, she was glancing at me with a look of such hopefulness and respect that I could not but feel extraordinarily important. The Normal girls, as though taking a cue from her, would also stare at me with an unwonted if insincere deference. I would feel then that no profession could possibly bring me more pleasure than the high calling of the Methodist pulpit, which, furnishing its ministers with houses, churches, ready-made admirers and the unparalleled privilege of monopolizing the hushed attention of large audiences week after week, gave one a life of elegant leisure and an opportunity to display his superior knowledge of the Bible, his infallible opinions, and great oratorical powers. But in the more difficult periods of solitary reflection I would always decide against a calling which seemed so lacking in opportunities for robust and varied adventures. Also, it might prevent one 51

from marrying an Episcopalian! Although I could not easily conceive myself marrying a member of a church which was, as everyone knew, nothing but an Anglicized Catholicism, I could not wholly ignore the fact that Doria 

Sessions was an Episcopalian. 

It so happened that during that winter when such momentous decisions were being forced upon me the country was in one of its periodic and fatuous states of economic depression. My father, after several years of incredible success and disarming prosperity, could find no work to do in Potsdam. Mama worried.
