Papa, with his characteristic confidence and optimism, looked upon the situation as one that was to be remedied by the simple procedure of going to some place where there was work. He decided to go to California. It did not matter that such a journey seemed to my mother almost as daring as a trip to China or an excursion to South Africa. Her fears and trepidations served to add glamour to the venture and to give Father that feeling of added importance which he imagined must naturally attend a hero who sets out for distant and dangerous lands in search of sustenance for his family. "Why, Etta," he would say in his most casual tone of voice, "you know I wouldn't think of going out there unless I was obliged to. 

But anybody can see that this town is all built up and in these hard times there's nothing else to do but go where a man can git work. Besides, the West is still young, and with San Francisco just been destroyed by an earthquake that's the place to go."

So Papa went to California. The excitement of his getting ready, the fascinating postcards which he sent to us children from Chicago, from Omaha, from Lincoln, from far-off Denver and the outlandish Salt Lake City, and finally from San Francisco, all filled me with a new ad- 52

miration. And at each thrilling postcard, every new letter telling of the Pacific Ocean, palm trees, orange groves, wicked Chinatowns, Los Angeles and incredibly perfect climate, there rose in me a fresh determination to become a builder like my father. It seemed that nothing could be more exciting. I would go to the Tech, become an architectural engineer, and then go into the building business with my father. Doria Sessions could not help but have a certain amount of respect for a fellow who could use a transit, build big churches, and make long trips to California.

As though purposely to add confusion and uncertainty to the problem of what course in life I should follow, my oldest brother Mathew, who had graduated from the Normal in the previous June and had accompanied Papa to California, got a job conducting a band in one of the suburbs of San Francisco. He began to send home very impressive pictures of himself in a magnificent uniform standing on the podium in front of his resplendent-looking musicians. The pictures were invariably accompanied by glowing newspaper accounts of his concerts, filled with extravagant praise of his "precocious conducting" and his "artistic cornet solos." The effect of those pictures, and the breath-taking flights of my imagination as I saw my own name emblazoned among such words of praise on the public pages of a newspaper, was too much for me. 

No longer could I be content with the prospects of the comparative obscurity of the building profession. No longer could I think of foregoing the imagined pleasures of being applauded, with my name in the papers. 

From the little bamboo whatnot where Mathew had kept the folios of music accumulated during his years with the Normal orchestra and the Town Band, I had many 53

times in the past carefully selected the most difficultlooking compositions, and, using my younger brother as an orchestra with his Christmas drum and tin flute, I had conducted the most moving, tempestuous and violent marches, overtures and storm music which my mind was capable of imagining. But now having achieved the dignity of thirteen years-and the size of a boy much older -I could no longer resort to such exercises of imagination in public. To have done so would immediately have placed me back in the category of childhood in the eyes of the rest of the family. Any public disclosure of my deepest thoughts or ambitions was inevitably accompanied by embarrassment. In the privacy of my bedroom I now set up one of Mathew's nickel-plated music stands and conducted my orchestras in silence, straining the efforts of the brass section, the tympani and cymbals to unprecedented fortes and incredibly fast staccatoes with terrific sweeps of my magic baton.

One of the Normal School girls, Alice Trine, who boarded with us, hearing me drumming over and over again on the piano a childish tune called "Chopsticks" with deafening effects, asked me if I would not like to have her teach me to play. I suspected that she had perhaps divined some hidden talent in me of which I was not yet aware, and that she actually could teach me to play the piano. So I consented. I began my lessons with the confidence that within a year I would be playing tremendously impressive solos at school assemblies and church entertainments. After two weeks I had reached the unalterable conclusion that piano-playing would forever be beyond the reach of my accomplishment. Secretly feeling that the accomplishment presupposed a faculty of which I was utterly devoid, I applied in private the designation 54

of "genius" (a word with whose strange and powerful significance I had just recently become acquainted) to all those amazing individuals who possessed it. As a person goes through life wholly unaware of certain virtues in an intimate friend until, suddenly, an occasion has arisen which has disclosed a knowledge, an insight, an eloquent articulateness utterly unsuspected, I came now, by my discovery of the apparently insuperable difficulties of music, to a revaluation of Mathew's achievements. 

Conceding him the possession of "genius" and the faculty of perseverance which I did not have, I willingly left unchallenged Mathew's superiority as a musician, turning temporarily to the vaguer task of becoming a great engineer.

It is thus that all of us come to one revaluation after another of the achievements, the failures, the hopes, the ambitions of some one else's career. A more intimate acquaintance with the medium of their expression, the difficulties they have had to overcome, the skill which they have acquired in order to make the most formidable feats seem easy teach us. Yet at no time may we ever be sure that the revaluation of a person or even a work of art, which we have just made, is a final one. Indeed until our intelligence, our curiosity and our lust are finally arrested in a state of irremedial petrifaction, we must continue to readjudicate the dance of life. Just as now in the opacity of my physical vision I must enrich my appreciation of a blue sky of early evenings in January, seen twenty years ago through the window at the foot of my bed in our house in Rochester; because today, having encountered the word cerulean in a line of poetry, I have, for the first time, looked up its meaning in the dictionary. 55

CHAPTER VII 

THE eldest son, my brother Mathew, had risen and developed to become the pride of the family. A serious, hardworking young man, I had frequently found in his spotless reputation a consoling, protective respite from the shame and shortcomings of my own repute. If I failed at geography or disgraced myself at grammar school "rhetoricals," it was palliating to reflect that, after all, the teachers and the pupils all knew that my brother Mathew was a distinguished graduate of the Normal School, and a very exceptional musician. They must thereby discern in me a great talent, an innate capacity which my present dereliction only momentarily belied.

In those early days Mathew's heroism was taken for granted. Anything which threatened to mitigate his greatness was squelched as a matter of course. I was passionately eager that he personally should enjoy the benefits of a reputation uncorrupted. That curious element of my vanity which sought to soothe and magnify the sense of importance which his name reflected upon me was mainly responsible for my efforts to conceal, extenuate, or forget any act or thought which might detract from his illustriousness. Quite naturally, also, if anything happened which offered a fresh assurance of his talent, my mind would seize upon it and enlarge its flattering implications. 

So great was the extent to which this practice was sometimes carried that it often presented the strange phenom- 57

enon of a sober belittlement of my own virtues in order that his might shine with a greater brilliance.

Yet, if in those early days of adolescence, Mathew became more of an oracle than an older brother to me, the respect which I gave his ideas was frequently nothing more than theoretical. There was in me a certain egotistical assertiveness, a manifestation of biological necessity, which made me resent, while theoretically approving, and avoid an undeviating imitation of those individuals for whom I had the greatest respect. A student who worships a great master will deliberately deviate from that master's interpretation of a sonata or a concerto in order to demonstrate that he, too, is an individual. He wishes to be an important entity, and hence worthy of his master's respect. 

I resented the superiority of Mathew's character. It seemed so easy for him to be good. Of course my resentment was more of the solar-plexus than it was of the mind. 

Very rarely did it come to the surface of my consciousness. Indeed, it is difficult now to find tangible evidence of that resentment. In retrospect I realize that then he was associated with my father. He seemed to be on my father's side while Mark was on my side. He stood for the virtue, the austere superiority, the almost unhuman righteousness of my father, while with Mark I found an easy companionship, a quick understanding, a warm support. 

Mark played football. He was the champion wrestler of the high school, a fast "track man" and a fine hockey player. He was very popular with the girls and he smoked a pipe. Often without being asked he would wash the dishes for Mama or clean up the house. He frequently used the seniority of his six years over me in an effort to lessen the severity of Papa's discipline whenever I got into trouble. Mathew would say, "Peter ought to be getting better 58

marks in school," or "He ought to wash his ears and clean his fingernails once in a while," or "It's a shame that Peter isn't half as interested in music as he is in baseball or hockey." Mark would praise my baseball pitching and tell me stories about his experiences with girls. Every one, whether lecturing me sincerely in the interest of my own welfare or teasing me on the pretext of warning me for my own good, would invariably conclude by saying, "Now do you think Mathew would have ever done a thing like that?" or "Why can't you be a little more like your brother Mathew?" or "Do you ever expect to get where 

Mathew is, if you keep on doing the way you're doing?"

My respect for Mathew was so great that I always thought that I ought to do things as he would do them. 

I nevertheless felt that I enjoyed doing things more as Mark would do them. That circumstance enlarged and complicated the conflict between my heart and my mind by implying that the latter could develop only by triumphing over the former. Indeed the aim of education then seemed to be to triumph over the things one enjoyed. 

Even physical exercise was ideally engaged in "for the aid and service of the mind." One must have a sound body in order to have a sound mind.

The evidence of my failure to develop any precocious intelligence is now, of course, very sadly plain. My adolescent mind accepted and approved of all those fanatical, perverted theories of "education" which were then considered all-important. Had it not been for my lower ganglia (that is, my gross body) I would very likely have been lost; I would have been so properly, so thoroughly "educated" by the time that I had finished school that I would never have had room for another idea. 

Most all of us have in our youth been exposed to the 59

perverted mind-development-mania of formal "education." Those who succumb present in their maturity that sad spectacle of body which has been stunted and distorted by suppression, a bundle of neuroses. They have minds which have been so preoccupied with hard tasks in their youth that they are now completely hardened: no resiliency, no plasticity left; not the slightest chance for the infiltration of another idea. "You may be sure," Miss Haywood, the teacher of the eighth grade, would say, "that the boys who have the highest marks in their lessons are the ones who will grow up to be our best lawyers, doctors, ministers, and engineers. They will be the famous and respected men in a few years. And the time is not so far off, as some of you boys think. Here you'll be going to high school next fall, and it will only be a short time then before you are men. And then if you haven't an education, what will you do? You'll have to dig ditches! You won't be able to play baseball or spend your evenings reading adventure stories. No, indeed."

The glistening snow of winter which invited me to go skiing, tobogganing and sliding down hill, to build formidable snow forts and to acquire the knack of throwing a snowball straight to the most vulnerable . . . the target! The red blood which charged my muscles with energy and enriched my gonads so that they would not be satisfied with those highly recommended mental activities of learning the multiplication table, playing scales on the piano, memorizing Longfellow's poetry, and parsing words from the "Lady of the Lake"! For those influences 

I am grateful; as I am for the thrilling flight of a cedar arrow and the interest of Grandfather, who taught me how to make it so that it would sail accurately through 60

the air; and for the unmanageable preference which I had in my youth for experiencing the pleasure of moonlit evenings in spring with the accompaniment of girls, as against the reputed joys of reading poetry which so felicitously described such experiences. Youth does not need to read poetry. It lives it-or should. Old age is the time for poetry: the poetry which is distilled from the lusty adventures of youth, and the lofty adventures of a mind which is nimbly functioning in maturity. Old age needs poetry as an anodyne for the sense of futility-the poetry of philosophy, the poetry of religion, the poetry which valiantly attempts-and that, of course, is the function of poetry—to give a meaning to this life which so obviously has none. 

Yes, indeed, I am thankful for those influences; I am grateful to Mark, who appreciated the speed with which I could throw a baseball and praised the well-timed "breaks" of my curves; and to Doria Sessions, whose exquisite fascination diverted the flood of my sexual emotions at puberty from the putrefying channels of religion and the paralyzing sublimations of being an "ideal student." It seemed so easy for Mathew to be good. Indeed, he was so good, such a joy to the pride of the family, that as the years went on he could not be improved upon-or, rather, he could not improve. He could not change. He learned to smoke, and to drink a little. He even discovered that he was an atheist. But until the War came along and he went to France, there was nothing of any importance that he could ever learn.

Yet it wasn't the War or it wasn't France which restored his mind—at least partially-to a degree of plasticity: it was the Sauternes from Gironde and the brandies from Cognac. 61

Pour wine for us, whose golden color 

Is like a water stream kissing flowers of saffron. 

Pour us wine to make us generous 

And carelessly happy in the old way. 

More cups! More cups! for death will have his day; 

His are we and he ours. 2 During this period I grew so rapidly, becoming so big for my age, that the phenomenon aroused almost daily comment from members of the family, visiting relatives, the clerks at the grocery-store, the Normal School girls, and the various ladies of the M. E. Church who couldn't avoid noticing me while paying their calls on Mama. Nearly every one had some theory concerning my extraordinary development. Mama, borrowing her conjecture from my maternal grandmother, supposed that it was due to the rest which my stomach had enjoyed during the period I had had typhoid fever some years before. Grandpa poohpoohed such a notion. "Huh!" he would interject, “you wonder why he grows so fast? You'd find mighty little mystery to it if you'd just set still a minute and watch him eat. . . . I'll be a donkey if he don't swallow enough at a meal to keep a dozen blacksmiths goin' . . . Huh!”

Mark explained that my growth was perfectly natural because I was outdoors in the sunshine and fresh air most of the time, working, playing, and exercising. If I had not been helping him with the chores very regularly he would insist that I was of course so lazy that my body, having nothing else to do, simply had to grow. 62

Mathew, before he went to California, had occasionally made the generalization (with an air of one uncovering a penetrating scientific fact) that: "In the field of human biology one discovers that there are, roughly speaking, two general types: one in which the mind predominates and which is invariably weak physically, sometimes small, and usually distinguished by extraordinary intellectual development; the other in which the body predominates, invariably growing very rapidly and to unusual strength or size, and frequently characterized by a dulness in school and a lack of mental development when it reaches physical maturity." (Mathew was a small, dark-haired young man with bright brown eyes-and a comfortable reputation for prudence and scholarship.)

We always think less of those virtues and accomplishments which we possess easily. If we speak German well, we listen more eagerly to words in praise of our French, which is not so good; or if we dance awkwardly we are much more aware of a gain in gracefulness than we are of our skill on the trombone which we already play with great excellence. So I was vastly less conscious of my physical development than I was of my mental inadequacies and moral shortcomings. Indeed, with the exception of one pubertal detail, I rarely thought of my anatomical evolution. I wanted to be big, of course, so I could play on the high school football team. I wanted to be bigger than 

Johnny Frobisher; and I wanted to be strong enough so that when the dashing horse which drew Doria Sessions and her mother past our house on their Sunday afternoon drive would suddenly decide to run away, I, with my best suit on, could plunge out into the road, and, throwing myself in front of the violent runaway, save the ladies from a fatal accident. But beyond those ambitions towards 63

whose achievement I did exactly nothing, I harbored no uncommon awareness of my physique save for that one exception which I have just a moment ago mentioned. 

And in that one detail I was embarrassed by the magnitude of my superiority.

Among us boys, during our glorious hours of swimming along the secluded shores of the Racquette River, there existed a rivalry which, occasionally abandoning trial of strength and endurance for simple tests of size, invariably made me a champion. The associations which attended such a championship were not always flattering towards its youthful possession in a community whose Puritan notions penetrated even into the most abandoned moments of adolescence. Shame was my reward then for a magnitude towards the dependency of which I had not exerted the merest voluntary effort, nor had but the slightest willing concern. In later years there were many enjoyable reasons which turned my embarrassment into pride and my indifference into pleasurable interest. 3 The absence of Mathew and Papa made the household more carelessly happy. Though the coterie of Normal girls who boarded with Mama had undoubtedly admired Mathew and yearned for his attentions, they seemed now to lose under the immediate spell of Mark's good-humored disposition and his unconstrained graciousness all their uneasy thoughts of ambitious marriages. Even Sundays now took on a dégagé atmosphere. After Sunday School, which I attended willingly since it was now not preceded by the unendurable confinement of an hour and a half of church, but rather by a lazy extra hour in bed, I could 64

change into my old clothes and easily win my mother's indulgence for activities which were frowned upon when 

Papa was at home.

Not that she gave me permission to spend Sunday afternoons making hockey sticks in Grandfather's little woodworking shop in the woodshed, but having once reminded me that it was Sunday and that I ought to stop playing and working for at least one day out of the week, she would leave me to my own devices, never snooping around to see what I might be up to, and offering no admonitions unless I happened to openly violate some Sunday regulation before her eyes. On these latter occasions-which, incidentally, I seriously tried to make as few as possibleshe would look at me with an expression which seemed to depict the shame and guiltiness she hoped I would feel. 

And she would say with calm piety, "Peter, Peter! Have you forgotten that this is Sunday?" or, "Darling, can't you find something else to do today, and put that off until tomorrow?"

Depending upon the degree of unwillingness which I felt, I would either leave the scene in silence, affecting a deep and irreparable sense of injury, or I would snarl my grief, “Gosh, it's getting so a fellow can't do a darn thing around here except sit in a chair all day and then go to bed!"-realizing all of the time, of course, that never before had I had such liberties on Sunday, yet greedily accepting the cakes and ale of my new life as though they were the bread and water of an existence which had succeeded a paradise of freedom and plenty. 

But Mark, on the other hand, having greater privileges now than he had ever had before, showed, by his acceptance of them as precious responsibilities, his innate aristocracy. Though he liked church no more than I did he 65

modestly felt that it was incumbent upon him to represent the family at services each Sunday, especially since Mama, who was occupied this year with the care of our new baby sister, could not attend. And if he had more money to spend than he had ever had before, since it was now his privilege to do the shopping for the household, he spent less and less on himself. And because throughout youth his good nature and easy amenability had made him quite immune to the harsher aspects of Papa's discipline, he also for that very reason felt less of the common spirit of rebelliousness toward adult authority. He graced his position of authority with the gentle urbanity of a young man who might have known no restraint throughout his life save that which was willingly self-imposed. With all the popularity which the exuberance of his eighteen years, the charm of his barytone voice, his athletic prowess, and the eminence of being a senior at the high school gave him, he made it a point of respect to be at home more often than usual, and a matter of duty to help me put the finishing touches to the bobsleds (we call them "rips" in those days) which I had been laboriously getting together from my old sleds and numerous contributions from Grandfather's workshop. When he discovered that the cushion I had secured for my rip had been purloined from one of the pews in the M. E. Church, he said not a word.

The Normal School girls, after the first few weeks of remarking to Mama at each meal: "Doesn't the house seem almost empty with Mr. Holland and Mathew gone?" and, "I'll bet you miss them awfully, Mrs. Holland, but I guess we miss 'em almost as much!" soon began to speak of 

Papa and Mathew with less frequency and, indeed, even they seemed to enjoy themselves more as they realized that the happiness of the household was something more 66

than a brief holiday from which they were suddenly to awaken into the constrained atmosphere of stern duty. 

These daughters of ministers, deacons, and pillars of sundry Methodist churches from the smaller towns surrounding Potsdam presently began to blossom from the quiescent, chrysaloid stage of their metamorphoses. Their faces gradually changed from their expressionless, green immobility into an open-eyed, alert awakening, bringing a sparkle to their eyes and changing the customary green of their complexions to a vivid pink.

One morning Alice, feeling so conscious of her new freedom that she was eager to test it to extraordinary limits, came down to breakfast without her corset on. To be sure, this most daring violation of a custom rigidly prescribed for girls of Alice's figure and her seventeen years was noticed by neither Mark nor myself, so perfectly were the dresses of respectable girls in those days in Potsdam designed to conceal the true contours of the maidens whom they were supposed to adorn. But my sister Ruth happening to put her arm around Alice as they left the dining-room together, withdrew it with such startled and conspicuous haste, blushing and giggling with the grotesque modesty of her sixteen years, that Alice, as though forgetting the resolution of boldness with which she had originally struck out into the uncharted territory of feminine freedom, suddenly became self-conscious and hastily shot a panicky, surreptitious glance toward Mark. The glance, an instantaneous survey of both of our faces, discovered an alert masculine curiosity written there. It fatally reopened those well-worn prudish channels in her own cerebrum, and thereby guided the whole flood and energy of her reaction into the task of painting her cheeks, her neck and her forehead a significant crimson. 67

Mark, with that intuition which is occasionally possessed by men but always exclusively attributed to women, not only sensed the concealed aspects of the immediate situation, but he also seemed to divine the yearning which had evolved them. Ever eager to put one at ease in the frequently embarrassing accompaniments of iconoclastic adventures, he changed his smile for a look of serious cogitation and said with an assumed scientific detachment: "If a girl today has the courage to go without corsets she not only shows a lot of good common sense, but she also looks ten times more healthy and attractive." 

Though I wanted to share in the respect which I suspected was due to Mark's point of view, I nevertheless felt the giggling bashfulness of the girls to be more exciting and was secretly hoping that some more remarks might be made which would have even a franker suggestion of feminine intimacies and sexual piquancy. It was as if my mind, so concerned with the lofty emulation of Mark's grand manner, so taken up with the admirability of this virtue which I was assuming, could not be bothered with the racy and forbidden preoccupations of the nerves which served my gonads and my adrenals.

I walked across the sitting-room to the big chair in which Alice had sat down so self-consciously but a few minutes before. From first posing myself with an affectation of extraordinary casualness on the arm of the chair, I slid gradually down onto her lap ( a habit which though begun years previously with the indulgence-if not interest-of the various bevies of Normal girls which boarded at our house year after year, still continued, though with much less frequency and much greater enjoyment on my part and an obvious embarrassment upon the part of the girls). 

My mind did not then pay enough attention to what my 68

body was doing to detect how patently its actions were belieing the rarified elevation of my cerebrum. It was not until the unwontedly yielding response of the flesh around Alice's waist suddenly sent a shiver of delight and a shudder of portentous danger coursing through my body that I realized with a flash the shameful ignominy of my position. I suddenly stood up and walked over to the piano. The censorship of my mind, having at last been brought to a critical consideration of the unmoral activities of my libido, and thereby having heightened my enjoyment with a tingling sensation of wickedness, began now to wage a battle with my impulses. 

That original urge which had impelled me to sit for a moment on Alice's lap to discover how her waist felt when it was not surrounded by the hard, unresisting wall of a corset, was now giving way to a desire which was customarily much stronger in me. I wished to see how 

Alice looked (indeed, to see how almost any girl looked) without her clothes on. And because of this conflict the ideal of "Pure Thoughts" (impure thoughts meaning almost any thoughts which had to do with the pleasant gratification of one's bodily impulses and appetites) and the irrepressible flood of my desire to explore the mystery of sex, I sat there on the piano stool in such deep abstraction that I neither noticed my mother come into the room nor heard her ask me twice if I would not water the horse and bring her a few sticks of wood from the woodshed. 

Her third request she preceded by an energetic shake of my shoulder. "My soul and body!" she cried. “Are you deaf, Peter, or wool-gathering, or sound asleep settin' up!

I've spoke to you twice to give the horse a pail o' water, and got no more motion out o' you than as though I were talkin' to a statue!" 69

And then as I looked up at my mother with the disturbed, guilty look of my adolescent conflict still written on my face, she smiled through her impatience and sought to extenuate the obvious stupidity of my reactions by saying, "My goodness, I guess it's 'cause you're growin' so fast you haven't got time for anything else."

But once out of the house my abstraction vanished. I began a round of alert physical activity which, commencing with watering the horse and cleaning out the stable, culminated that evening in taking my new "rip" over to the 

Cedar Street hill. There in the frosty sparkling loveliness of a midwinter night, I shed the melancholy conflicts of adolescence for the untroubled merriment of youthful exhilaration.

Thus indeed are all the problems of adolescence solvedin the spontaneous pleasure of the outdoors, free from the corrupting hot-beds of schools and churches, and the prurient atmosphere of Puritan households; away from the pollutions of adult attention, adult respectability, and adult ideals. The solution to my first grave problem of adolescence was fortuitously made easier that night on Cedar Street hill in the miraculous circumstance of meeting Doria Sessions face to face for the first time, feeling the substance of her divinity and hearing the music of her voice. 

Although Ruth had known Doria Sessions for several months and had brought her to our house on numerous occasions, I had never met her. A circumstance which was entirely due to the hypocrisy which had blossomed so in me that I was already a master in the practice of affecting indifference to the very things which fascinated me the most with their promises of unbelievable pleasures. I could display also the most pretentious enthusiasm for the repulsive manners and substances of Righteousness, whose single 70

recommendation was that it helped me along the path to Heaven-or, rather, more importantly, it snatched me occasionally from the road to Hell. 

Yet in those moments of happy solitude, away from the influences of home, church, and school, the tantalizing attentiveness of the swarm of Normal girls and the distractions of adult civilization-that is to say, during those moments of self-reliance and courage I often had-I was inspired to resolve that the very next time I saw Doria Sessions I would speak to her with the gallantry of a knight, smitten in love by his Lady and valorous enough to declare a hopeless bondage. But later as each opportunity presented itself, the stability of my resolve became a phantom and vanished in the ethereal limbo of neurotic desires. Just as even our strongest desires, if they are accompanied with the conviction that they are unattainable, will never get beyond the state of wishes.

Confidence or desperate necessity is the impelling stimulus to action, as it is the fructifying fertilizer of sound philosophy. It was desperate necessity which forced me to meet 

Doria Sessions that night in midwinter on Cedar Street hill. 

When I stood there proudly by my rip waiting for the passengers to fill it up so that we might once again guide the fleet vehicle on another fast and thrilling descent to the bottom of the hill, I could not know that out of the shadowy figures which stood there, the presence of Doria Sessions would suddenly materialize. Nor could I know that that presence once materialized would immediately come toward me and sit down on my bobsled directly behind the position which I must necessarily take in order to steer it downhill. Not being forewarned, my will to retreat was overcome by the more powerful necessity of appearing to be a gentleman before the eyes of Doria. As the bobsled filled up 71

I took my position as the brave guide of the precious cargo and presently started on the swiftest, most thrilling, most wonderful journey that I had thus far taken in my uneventful life.

Mutely, doggedly, I took my position on the bobsled. My whole being was electrically charged with strange, ineffable sensations aroused by the speculation as to whether Doria would, according to the customary ritual of sliding downhill, place her hands over the top of my shoulders or around my waist. And when, after sitting down, I felt her gloved hands gently grasping my sweater it was as though an electric circuit were suddenly completed. All the accumulated electrons created from month to month by each new vision and thought of Doria, stored up in the pole of my receptive body, instantaneously discharged themselves back into the hands of her who had generated them. My substance shuddered with strange delight. I grasped the steering-bar in front of me and yearned for the rip to start its flight down the hill, wishing that it might gather such speed and encounter such danger as would require me to display superior skill and superhuman courage. Now that I had at last come in contact with Doria Sessions-that her knees were pressing against my sides and her hands were grasping my waist-I felt that I could and that I must do something to prove to her that by her contact I had ceased being a common mortal and had suddenly become a god.

Though the bobsled was heavily loaded and traveling with a speed at least equal to any that it had ever made before, it seemed to be going disgustingly slow. It would have taken the speed of lightning to satisfy me. Even that would have been an incident which I would have accepted with easy nonchalance. The world was at my feet. With 

Doria sitting so closely, so snugly, behind me, a new vista 72

of life appeared before me: a vision of myself striding triumphantly over the world, looking down upon it with an infinite pity and understanding.

At that moment, as though to retard my spirit from soaring to far more dangerous heights, the rip came upon the mundane peril of a sharp curve. I leaned toward the inside to indicate the change in the center of gravity which would be necessary in order to make it without spilling. Doria failed to follow my signal the curve was upon us in a flash! "Lean!!" I shrieked. But it was too late... the rest of the passengers, failing to get the signal, were unprepared. 

The rip swayed precariously, and suddenly shot over, catapulting us swiftly into the hardpacked snow of the curve and scraping several inches of skin from my left shin. There were howls of disapproval and derision. "Why don't you learn how to steer?"... "For Cat's sakes, give us a warning before you try to murder us!” . . . "Holy Moses! You'd better take a few more lessons before you take any one else for a ride!”

I stood up, torn between the urges to vindicate myself and to protect Doria. "For the love of Mike," I retorted, "don't you all know enough to lean when you go around a curve?" "Yea! Yea!" they shouted. "Don't you know enough to give us a signal!"

I looked toward Doria. She was still sitting in the hardpacked snow holding on to her ankle. My mouth was already open, to scream the announcement that I had given a signal. But instantly the idea vanished. There came to me the realization that I might for the first time in my life be a hero. I rushed toward Doria, exclaiming, "Are you hurt? ... I'm sorry! ... It was all my fault! . . . Did you 73

sprain your ankle! . . . It was all my fault!" I did not dare touch her, since to have done so would have brought down upon me the ridicule of the rest of the crowd. But as I stood there leaning over her she looked up, wiped some tears from her eyes and said, "I'm all right . I'm not crying. It's just the cold breeze that makes my eyes water. 

Besides, it wasn't your fault, anyway; it was mine." 

Then, like a lady of twenty instead of fourteen, she reached her hand toward me with the unspoken but unmistakable indication that I might help her up. As I did so a new feeling was born within me. I experienced the sensation of being a hero. I felt the reward of self-sacrifice and had dim inchoate intimations of the blindness, the futility, the pitiable emptiness of a life devoted wholly to selfvindication. Yet somewhere in the midst of this elated feeling there resided an element which could neither be resolved into the emotion of pleasure nor eliminated. An element of censorship made me sense that my heroism, my self-sacrifice, had not been deliberately intended without regard for my own selfish feelings, but rather had been suddenly provoked by the opportunity of appearing before Doria as a much better and greater person than I actually was.

But now, as Doria had risen before me, such considerations lost all chance of entering my consciousness, since I was involved with the more important problem of when to let go of her hand. If I should release it too soon she might think that I worshiped her less than I truly did, or that I was angry at her for causing the rip to tip over, or—worse still-that I was unaware of the customs and manners of her class of society and hence did not know just how long a girl's hand should be held under such circumstances. If I did not release it soon enough the rest of the crowd would 74

be embarrassing me with remarks about my attentiveness, which would be unforgivable. 

Again I resolved to sacrifice all of my feelings in order to help her. I would continue my solicitude until she indicated that she had no further need of it. And once more I was to be convinced that genuine virtue always received its reward, for just as I determined to forget myself in her service she peered intently into my face, smiling-not an ordinary smile, not frivolous or perfunctory, but one which expressed such feeling that it might well have turned into tears—and tears and opening her eyes wider as if to indicate that I might have the privilege of seeing better into her very soul which must have resided directly behind them, she said, "Thanks for helping me and . . . and everything . I'll be all right in a minute, if you'll just help me walk a few steps." The rip had been righted by the other boys. I led her over toward it and insisted that she sit on it while we drew her uphill. · • 4 At the top of the hill Marian Premier's house sat back from the street across a broad lawn decorated with iron statues of a St. Bernard dog and a many-antlered stag, an imposing mansion of the period of the 1890's. From its elaborate bay-windows and impressive, silo-looking turret guarding the right front corner facing the street, to its florid cornice made up of every conceivable twist which nature had found possible to evolve in the minds of a carpenter-architect and a resourceful woodturner, it expressed the height of artistic yearning of Mr. Premier-and indeed of the average successful merchant, banker, and manufacturer of Potsdam in those days. I had been in it 75

once, at a party which had been given by Marian a few years previous, during the height of Papa's rise and prosperity. Marian had had many parties since then, but neither 

Ruth nor I had been invited, despite the fact that during all of that period in which I was not invited to her house she was extremely nice to me in school, paying me more compliments than ever, talking with me in a jolly way as though to indicate to the rest of the boys and girls that I was one of her treasured friends. 

Now as we reached the top of the hill and I saw the lights gleaming through the plateglass windows of the Premier mansion-giving them the appearance of burnished brass shields held at the unprotected openings of dark castle walls and warning such interlopers as me against ever trying to enter-I did not, for the first time during the many winters I had been sliding down the Cedar Street hill from this very spot, feel that wretched pain of envy and inferiority. I smiled to myself now as I gazed at the house with the consciousness that the girl who was riding on my sled was so far superior to any of the Premiers that they would figuratively break their necks in order to ingratiate themselves with her and her family. While I, but a moment before, had been allowed to gaze through this girl's eyes into her very soul as she looked at me and spoke to me with an expression which, I was sure, she had never given to another person in her whole life. 

I even felt sorry for the Premier family, who had struggled so hard to curry the favor of Doria Sessions, when I thought of the possibility of their coming out of their castle and observing that after all of their efforts the prize which they sought had fallen to me, a parvenu. As I looked toward the house I hoped passionately that the big, heavy plateglass door would open and Mrs. Premier would come out, 76

come down the walk to witness me in all of my glory. The door stayed closed. But as I turned around to assist Doria from the bobsled Marian Premier came hurrying up the hill. The news of the accident had been relayed to her on her last coast to the bottom. She rushed to Doria's side just as I was helping her to stand up. "Darling, darling," she cried, "are you hurt badly? Come into the house and Mommy'll call Dr. Botsford immediately! He'll hurry right here! Oh, you poor dear! What is it, your ankle?" 

I was angry. Why couldn't Marian Premier leave Doria alone? She saw-or at least she ought to have seen-that I was taking care of her and that she needed no further assistance. I clung to Doria's arm with defiance and was soon repaid for my tenaciousness. "I'm all right! I'm all right!" Doria insisted. “It's nothing. 

Thank you! Thank you! I just banged my ankle and it was a little sore at first, but really it's all right now." 

My hopes soared; my elation grew; but just as my triumph was about to be complete, my prize threatened to vanish from my hands. "I will go in your house for a few minutes, though, Marian," said Doria, “. . . if you don't mind. I feel a little cold from riding up the hill instead of walking."

It was then that I suddenly and at last came down to earth and realized that, after all, I was an alien to such superior beings as the Sessions and the Premiers. A wave of resentment surged up within me, from the soles of my twice-tapped shoes to the frayed edges of my three-year-old toque. Abruptly releasing Doria's arm I stood there, my face flaming with embarrassment, bitterness and defeat. I would have turned and run had I not thought that to do so would only add to my disgrace. Forgetting all about the glance 77

which she had given me at the bottom of the hill I was only certain that when any one more nearly approaching Doria's own social class was around she was eager to forsake my assistance for his company.

But just as my wretchedness seemed to have reached the depths of all that I could bear, she turned to me. Like a princess in a fairy tale, who at the critical moment will not allow her lover to be slain by the king, she said, "Peter, you'd better come in too, because you must have hurt your leg awfully when the rip fell on top of it.” And as I stood there mute and immobile, unable to displace the misery and resentment which had been gradually overpowering me, unable even to grasp at once the full meaning of the magic words which had just been spoken, Doria seized my arm and started walking toward the house. 

At last I was in the Premier mansion again and I had in effect been brought there under the auspices of the most captivating member of the Sessions family. While I had never entered a household of any importance before with such triumph and such ease, the little trivialities of velvet rugs, ornate bric-à-brac, Mrs. Premier's haughty noseglasses, the unwonted solicitude of Mr. Premier, the everimpressive curtain of beads and seashells which separated the parlor from the sitting-room-the ponderous distraction of material things-all combined to prevent me from savoring and enjoying the victory of the moment. 

Mrs. Premier smiled at me with such warm hospitality as made her severe, sniffish-looking face appear almost kind and human. “When do you expect your father back from 

California?" she asked me. "Isn't he fortunate, though, to be spending the winter in such a heavenly climate!"

I forgave and forgot the fact that she had not invited me to any of Marian's parties since that first and only occasion 78

years before. I was ready now to accept the exclusiveness of her society, against which I had previously so bitterly complained to myself, as a quite natural and necessary condition for maintaining a certain standard of excellence. I felt that now, having been accepted by the Sessions and the Premiers, a further extension of democracy on our part was not only unnecessary but very undesirable.

Later, when the coasting party was over and I was drawing Doria home on my bobsled, all thoughts and feelings of social triumphs vanished from my mind and senses, leaving me with the pure, unviolated happiness of serving this princess whom after years of Galahadian restraint I had at last so fortuitously met. I knew that life could never again be the meaningless round of the trivial pleasures of youth which I had previously known. It opened up now before me a vast promise of magnificent splendor and perpetual bliss. I knew that I could now ascend to such heights or accomplish such feats as had before seemed impossible. My strength had so increased that the bobsled which I was at that moment pulling seemed as light as a feather. I changed the rope in my hand from one finger to another, proving to myself that what had formerly seemed a task for my whole hand was now, with Doria as a burden, but a bagatelle for my little finger. I did not doubt that, having come to myself, having discovered the tremendous sources of strength within me, I would some day quite naturally become the president of the United States and that Doria, as my wife, would come to me for advice upon important matters.

But as I drew near to the Sessions house and could witness again the imposing grandeur of its Georgian majesty, a sense of disquietude assailed me. The purity of my happiness was no more, giving way to the speculation as to how 79

I was ever to become anything very great if I did not already live in a house as magnificent as the Sessions'. I turned to look at Doria (perhaps to console myself with a joy which was permanent-her reassuring loveliness) and encountered her smile. "Let me walk now," she said. "I feel like walking. My ankle actually doesn't hurt any more. Besides you must be awfully tired, drawing me so far." She laughed with a cheeriness that sent chills of pleasure through my whole body. "You get on!" she cried. "Let me pull you! I want to get warmed up."

The thought that she might walk by my side if she got off the bobsled did not influence me to urge her very strongly to ride the rest of the way, and as she stood up and took a hold of the rope alongside of my hand, I had the power of only a feeble protest. Encouraged by this intimacy 

I began to tell her with stammering words and halting sentences (which after they were uttered, I was certain, had destroyed all of the beauty and the eloquence of the ideas which I had intended to express) of how extremely happy I was to have met her and to have talked to her even though I could not at the same time help feeling very sorry that she had to injure her ankle in order to bring it about. "Oh, that's nothing," she said, “my ankle. Don't feel bad about that. It was all my fault anyway. Besides, I'm awfully glad it happened."

And then I tried to paint for her the pictures of all those many occasions upon which I had tried to conceal my interest in her pictures recurring so vividly upon the screen of my memory that I lost all self-consciousness in delineating them before her mind's eye. I told her about the day at the ice rink when I had skated so violently and so preoccupiedly as she passed by with my sister; the innumerable 80

occasions when I had seen her a long way off and had crossed the street in order to avoid the embarrassment of meeting her face to face; the time, when on coming into the house and unexpectedly hearing her voice, I had passed from the dining-room into the reception-hall to avoid her instead of going through the sitting-room, which was my usual custom.

I was rapturously surprised to learn from her lips that those same pictures which I had supposed had no actuality and certainly no importance in the mind of any person but myself were present in all their details on the precious curtain of her memory. It seemed to me that this revelation of 

Doria's unavoidably disclosed a virtue in me which in my most egotistical moments I had never dreamed that I possessed. I felt magnified for a moment into the stature of a superior being. Although the virtue was one which I could not name, nor to which I could assign any definite tangibility, I vaguely sensed that I possessed some sort of attractiveness to girls. I began to wonder what it was, if there might not be some hidden quality in me which I couldn't see but which was startlingly apparent to Doria. Otherwise why had she remembered so vividly all of those many occasions long past when I had so fleetingly and inconspicuously appeared before the veil of her consciousness?

We were now standing in front of the Sessions' house. 

It was time to say goodnight. But having met my princess at last and having discovered that by some miraculous, inexplicable circumstance I was perhaps endeared to her, it seemed that nothing would be harder to bear than to ever separate from her again. If I could only live in the same house with her I was certain I could accomplish tremendous things. That very thought started a stream of fantasies flooding through my mind which became at once so 81

intimate, so daring, so severely forbidden, yet so transportingly wonderful that I could only listen and talk mechanically as I concentrated my attention upon their imagined delights. 

Doria, noticing my abstraction, suddenly exclaimed: "Oh, I'll bet I know what you're thinking about! Your mind is 'way away from here! What will you give me if I can guess where it is?"

The nature of her challenge awakened me to the reality of the moment. The mere suggestion that she could even dare to imagine what I had actually been thinking about -the very boldness of the thought that she could pry into the contents of my reveries which, though wholly concerned with her, I could not in my moments of wildest imagination picture her divining, since I was so positive that she was so far above such impure ideas-all sent a shudder of delight and terror through the tingling reaches of my senses. 

Accusingly she insisted: "You were thinking of Marian Premier!"

And then by that strange phenomenon of our ego which, in the naïveté of adolescent love, assures us that there is no more flattering or more certain evidence of the bondage of another person to ourself-no more positive stimulus for the inflation of our feeling of self-importance-than the manifestation of jealousy, I suddenly came to realize that Doria was, after all, a very human princess. She was a girl, primarily, beneath the halo of divinity and royalty which my imagination had given her. With that realization there came both sadness and courage; the melancholy sadness that accompanies the revelation of the irrevealablethe discovery that she whom we thought was impossible to attain is after all attainable; and the brazen courage which 82

annihilates our humility when the divinity whom we have worshiped discloses passions like our own. Just as a goal which we have long considered to be utterly unachievable by ourselves and hence more precious, more wonderful, more valuable than anything else in the whole world, loses a large part of its magnificence in our eyes as we awake one morning to discover that we have achieved it, so Doria the princess now, despite all of her wealth, her beauty, and her social superiority, became, in the midst of my exaltation, a girl who had fallen in love with me.

After I had said goodnight to her and was pulling my bobsled home through the world of stars and snow crystals, familiar, friendly maple trees and golden-lit windows, walking up Elm Street (though hardly conscious of walking since my feet never seemed to touch the hard-packed snow of the sidewalk) and thinking of the triumph which

I was about to experience when my family and my friends found out that Doria Sessions liked me, the whole appearance of the universe changed before my eyes. Instead of the phantasmagoria of strange substances external to myself for which I had learned throughout the thirteen years of my life names which signified almost nothing, I saw it all now as an anthropomorphic world-material things emanating a spirit of which I was a part, a million friends with whom I had a special relationship of perfect understanding, love, mutual aid, and unique superiority. The cedar hedges smiled at me and said, "Good for you, old boy!"

The vast, blue ceiling with its millions of sparkling yellow eyes continually gazed down upon me with a look of understanding and kept whispering, "You're coming along fine! I am beginning to feel very proud of you!" Old Mrs.

Prince's house stared at me with an old-maidish sniff, but it, too, realized that I commanded respect. 83

I closed the kitchen door behind me and shut out that universe which I had known so intimately during the ten minutes it had taken me to walk from the Sessions house to my own, but there remained the memory of its magic, the stamp of an unforgettable awakening, as I confided to Mama with hysterical triumph that I had walked home with Doria Sessions and that she had invited me into her house. 84

CHAPTER VIII 

Not all of the baths of our household could be taken on Saturday night, so there was always a residue of ablutions which had to be crowded into the brief and bustling hours between late Sunday morning arisings and the deadline of the first bell for church services. Invariably, inevitably, there was a feverish rush of last-minute preparation for church. On account of the emergency there was a general let-down, a sort of hysterical negligence, in the observation of many of the conventions and proprieties of a very Methodist and very moral household. The Normal School girls would carelessly hurry through the halls from the bathroom to bedroom, bedroom to sitting-room sewing-basket and back to bedroom in a disarray of flying kimonos, uncovered petticoats and uncombed hair, which, under normal circumstances, would have been convincing evidence of incorrigible wantonness. 

Doors would not always be tightly closed when one was in the process of dressing. Seven-year-old Johnnie could dash from the bathroom into Mama's room with his clean underwear held in front of him and not "a stitch of clothes on to cover his nakedness." The bathroom door might just as well have been without its bolt, because during these hours it could not be used to insure privacy, for if one of the girls was bathing, another had to use other appurtenances of the bathroom for curling her hair, and if Johnnie were scrubbing his neck or dabbing futilely at his ears, 85

either Mark had to take a shave or I had to have a reluctant bath.

These periods of scurrying and excitement were always a pleasant interlude for me between the pain of getting out of bed and my mother's last-minute inspection as I went out the door and started down Elm Street for Sunday School. Though the spectacle of Alice or Jessie or Caroline in kimono or petticoat was not a particularly exciting one in itself, it always seemed to suggest a world of visual delights which might be terrifyingly wonderful. 

But on this particular Sunday morning, after Doria's transcending revelation of the night before, I consciously banished all thought of wicked curiosity, all lascivious pleasures of peeping, and went about my preparations for Sunday School with all the dignity that I imagined the husband of Doria should have. I was so determined to be upright that 

I bent backward. When necessity took me past the open doors of the girls' rooms I was not satisfied to look straight ahead, but I must also close my eyes. When Mark, coming into my room to get back a necktie, began to relate to me how during the night before, in coming down the upstairs hallway he had met Charlotte arrayed only in her nightgown and had stood there for five minutes embracing her and kissing her until a noise had warned him that their surreptitious spooning might be spied upon, I listened coldly with no show of interest. I struggled with the desire to tell him that such foolish things as he was describing to me no longer held my interest. While Mark, realizing I suppose that there might be other reasons, more momentary and physiological, for my indifference to his voluptuous recital, smiled indulgently and left the room without another word. 

But just as I was struggling before my mirror with the starched rigidity of my stiffest Sunday collar and thinking 86

of how I could contrive to let Doria see me in my Sunday best without appearing to urge my presence upon her, Alice, who had stimulated my imagination just the morning before by coming down to breakfast without any corset on, called down the hall, "Oh, Peter!... Peter, could you come here a moment and do something for me?" The power of habit, the habit of responding eagerly to that particular tone, that irresistible inflection in one of the Normal girls' voices, impelled me to reply, "All right, I'm coming." 

I need not have answered Alice at all. As I walked down the hallway to her room I came to the conclusion that for just this once I would let my newly acquired dignity take a vacation. I pushed open the door of Alice's room without knocking and saw her sitting before the mirror of her dresser clothed in a thin cotton undervest and a pair of muslin drawers. Whether Alice, having set out on the road to emancipation from prudish restrictions, became so bold as to extend her liberty to the point of insisting that there was modesty even in nudity, or whether, sensing by her intuition that any great display of embarrassment—now that I had seen her and the harm was already done-would simply aggravate a pruriency in my supposedly innocent mind, I do not know. Whatever the cause she made no fuss beyond a momentary and natural expression of surprise as she turned her head and saw me standing there staring at her.

Making no effort even to cover her voluptuous knees, which were completely nude, she said, "Peter, will you go downstairs for me and ask your mother if she's got any white elastic?”

And at that instant, partly from the casualness of Alice's voice with its suggestion that between us there existed a 87

profound understanding, an intimate camaraderie which preserved any and all of our actions from the necessity of defense or explanation, and partly because the intimacy of the scene suggested to my imagination that I was once more acting the part of Doria's trusted husband, I changed from a being dominated by the passion to see and to feel into a super-Sir Galahad. I went downstairs on my errand, pitying those poor weak creatures who had not yet steeled themselves in self-control.

Too late we learn that our life has its own plan, that it takes its pattern from nothing else that has preceded it or is contemporary. If Shelley wrote sublime poetry at the age of nineteen, we—no matter how passionately we live solely to emulate Shelley-will struggle like a lion and bring forth sonnets at twenty which are sonnets only by the courtesy of our critical indulgence. And if Schumann said of Brahms when Brahms was in his tender twenties: "Hats off! gentlemen, a genius!" we-no matter how hard we punch the piano, grow a beard, study theory and smoke Brazilian cigars-will in the end discover that our string quartettes are not masterpieces, but are played mainly because certain fiddlers and 'cellists are our generous friends. Our life follows a capricious pattern. We are, in the final analysis, distinct individuals although we belong to the Elks and sing songs at the Rotary. 

So I found in my quixotic career as Sir Galahad that there were various embellishments and bastardized imitations which not only expediency but the necessity of being myself demanded. And if in my Knights-of-the-Round 

Table conduct toward Doria I called her Guinevere and 88

plagiarized certain other prerogatives of Sir Lancelot to accommodate the lure of her lips and to satisfy the compromises which I had to make with the biological insistences of my own peculiar make-up, she accepted me as a veritable Galahad, allowing me to carry her books home from school, saddle her pony, make a stirrup of my hands while she mounted horseback, and embrace her warmly when the impulse of the moment demanded it and there happened to be no one looking. We talked of marriage, undying love, and fidelity to death. We plotted ways of circumventing the disapproving eyes and meddlesome tactics of her spinsterly aunt; and when, just before Lent-my father being still in California and in the absence of his usual restriction I was allowed to step on the stage of the Town Hall Opera House and become an actor in a home talent show in which Doria was one of the star performers-I decided that instead of becoming an engineer I would make myself a great actor, she confided to me that she hoped more than anything else to become an actress. If she did we could travel around the world together sharing the applause of enthusiastic audiences. 3 

Winter was coming into its grey decline. March thaws had turned the world outside into an ashen harmony with the atmosphere of Lent. The blackboards in the eighth grade classroom at school reflected the dull and deadening influences of parsing and extracting square roots. Black crows shrieked in the tops of barren trees. At home the chimneys of the kerosene-oil lamps grew smoky and discouraging. The only bright moments in my leaden world were those which I spent in the dazzling splendor of the 89

Sessions' house. There, all too rarely, Ruth and I went to practice acting and to rehearse cantatas for Easter. 

But even there the atmosphere of seriousness penetrated disquietingly. While I wanted to become a great actor, as 

I had wanted to become a great musician and an engineer, I did not want to be bothered with uninteresting tasks of memorizing recitations, learning gestures and making myself enunciate properly. If I could not play at becoming an actor, but had to work instead, the excitement of applauding audiences would lose a great deal of its glamour. 

Doria's aunt, who taught elocution and was the head of the only musical conservatory which then existed in Potsdam, insisted upon telling me not to say things in a sing-song voice, that I must use more expression and that I must not stand before my audiences like a bump on a log. As though despairing of my ever learning to express myself properly by her merely telling me how to do so, she would ask Doria to make my speeches as they should be made, so that I might at least learn something from her example. 

I would sink into a dudgeon from whose depths I would look upon the scene before me as one in which I could never achieve the superior rôle that I was so certain in the beginning would naturally fall to me. 

Doria would use a more gentle manner in trying to impart to me the technique of the art which she had already so precociously mastered under the instruction of her aunt, complimenting me upon an aptitude which did not exist and encouraging me in an ambition which I was growing less and less eager to realize. The joy which I had originally felt in being at last received into the Sessions' parlor and occupying so much of Doria's attention under such dazzling circumstances became inversely proportional to the number of occasions during which she demonstrated her 90

own histrionic ability and attempted to develop mine. I said to myself with the repetitiousness of an adolescent who has discovered at last the consoling truth of a tiresome and hackneyed proverb, "Alas, true love never runs smooth.

Alas, how well I now realize it." During those melancholy moments when I had to pile out of bed into the cold greyness of early morning to dress, take care of the furnace, and water the horse, I felt that though the sadness of my life was unique I would bear it. Sir Galahad, of course, went through a great deal, suffering terribly, but he could not have known of the unequaled pain, the transcendant pleasure of such unparalleled love as I had for Doria. ... 

Late that spring it was pleasant to go about saying: "Papa is coming back from California," and to hear people answer, "Oh, indeed-he must have had a wonderful time 

I'll bet you'll be tickled to see him back." Though his absence had meant for me a holiday from physical chastisement, it was wonderful to feel the distinction and importance of having a father "return from California." The memory of harsh discipline was momentarily submerged in the anticipation of social aggrandizement. 

He arrived with his telescope-grip filled with oranges, picked with his own hand from those unbelievable trees in that incredible country. His bags, boxes and suitcases were veritable mines of souvenirs: picture books of Pike's Peak, the Grand Canyon, San Francisco's Earthquake; sea-shells and star-fish from Catalina Island, pieces of the Petrified 

Forest, baskets of orangewood, trinkets from Chinatown.

For days the greatest hardship in life was to have to leave the contemplation of this booty and the fascination of Fa- 91

ther's replies to endless curious questions for the hollow monotony of school. In desperation I would carry off photograph albums and mementoes to display proudly to the teacher and my companions. When this lost its novelty and impressiveness, it left me with the sad emptiness of a declining feeling of glory and importance. But the experience taught me nothing, for I was not yet ready to learn of the treacherousness of pleasure and pride unseasoned by conflict and unearned by struggle. I went my adolescent way, in search of something for nothing. 

The elation of the household over Father's return was as shortlived as the reflected glory which I experienced therefrom, for not long after his arrival the futility of his expedition to California became more and more apparent when all of his excuses and explanations for his failure to get a job did nothing which we could see to improve the gradual decline of our comfort. There were bills to be paid at numerous stores and he had not brought back gold from California to pay them. There were things which we wanted to buy, but could not. We must pinch and strain, suffer embarrassment and penury because he had failed to do what he had set out to do; what he was supposed to do; or what we expected him to do.

So, with the protected and fatuous point of view of youth which demands and cries for so much, and in turn gives so little and so arrogantly, I inwardly resented and blindly criticized his failure to provide me with security and comfort. The figure of omnipotence which my childish imagination had once given him now began to crumblejust as do the images of perfection which, born in our own minds, we transplant and incarnate in some one else in order that we might have the pleasure of leaning on a strength which we ourselves cannot achieve. When they 92

prove themselves illusions, it arouses us to cry out to the world that we have been injured and deceived, cruelly and without reason, forgetting, if ever realizing, that the world owes us nothing.

I was thinking that I would work and earn money. 

Though such an upsetting circumstance was hard to reconcile with the chivalrous career of a young Sir Galahad, the influence of the Alger books was strong enough to enable me during the emergency to see myself as composite hero of "Strive and Succeed," "Sink or Swim," "Pluck and Luck." In prospect, the main factor to disturb me was the contemplation of what my companions would think (what Doria would think of me) if I had to go to work. 

Becoming a hero was extremely pleasant to dream about, but the embarrassment of the struggle was always there, reminding me and urging me to postpone the commencement of my efforts until a more propitious moment than the present one. School had come to an end and summer vacation had started, but I was not yet quite ready to put my decision to work into definite execution. 

One afternoon Father, who had in the face of necessity swallowed his great pride as the builder of the M. E. 

Church and taken odd jobs about the town building piazzas, making petty alterations, putting on screen doors and shingling roofs, came home early from work and asked me to step into the house, saying that he wanted to see me. The seriousness of his voice reminded me of similar portentous requests which had always preceded a whipping. Despite the fact that he had been home for many weeks and had used only his voice as a means of disciplining me, I trembled guiltily as I walked into the sittingroom.

But just as I was determining in my mind to exert my 93

utmost powers in the support of my innocence (innocence of what I did not know) and speculating even upon the possibility of brave defiance, I discerned in the expression of his face into which I peered most anxiously as I entered the room in order to discover, if I could, the degree of his anger-that twinkle of amusement, and satisfaction with himself, which invariably preceded the conferment of his favors and the perpetration of his rare jokes. Such a glow of pleasure began radiating through my being that I immediately felt that nothing he could give me or say to me would furnish me half the joy as the unspoken assurance which I had already received that, whatever my sins were, they had not yet been discovered and thus I was neither to be punished nor forced into the issue of rebellion or acceptance. 

My father said to me, "I have some good news for you!"

There were several moments between his announcement and my full realization of the possibilities of his words. "You know Mr. Austin, who is blind and a dealer in horses and cattle and farm machinery? Well, he spoke to me today about a good reliable boy to drive him around the country. 

He is familiar with roads, and knows all the people. It would be quite easy for him to direct you. Would you like a job at five dollars a week?"

In the face of such an unbelievably wonderful prospect of actual, tangible happiness, I thought it would be crazy to show how I felt. If anybody knew how much I would like a job driving a horse around the country, driving all day with nobody else to take the reins out of my hands, he would certainly feel that it wasn't right to heap so much pleasure upon a single boy. If Papa knew how the very thought of earning five dollars a week-of my own money -sent shudders of delight up and down my spine he could 94

not help deciding that such intense happiness was actually sinful. So when I said half-enthusiastically to my father, “I guess it would be all right. . . . I guess I'd like it", it was not because I could not conceal my enthusiasm that I permitted some of it to enter into my remark, but because I hoped to convey with the affectedness of my assumed feeling the impression that though I was very willing to do whatever he might suggest I was not too eager to drive a horse about the country or to get five dollars a week for doing it.

The next morning, in my irrepressible eagerness, I was up before six o'clock and eating breakfast with my father in preparation for calling upon Mr. Austin a full two hours later. It seemed taken for granted that I must realize what a lucky boy I was and that I must be very careful not to let my enthusiasm run away with me. Before eight o'clock

I was at the back door of the Austin house, waiting in hope and trepidation, saying one moment to myself, "Of course, I'm going to get it! I'm going to get it!" and the next moment, "You're crazy! You're crazy! You won't get it! Not a chance! Not a chance!" 

The door opened and Mrs. Austin said, “Good-morning, sonny, won't you come in? I guess you're Mr. Holland's boy. I'll call Mr. Austin and he'll be right down to see you."

I entered into a misty, tenuous state of suspense like that which would overtake my whole being during that interval between the instant at which I would leap from a high springboard and the second my body was enveloped by water. I was a helpless mass hurling through the air waiting for whatever words Mr. Austin might say to interrupt my flight through the nothingness of uncertainty with the solid substance of a decision. Or perhaps I was 95

like that amphibian creature, the frog, leaping from a watery world for the first time on to the promised land of new experience into that incredible atmosphere called air, existence in which calls upon new powers, new adjustments, new responsibilities. I have never felt more daring, more enterprising than I did that moment when I thrust myself by my own will and physical propulsion before Mr. 

Austin to ask him for the privilege of driving his horse around the country for him and implying that I was willing to accept five dollars a week from him for doing it. 

I had met one blind person before in my life and he was an extraordinary individual. He frequently played piano solos at chapel in the Normal School, and his reputation as a good student was known far and wide. His ability to go almost everywhere with only the external help of his cane was one of the great miracles of life which had been violently impressed upon me each time I attempted—in imitation of him-to walk along the street with my eyes. shut. And now as Mr. Austin walked toward me, smiling and extending his hand, I again felt that inexpressible awe and wonder which one commonly feels in the presence of a genius, a miracle, or an inexplicable universe. As he placed one hand on the top of my head, letting the other move sensitively over the muscles of my shoulders and arms, all thoughts of the primary purpose of my visit vanished momentarily from my consciousness. I said, "How do you do, sir?" with the respect and feeling with which I would have spoken to God. I did not know then, but sensed by that instinct which youth has for divinity, that a man becomes a God when, out of the trials, the struggles and the handicaps of his existence, he makes an art in life which transmutes them all into a triumphant harmony, a dynamic peace which welcomes strife. 96

"How are you, my boy?" He spoke to me with dignity and kindness. "You're tall enough and strong enough. Can you harness and hitch a horse all right? No need to ask you if you know how to drive, eh?"

An invigorating feeling of confidence and courage came over me. "Yes, sir, I can. I've harnessed and hitched all of 

Father's horses." As I heard myself say "Father" instead of "Papa" I felt exultantly that no greater proof of my newly acquired manhood could possibly have been given. The success of my life no longer depended on the mere task of getting the job I had come after. "Well, young man, you can start immediately. I'll show you the horse that we're going to use today and while I get a snack to eat you can be getting him ready."

As I descended the rear steps into the garden I reviewed the strange surroundings, through which I had passed but a few moments before with such trepidation and hope, as the familiar and friendly scenes of a victorious battlefield. 

The brown-and-white barn (through whose open doors I had viewed the formidable array of brand-new mowingmachines, brightly painted reapers and a terrifying brigade of other farm implements), the severe rows of apple trees and currant bushes, the threatening neatness of the vegetable garden, all appeared to me now as cohorts I had conquered. 

Mrs. Austin explained to me that her husband took care of everything outside of the house with all the resourcefulness of a king who was in full possession of his powers of vision. It seemed to me now that as his knight I had come into some of the greatness which a moment ago I had recognized in his presence. I was eager to show him that 

I was a worthy knight.

After he had pointed out to me the spirited chestnut stallion which we were going to use for the day, explain- 97

ing to me that his name was Old Reliable, I seized the harness which he indicated and began my labors with speed and enthusiasm. However, before I could get the harness on Old Reliable's back, the king, in whose service I had just become a knight, gently gave me my first rebuke. "Old Reliable would like a grooming before he has his harness put on. He's a pretty proud old horse, and he always feels better when his coat is clean and shiny."

I was deeply ashamed that I had forgotten a duty which suddenly appeared to me to be of the greatest importance. 

And though I could not then consciously appreciate the fineness of the spirit which insisted upon excellency in a detail which he could not see and hence apparently enjoy, I did feel the kindness of that spirit which was so great that it could even consider the pride of a horse. With those simple words the dignity and pride of the animal were so vividly imparted to me that I determined at once to take them into my own keeping and to respect them as carefully as Mr. Austin did.

I led the horse out from the stall and groomed him furiously until his coat glistened and his forelock bristled with appreciation. When, having harnessed him to Mr. 

Austin's well-kept buggy, I drove him out to the steppingstone, my whole being glowed with his contagious pride. 

I felt a great happiness over a deed well done. 

Mr. Austin appeared in the front doorway dressed in a duster and cap and carrying a cane which he called his "sounding stick." Unhesitatingly he walked straight to the stepping-stone, then around to the front of the horse, feeling of the traces, hold-back straps, bridle and reins, and running his sensitive hand over the stallion's legs and belly. "You'll do, my boy! You'll do!" In a moment he was back 98

to the carriage step again and climbing into the buggy beside me. "Just one thing this rein is twisted. I do like to have them straight." Strange things were happening to me, as the impressions of new experiences were flashing through my senses. For the first time in my life I did not resent a criticism. I became dimly conscious of the majesty of human dignity. 

This blind man inspired me with a new feeling which had nothing to do with his blindness.
