His outspoken manner, the absence of apology, the beautiful directness, the sincerity of his smile, gave to everything he did, everything he said, an air of such genuine authority as I had never before encountered. My admiration poured out of me. "I can't see how you do all these things! I can't imagine how you do it!" I closed my eyes for a moment to reassure myself of that lost sensation in a world of blackness. "Well, sonny, perhaps some day you'll understand. You know now, don't you, that if you prune an apple tree it gathers more strength in the rest of its branches. Well, the same thing applies to a human being. If you take away some of his faculties he has to develop others to make up for the loss. There are a lot of things in life which we creatures think we can't git along without, but when we haven't got them any more, we find that we can."

I was in a new world. For the first time things such as 

Mr. Austin was saying seemed to have a real meaning. I had never before heard words which were so convincing. (Fifteen years later the prophecy of them was fulfilled with an added significance a significance, however, that had never been intended. I kept saying to myself in those first hours of darkness, when my eyes would open and yet would not see, "Some day you'll understand . . . indeed, indeed, some day you'll understand!" I touched again the 99

greatness of Mr. Austin and arose from that magic contact with a mended faith and a new courage.)

We were trotting along Pierpont Avenue, getting out into the country on to the road to Brownville. I was thanking Providence for the kindness it had shown me in giving me such a wonderful experience. I was no longer working now. I was driving a magnificent horse for a great man named Mr. Austin. And Mr. Austin was telling me a story: "It was just out along this road, about four mile, on a crackin' good farm owned by Tom McDougal-one of the squarest Scotchmen in this here section-about twelve years ago, that I had the occasion to examine some of his stock. 

I got mighty interested in a big three-year old stallion—a

Kentucky thoroughbred and as spirited as they make 'em. 

He reared around considerable while Tom was putting the halter on him, and as I looked at the flash in his eye I said to myself, "There's a horse that I'd like to own.' We walked him out into the barnyard where I could see him move around a bit-and there, sonny, we made a mistake. We stood there talking over his good points when suddenly he gave a big leap, throwing his head high in the air and snatching the halter shank out of Tom's hand. I thought for a minute a bee had stung him, for he seemed to turn into a plunging demon. In a twinkle he was rushing straight for me, with mouth wide open, teeth bared, and those big eyes of his blazing like something possessed. He looked more like a fiery dragon you read about in your fairy books than a domestic animal. I was rooted to the ground. I couldn't move. Within a few feet of me he reared on his hind legs and striking out with his right front foot he caught me full in the face."

I turned to look at Mr. Austin and saw on his face neither fierceness nor melancholy, but a calm smile which, as I re- 100

called it so vividly many years later, seemed to express some secret triumph, some hidden joy. "And, sonny," he went on, "that is the last thing I shall ever see. He knocked me unconscious, started to paw me around on the ground, and if Tom's shepherd dog had not rushed up and grabbed him by the heel-that dog had always been a great friend of mine-he would have finished the job which he had started so well." 

Tears had been coming into my eyes and for a moment I had tried to stop them, but now I felt so deeply that such things as keeping back tears did not matter. “And did he kill the horse?" I blubbered out. "No, sonny. When I got up around again I went out there with a friend of mine and bought that beauty, because that is what I had wanted to do in the first place." 

I was sure now that he was going to tell me that he had taken revenge on him himself. My Christian training still permitted a great deal of room for revenge. Yet there was something in me that made me hesitate to ask him baldly what he had done to the horse-something in me that dimly sensed this man's incapacity for inflicting cruelty. But I was curious. "Where is he now?" "Why, sonny, he's right there in front of you." 

Though I heard precisely what Mr. Austin said, other words kept echoing through my mind: "He should have been cut to ribbons!!! He should have been cut to ribbons!!!" The reins tightened in my hands and I looked toward the whipstock. But of course there was no whip.

Then I began to wonder where those words kept coming from. I felt ashamed of them. They didn't belong to me. 

Where had I heard them? Ah, at last . . . years before on the farm in Morristown I had heard my father say them. A neighbor had been kicked by one of his horses. Father had

ΙΟΙ

said, when relating the incident, that the man should have taken the whip and "cut the horse to ribbons." "He should have taught him a lesson." (Father was always teaching lessons by means of the horse-whip. Not that he was cruelhe was really a kind man. But the "Virtues" in this sinful world had to be upheld and abetted by him; even at the cost of using Solomon's rod.) 

Mr. Austin, I realized, was not a "virtuous" man; that is, he had no "virtues" to uphold. There seemed to be nothing outside of his character-no principles, or ideals, or righteousness-which he felt it his duty to support. He was himself, first, last, and always; after my brother Mark, he was the first man I had thus far known who gave me a sense of the dignity that was possible to a man. When he said anything or did anything I never had to wonder how much of either was a lip service to righteousness and how much was genuinely sincere. It was all sincere!

As we traveled along over the winding, beautiful country roads Mr. Austin would direct me, now to this turn, then to that, past one farmhouse and in to another. Everywhere the farmer's greetings would be very cheery and respectful. Mr. Austin would examine the cattle and horses with uncanny cleverness, running his hands all over them, feeling of their teeth, talking to them like a doctor with a patient, and asking the farmer but one question: "What color is the beast?" Then he would state his price. If the farmer accepted, all right; if not, nothing more was said about it. He never bargained, and the men with whom he dealt had learned never to try to urge him. 

We would be out in the country for two or three weeks at a time, traveling over the greater part of St. Lawrence 

County, and never at any time did Mr. Austin lose his bearings. He asked me to tell him about everything which 102

struck me as being new in the countryside, especially new houses, or barns, or silos. After I had told him about some detail which I would immediately proceed to forget, I would be amazed several weeks later to pass the same spot and hear him repeat to me what I had told him. I was so proud to be "his eyes" that I presently got into the habit of telling about every little thing as we passed by; until he would reach over with his hand and squeeze my arm very kindly and say, "You'd better take a rest now for a while, or you'll be all tuckered out." He did not talk to me very much, but when he did speak his words were full of meaning. He had a way of teaching me things that made me more and more eager to have him continue.

In late summer, on our last trip, I began to feel that to leave Mr. Austin and go back to school would be one of the greatest tortures that I could ever endure. I had told Father that I would like to work for him forever and I had urged him to let me stay with him and not go back to school. 

Father's work throughout the summer had not been especially profitable and I had begun to feel that my five dollars a week would help out a great deal. (When I was with Mr. Austin I was always filled with generous impulses to help.) "That's the proper way to feel, Peter," Father would say, “and I'm glad you've got that spirit. But if you worked now, when would you get an education? And don't forget an education is the only way nowadays of getting anywhere in this world. You see what Mathew's has did for him."

But for a few weeks, at least, I saw that Mathew's education could not bring me half the pleasure and satisfaction that I derived from my association with Mr. Austin. In the end it was Mr. Austin who convinced me that I ought 103

to go back to school. I told him that I wanted to stay with him. He smiled first and then his face grew serious. "Well, young man, that wouldn't be right for yourself. And the most important thing for you is to be square to yourself. 

If you learn to be just with yourself, you'll be doing more for other creatures than could ever you do any other way. 

Become independent. Don't lean on other people. You see, if you kept on working for me you'd be depending upon me . you'd be leaning on me. You don't want to do that. This is my job. You don't want to become a cattle dealer. I know that. You told me you wanted to become an engineer. Maybe you do. But if you find out you don't, take up something else. Just be sure, whatever you do, that it's what you want to do, not anybody else. People may tell you that that's selfish, but you'll find out that it does a lot more good in this world than most of this unselfishness that they talk about." ...

When we had returned home and I was saying good-by, Mr. Austin tucked three ten-dollar bills into my pocket. "There, my boy, you've done a very good job. You're going to high school this fall, aren't you? Well, go and do as good a job there." He clasped my arm and looked at me with that curious, visionless expression which seemed somehow to penetrate deeper than any eyes possibly could. "Good-by, and come over to see me and Old Reliable once in a while." 

I walked out of the yard, happy and crying. I felt so good and yet so sad that as I walked along the street I didn't much care whether people noticed that I was crying. 104

CHAPTER IX 

WHEN it was decided that we were to move to the village of Morristown-following my father's visit there during which he had learned that the town was just coming into a great boom, that some wealthy people were going to build a big hotel and make a summer resort out of the place, and that the village trustees had voted to erect a Town Hall-my mother went about the house with an inspiring smile of relief on her face and a perennial eagerness to tell Ruth and Johnny and me how much better life would be there for all of us than it was in Potsdam. 

She had been born in Morristown, married there, and had lived there most of her life. She loved the little village of five hundred people, the old St. Lawrence River, the little 

Methodist church, the rustic ease of its society, as she had never learned to love Potsdam or its people. 

In Potsdam she would say: "Things are a little bit too fancy. There's so much education here that it makes a poor ignorant person like me feel uncomfortable. It's fine for the children, but I guess I'm a little too old-fashioned to git used to it. I enjoy more talkin' to people that ain't always so particular about their English. And . . . I don't know... but somehow the people in Morristown seem different. They may not be so stylish, but they're a plagued sight more friendly. And that ain't all," she would go on. "I'll tell you this much, that it don't cost half as much to live in Morristown as it does here." ..

It was not that Mother didn't like Potsdam. Indeed, she 105

liked it very well. But she was always eager to see in whatever change Father made a new hope, a new security, a new promise that the family was going to get along better than before: that they would stay together, that the children would go to school, that the simple joys of domestic life would continue uninterrupted. Once shortly after her marriage she had gone to an old fortune-teller who had told her that she would have a large family and that she would always be happy so long as they were together, even if they lived in nothing better than under a shingle in the corner of a fence. Mother had believed that prophecy religiously. 

Indeed, it was more than a belief. It was an instinct, so strong and obvious that when the fortune-teller had simply told what she had seen so plainly written on my mother's face, Mother—feeling no doubt that the depth of her instinct must certainly be unrecognized by any one except herself and those strange people possessed with occult powers-immediately decided that henceforth she could not help having "some faith, at least, in fortune-telling." So now that it was decided that the family was to move to Morristown she immediately set about preparing herself and her family circle for the greater happiness which she knew was going to exist there for all of us. 

I was eager at first and could think of nothing but the excitement of moving, and of those pictures of the St. 

Lawrence River which my imagination now so anxiously conscripted from my memory, enlarged upon and colored for the enhancement of my anticipated pleasures. But after the rooms of the old house in Potsdam were emptied of their furniture, I had a feeling of regret and melancholy. 

As I stood there looking for the last time out of the front door through those colored panes of glass, thinking very consciously that I was leaving, leaving, never to return 106

again, that house suddenly appeared to me as a castle in fairyland-from its earth-floored cellar, where Mother used to store her pickled pears, to the rough-timbered garret with its dark cobwebby recesses under the eaves. (And now, whenever in those moments of wistful reverie I try to construct the image of an Ideal Home, the perfect domicile of comfort and happiness, my fancy forever comes back to that house, bringing with it a nostalgia that is as inescapable as it is beautifully sentimental. No other kitchen could ever be like that kitchen with the cookstove roaring on a winter's day and a batch of bread and cookies pouring out their ineffable odors from the oven! No other parlor could ever be like that parlor where I had listened to an orchestra with all its fascinating instruments for the first time, and where in hours caressed by magic I had danced around so many Christmas trees with their bright candles sparkling into the dim corners where Santa Claus might possibly be lurking, the frost-covered window-panes glistening with Christmas cheer! . . . Ah, those Christmases! . . . Can it be perhaps that all of us with our wistfulness and yearning, our melancholy futility, are thus because we are forever searching, searching in vain, to recapture the felicity, the charm, the unadulterated merriness of those golden anniversaries of our youth? ... We can't begin to count again the host of things we seek in that enchanted house of our youth. We do know that ever since we have grown to the hard realities of manhood we have always had the hungering desire to go back there some day, buy the house, and live in it for the rest of our lives. What we seek most there we do not know—perhaps it is the impossible fairyland of our youth; perhaps, after all, we would revert to infantilism.) ... 107

Doubtless some one has said before that rivers get into one's blood. Happy is the man who has experienced it. 

Supremely fortunate is he if the river happens to be the St. Lawrence; for with the majesty of that great river incorporated into his being a man may face life with a magnificent courage, a splendid dignity and a vision of rich and rugged beauty. It will forever be for him a mighty pattern, an inspiring panorama of a Promethean struggle, fortitudinous and serene. People speak of the sacred Ganges; but the St. Lawrence never aspires to mysticism or sanctity. 

It is ever grandly human—the Titan always, in preference to the Olympian. If there are those who prefer the futility of the sea—that huge amorphous metaphor of human existence, which strains and struggles only to remind us of the essential and tragic purposelessness of life, which holds so mercilessly before our eyes that constant symbol of the infinity of death-there are others who see in the stalwart, steady flow of a river the invigorating symbol of an art in life which transcends futility. 

But just as there are people who live in the shadow of the Louvre, the Metropolitan or the Cathedral of Chartres and pass by each day without the slightest change in their blood-pressure; human beings who turn the dials of their radio from a Bach suite to the more stimulating rhythms of Coon-Sanders' jazz; solemn mortals who read the Psalms of David and then mouth them in church; so I, arriving in Morristown, looked upon the St. Lawerence and saw in it a grand place to go swimming, a swell place to fish, an ideal spot for skating in the winter, and a perfect river on which to go boating.

The masterpieces of art and the majesties of Nature are 108

indifferent to our appreciation. Day after day the St. Lawrence unfolded before my obtuse gaze the glories of its elements. In the morning it was a limpid mirror, reflecting the bottomless sky whose clouds vainly attempted to conceal a blue infinity. In the afternoon, when a fierce breeze from Canada swept across it, it would suddenly become a lashing mass of white-capped exuberance. In winter it would change its dress first to a glistening shimmer of frozen silver, then to a brilliant garment of drifted snowcrystals. And though the preoccupations of my adolescent pleasures were like a tough skin which defied the penetration of any sensation or idea beyond such half-realizations as, "Isn't the river pretty tonight in the moonlight?" or, "Gosh, doesn't it look great in a storm!" year after year that river kept saying: "Look at me now, for I shall never be the same again-tomorrow I shall be different, and day after tomorrow also, and on all the days from now to eternity I shall make a different pattern from any I have made before! Look at me now and see immutable mutability. . . and remember, Peter, that I keep going . . . it may all be purposeless, but, nevertheless, isn't it beautiful! ... 99 Day after day the St. Lawrence was gradually penetrating my obtuse gaze, until many years later, when through the wards of City Hospital I would hear the rattle of that iron ambulance as it carried the dead off to the morgue, I saw it flowing: eternally, lyrically, beautifully flowing, in death and in life its courage bestowing. 3 From my window, in the little brown house in Morristown into which we had moved, I could look out across the 109

St. Lawrence to the beautiful and foreign shores of Canada. 

I remember that as I would stand there in the morning, clothed only in my underwear, not enough awake yet to be aware or interested in what the day might bring to justify my proceeding any further in the process of dressing, the varying shades of green and brown in the pattern of Canadian fields would hold me fascinated-pastures, woods and lawns, the whole panorama of the land appearing to be tipped up from the shore-line like a huge map lifted by one edge at an angle from its horizontal flatness. 

The room itself in which I stood was something toward which I unconsciously turned my back with relief. As I kept complaining to my mother after the glamour of its novelty wore off, it was an awful come-down from Potsdam. And as I stood there gazing out its window it seemed as though I must find in the strangeness of those foreign fields a strangeness which my imagination, responding to the demands of my preconceived notions, insisted upon giving them-some beauty, some pleasure, some wonder which would absorb me with its novelty and uniqueness, to compensate for the lost joys of a more elegant existence in Potsdam. And during those few moments of vagueness which marked the transition from sleep to the bright concern of thorough wakefulness, the fascination of gazing at those foreign fields would submerge my half-conscious complaints of the inadequacies of Morristown.

I was always, during those moments of the morning which preceded my arising from bed and standing before the window, irritatedly reminded that our present house had no bathroom or toilet, and that irritation would cause me to despise all the more those nocturnal concessions to the absence of water-works in Morristown. A custom which in the beginning seems to us quaint and not without charm 110

grows despicable and annoying once we are forced to adopt it and expose ourselves willy-nilly to its shortcomings. Moreover, the fact that there was no furnace in the house and that my room, which I no longer held in private, but had to share with my younger brother Johnny, was heated by the solitary, ugly black stovepipe from the kitchen stove, made me unhappy.

I, who had wanted to live in a house like the Sessions', must now struggle for the greatness I aspired to in a lowly, countrified place like this. It was deadly discouraging. What could I achieve in a house so uncouth, in a town which had no opera house; whose sidewalks were clumsy affairs of wood instead of concrete; whose Methodist Sunday School had no orchestra; and whose only schoolhouse was a rustic frame building instead of a classic monument of sandstone and granite?

Yet as I would stand there by the window, immersed in that view across the St. Lawrence, I would momentarily be lost in a different and happy world. The elation I felt would stimulate me to imagine enjoyable things which the day might bring; and suddenly I would take an interest in dressing. I would throw on my clothes, rush downstairs, grab a cookie from the breakfast-table and dash out of the house down to the river.

Along the shore of the river I was happy. If the morning was calm I could walk out on the boat-works dock and peek down into the clear water, always with the expectation of seeing a big pickerel or an enormous black bass. 

If the river happened to be rough I would watch, fascinated, the waves pounding the rocky shore or hurling strange pieces of driftwood onto the gravel beaches in the little "harbors" which I had named after the principal seaports of the world. Sometimes I would hear the seven o'clock 

III

whistle of the sawmill, and would then have to rush back to the house to eat my breakfast and get ready for school. 

From the river to the school was like a journey from sunshine and happiness to a world of dark discomfort. The school in Morristown, instead of being founded upon aspirations for enlightenment, or a yearning for knowledge which would enlarge the spirit and set the soul free, seemed to be a reluctant concession of the village trustees to the newfangled nonsense of "book-l'arnin'." Its sessions were opened with long prayers and readings from the Bible. Its teachers were selected on the strength of their adherence to one of the three dominating evangelical faiths. Its consuming ambitions apparently were to use the courses of its curriculum as instruments for teaching the students a stern discipline, a rigid respect for tradition, a harsh adherence to duty. The boys knew scarcely any games and the girls, with but a few exceptions, dressed in old-fashioned clothes and wore their hair in pigtails.

High school was a miserable disappointment. In Potsdam 

I had had such happy anticipations of the grand life I would have when I at last graduated from grammar school and passed into the superior atmosphere of high school. 

There, such a step marked very noticeably the accession to a proud dignity, an enviable elegance. But in Morristown there seemed to be not the slightest distinction attached to such a momentous change. There was no football team on which one could become a hero, no glee club where one could have a jolly time. Most of the boys played baseball very poorly, and it was impossible to organize them into a team. They wanted to play three-o-cat, and to bat all the time. 

If they couldn't bat very often, they lost interest and either quit or played indifferently. For the most part they were crude and stupid and seemed utterly unaware of such a 112

thing as real companionship. It seemed as though I would never again have a good time in school. I was convinced that even the algebra and the Latin-which were new subjects to me, painfully dull and inscrutable-would be entirely different if I were only learning them in that wonderful high school back in Potsdam. Often when we get away from a thing, a person or a city which annoys us, and see it from a distant and different perspective it takes on a charm which we had never dreamed of. Morristown possessed the virtue of being able to be seen from a distant perspective. When I, having taken a ferry across the St. Lawrence to Brockville, would stand on the Canadian shore and gaze back at the village from which I had just come, that tiresome and too-familiar world in which day after day I had so many problems and experienced so many disappointments would suddenly appear so lovely, so endearing, so strangely fascinating, that I immediately would become impatient to get back to it and explore those new and indefinable delights which the glamour of distance now revealed. 

Morristown would then appear to me as a beautiful village of terraces. I could see plainly on the first terrace along the shore the railroad depot, the charming pill-factory, the motorboat works and the sawmill. On the next terrace the business street-with its hodge-podge of storefronts, blocks, the Free Library, the Frontier House, and the stone domicile of the weekly newspaper-lost its ugliness, and into the foreground of industry and the background of softly outlined trees and the varicolored dwellings of another terrace above it, it blended like the rugged daubs in a virile 113

painting, transforming themselves, when seen at a distance, into magic and graceful chiaroscuros of highlights and shadows. From still another terrace the old stone windmill seemed to survey the village at its feet like a hoary, paternal sentinel, observing, without interfering, the antics of its charges.

Then in my imagination I could see myself traversing the familiar yet now strangely foreign streets of this distant town. I was taking flying dives from the very top of the old windmill down past all the terraces, over all the house roofs into the aquamarine waters of the river. I was standing, giant-like, behind the green schoolhouse, giving it a shove with my foot and watching it roll, heels-over-head, cellar-over-chimney, down the steep terraces into the river. 

Indeed, from that perspective-and with the aid of my imagination-I was doing so many of those things which I had long wanted to do in Morristown but could not in the face of the insuperable obstacles which then confronted me, that I at once became happy over the thought that I lived there and that I would soon be going back to it to explore it anew.

Once I was back, the familiar scenes assailed me, and, strive as I would, I could no longer recall those "indefinable delights" which I had so distinctly anticipated but a few hours before.

When I came in view of our house, which was on the second terrace from the river, I tried in vain to place it securely in the vision I had seen from across the river. The locust tree, which (in itself, very beautiful) stood in the front yard, once again stuck out like a rugged daub of glory, refusing to blend with the bricks of the house chimney, the iron pump at the well, or the drab little house in 114

the rear of the yard which, when speaking politely, I called "our bathroom."

From my visits to Canada I would usually return just in time for supper. And so it was that once I entered the kitchen all thoughts of the Morristown I had seen from across the river completely vanished from my mind. I was hungry! And the sizzle of fried pork, the aroma of newly baked bread, the sight of steaming boiled potatoes, would assault my senses and the satisfaction of my appetite would practically dominate my whole being until supper was nearly over.

One evening shortly after our arrival in Morristown, when supper was over Mark and I went down to the river to see if we could find a boat with which we could go rowing. Coming upon a cluster of private boat-houses, we stopped for a moment to watch an old man as he glided up to the community dock in a trim little skiff. As the man in landing looked up and saw us, he held aloft a string of several good-sized fish. "Ain't they nice ones!" he exclaimed with a tone of familiarity before we had time to say a word of appreciation. "You bet! . . . They're swell!"

Mark and I were unaccustomed to having any one whom we did not know speak to us, and this display of neighborliness was as unexpected as it was welcome. For I imagine that we both decided that luck had come into our hands and that here was a friendliness which we could advantageously use to help us in our task of finding a boat for our purpose. But if we were so sure that the man did not know us we were certainly mistaken. "Say, ain't you two boys the sons o' Amos Holland?"

This was good luck twice over. If this man was so old and kindly looking-yet not too old to enjoy fishing-if 115

he knew Father, he might even let us take his own boat. "Yes, we are." "Well, now I was right, after all. I saw you two fellers movin' in. I live in the house across the road. My name's 

Captain Wells. . . . As I say, I saw you movin' in and I says to my daughter, 'There's a couple o' Amos Holland's boys or I'll be capsized and foundered!' And she says, 'No such thing.' But I reckon I know chips off the old block when I see 'em."

By this time we were standing on the dock dividing our eager attention between Captain Wells' words, his alluring skiff, and the fine string of fish. "I'm right glad your folks have come back to Morristown. I knew your father well when he was here before ... a mighty good man, ambitious, honest... and, say, how he used to be able to play the bass-horn! . . . Rather fond of the girls too he could of had most any o' them around here, I guess. They seemed kind-a partial to him 'cause he was a teetotaler." 

The old captain looked at us with a sheepish grin. “He's give me hell plenty for gettin' drunk. He wa'n't one of those sissies, neither. I seen him fight like a damned Injun once when he got mad." • • Now seemed the propitious time to ask our question. "Do you know, sir," said Mark, "where my brother and 

I can get a boat to go rowing in?" "You're damn' right I do. You can have this one. I'm through with it for the day, and you can have it, fishing tackle 'n all, free o' charge. Do you fish?" Of course we knew all about fishing, but we listened with a grateful show of respect while Captain Wells explained to us how to troll and advised us upon the particular spoon to use. 

Three-quarters of an hour later I caught my first pickerel.

The excitement of that moment convinced me that despite 116

everything else I was going to like Morristown. If I couldn't play games at school, and there were no boys who wanted to make a companion of me, I would find my fun at the river, fishing, swimming, rowing and exploring. 

But in a few days I was more alone than ever: Mark left home to go to business college. It was no longer fun to go fishing; and to go in swimming without any companions was no more interesting than taking a bath. Meanwhile Ruth, with her warm and genial disposition, had made close friends of the girls across the road. They were together a great deal, talking gaily of dresses and parties, fellows and school. For a while I envied and avoided their jolly company, trying again and in vain to get some of the fellows at school interested in football, and feeling in the egotism of my youth that I must somehow find a way of showing the superiority which belonged to me for having come from the much larger and finer town of Potsdam. 

But when I would show the boys what I knew to be the best way to kick a football and try to explain to them the very rudiments of formations and team play, they would listen impatiently for a moment and then grab the ball and boot it around in the most unorganized fashion. If they were going to learn football—or indeed if they were going to learn any of the "interesting things which we used to do in Potsdam❞—they were determined at least that they were not going to learn them from me. I was discouraged and resentful. In my resentment I remembered an expression which I had heard Doria Sessions' Aunt Jane (who was so fashionable and superior to all these clumsy people in Morristown) make on occasions which seemed to me now very similar to the one I was going through: "This is a bore! . . . such a bore." And so I-not knowing quite what it meant, but dimly feeling that it expressed very wonderfully the 117

attitude which I hoped the boys and girls of Morristown would realize that I had toward them-went about saying: "This school is such a bore!" or, "This town is such a bore!" or, "This life is such a bore!"

For weeks the expression was my talisman. One afternoon, being kept after school by Miss Darrel to write a theme for English (an assignment which was already several days overdue), I said to her, because she was my favorite teacher, very good-looking, and the one person of all whom I most wanted to impress, "This school is such a bore!"

Miss Darrel looked at me with a strange expression, her hazel eyes sparkling with a curiously affectionate radiation of amusement, sympathy, understanding, and impishness. "That's all very well, young man. But do you actually know what you're saying?"

I did not know why then, but the mantle of pedagogy fell from her shoulders. She became a woman, beautiful, twenty, maternal, and understanding. I suddenly realized, not from what she said, but from the way she said it, that she was accepting me for a moment into a relationship of friendly understanding. "I certainly do!" I replied with an increased air of importance and magniloquent bravado. "This whole town is a terrible bore!" I walked back to my seat, swaggering with an arrogance I did not feel; and trembling with the hope that here at last in my English teacher I might find some one to truly appreciate me. "Well, Peter, perhaps it's not the town's fault. Did you ever stop to think that it might be yours? You know, the other students in high school don't find it so dull. A great many of them have a much harder time of it than you do." 

She was scolding me just like every one else. 118

My hope was rapidly vanishing. I got out some paper and pretended to write my theme in an effort to cover up my sense of injury. Alas, no one was ever going to understand me. "Potsdam is a very nice town, isn't it?" 

What Miss Darrel was saying was startling enough, but the tone of her voice was even more so. Could it really be that she was trying to tease me-my English teacher who was so severe, and beautiful, and dignified in front of her classes? My heart pounded foolishly. I did not know why, but I felt that if she liked me well enough to take the trouble to tease me, that that was more wonderful than an obvious display of kindness. Still I had to show her that 

I couldn't be teased-that I was too old for that. “Yes,” I said. (I was talking now with a calm dignity.) "I liked it very much." Not a word more would I say. "So did I. I lived there for a year, you know, when I went to the Normal. It was very nice; but I think Morristown's nice, too." 66 .. You lived there??!" "Why, yes. Didn't you know it? I knew some of the girls who stayed at your house." Her eyes twinkled merrily as she added: "And they told me you were quite a nice boy, too. . . . I had hopes that you were going to be one of my best scholars." 

I suddenly felt sick and miserable. Oh, the bitterness of regret . . . the pain of an opportunity lost! If I had only known that Miss Darrel had lived in Potsdam, I would have shown her before what a wonderful scholar I could be. I would have acted more like a gentleman. But now I was disgraced. It was hopeless to think now of ever being comfortable in Morristown. To all of those unsuspecting people who had listened, so impressed, to my stories of 119

Potsdam Miss Darrel had now simply to say a few words to brand me as a terrible liar or to expose me as a wicked impostor. I felt so ashamed that I could no longer look at her. I bent over the paper on my desk and under the title of my theme, "The Runaway," I wrote words and words which meant precisely nothing. "I hope," Miss Darrel was talking again, “I mean I really believe that you can be the best scholar in my Freshman class if you'll just get down to work. Supposing you start right now and see what a nice theme you can write for me." 

Holy Moses! She wasn't going to be a traitor to me, after all! She wasn't going to tell! She was still going to give me a chance. I tore up the sheet which I had started, and for five whole minutes I went through the terrific labor of thinking-struggling, concentrating-with my head in my hands. I was desperately trying to conjure up a story which would stamp me at once as a genius.

At the end of my deep thought I wrote again at the top of my clean sheet of paper: "The Runaway." But this story was to be different. In it I was to make myself the hero which I had always wanted to be the rescuer of Doria Sessions and her mother from the terrible fate of death and mutilation at the hands (or rather, the heels) of a runaway horse. . . . It was a Sunday afternoon, and as Fate would have it I was dressed in my best clothes, shoes brilliantly polished. The horse was wildly, madly galloping up Napoleon Avenue (Elm Street of Potsdam) and I, seeing it coming in the nick of time, dashed out into the road, gave a lunge for its head, seized the bridle, and, after being dragged along for nearly a quarter of a mile, finally stopped it. ... Standing there by the horse's head, ragged, bruised and bloody, I coolly surveyed the occupants of the carriage 120

and expressed my hope that they had not been injured . . . etc., etc. . . . until I at length drove them home safely, modestly receiving their tearful thanks.

When I had finished the story-in which I hoped Miss Darrel would recognize some obscure evidence of a latent genius-I walked very deliberately up to her desk, placed it before her and started to leave the room. Tomorrow, when she would have read it, she would call me before her, praise my talent, and turning to the rest of the class she would read it aloud, commenting upon it when she had finished, as "one of the finest examples of Freshman writing which I have ever had the privilege to encounter." 

But when I came into the class the next day, after a night of sleeplessness and dreaming upon my imminent recognition, she said, "Good morning," and not another word. My heart had been beating so violently that I could hardly speak. Now it seemed to stop. Oh, the agony of that moment! I had to have some relief. So I kept murmuring to myself: "Perhaps she hasn't read it . . . of course she hasn't read it!" But still I couldn't wholly soothe my discouragement. "Suppose she has read it?" ... "Well, then maybe she's going to wait until after class to speak to me about it!"

At last, in the vicissitudes of class recitation I forgot the grief of my uncertainty—just as all of us dismiss in a succeeding moment the “unforgettable" wretchedness of our present and "incorrigible" doom. I floated along on a stream of revery-recalling the familiar, kindly tone, the friendly smile, the sympathetic gestures of Miss Darrel of the afternoon before, and seeing in everything she now did in class, every question she asked, every movement, every comment she made, a special meaning whose significance was meant 121

for me alone. The refuge of my bliss was threatened only when for a moment I would wonder if the smile which she was giving to some other boy in the class was half as sincere as those which she gave to me. 

At the close of class I had forgotten that I had hoped but a short while before that she would detain me in order to tell me how greatly she had been impressed by my story. 

And it was not until I was on my way home after school (thinking self-pityingly of the added chores which I now had to do since Mark had left for business college, and deciding that of the two evils-doing chores and staying after school-the latter was by far the less objectionable) the feeble ray of that unfulfilled hope returned to my consciousness and made me miserable. At least she could have corrected it and handed it back. That was the least she could have done. That's what she's there for, isn't it . . . keep me after school to write it . . . and then not take the trouble to show me my mistakes. . . . If she wanted to keep me after school to write it, I can't see why she shouldn't keep me after school to correct it. The unfairness of older people was appalling. Not only did they fail—with the exception of Mark—to understand you; they always seemed to give you no consideration whatsoever. What was 

I going home to? To do chores: groom horses, clean out the stable, cut wood, grease the wagon. It would be almost supper time before I got through, and after supper I'd have to study. What did they take me for, anyway-a mule? 

Didn't they ever expect me to have any fun?

The worst of my misery was not expressed. It came from the deep and uncomfortable suspicion that most of my unhappiness might-as Miss Darrel had said-be my own fault. And such an idea was too terrible to express. 122

The contract for the new hotel had at last been let. Father was to start work at once. The jubilation of the household had rarely ever been so great. If Father stopped cracking jokes to become serious for a moment, and following his cue we hushed our hilarity, he would soon reveal to us that his very seriousness was facetious, and would scold our stupidity with such a "delighted" smile on his face. (following the example of his then-reigning idol, Teddy 

Roosevelt) that my insides would tremble with pleasure.

Yet the relief, the joy, the excitement of the news-magnificent as it was-was no more wonderful to me than the pleasure I felt at these periodic displays of warm humanity in my father. For ever since he had returned from California there had been growing in me a feeling of sadness (whose real nature I now recognized to have been pity), a half-conscious, melancholy worry concerning Father's happiness and welfare. Now that he chastised me no more -perhaps due to my precocious size and to the circumstance that my younger brother, Johnny, occupied most of his attention in that department-the very direct resentment which I had felt toward him on that account had been gradually vanishing. I could not help feeling sorry over the fact that all that he had set out to do in the field of my moral and religious welfare by such strenuous applications of "beech-oil" had actually ended in a miserable failure. I saw myself as a worse case than ever before. It seemed to me that the older I grew, the more sins I learned to indulge in. And knowing that it had been Father's aim to prevent just that, I could not avoid criticizing his blundering attempt. Once having criticized, I could not help feeling sorry for him. 123

The intrinsic qualities of an idol apparently depreciate when the virtues with which we have originally adorned it begin one by one to vanish from our estimate. So the whole stature of my father had sunk lower and lower in the categories of my esteem, and the spectacle of that decline revealed to me the first glimmerings of tragedy. Thus in my adolescence, though I did not know why, any pleasure which my father could salvage from life to compensate him for the failure of his ambitions (the collapse of his austere eminence) would give me more joy than his orthodox successes. It was as though his decline from the loftiness of deity had provoked in me a hope that he would now support the Devil. 

I was glad when my father asked me after supper if I had greased the wagon to be able to tell him that I had. I felt a great satisfaction in having been able to do that much to help him. He must have seen that satisfaction in my face because he in turn seemed to be more pleased than he normally was over the mere performance of one of my regular chores. "If you keep on like this I guess I'll have to hire you to work for me. I could use you Saturdays and afternoons during the week . . . doing some teaming . . . and you could earn a little change." He looked so pleased over the prospect of my working for him: as though he had at last found a son (if only thirteen years old) who might take an interest in his business even though Mathew and Mark, turning to other fields, had gone away from home. While I, happy and eager over the possibilities of this new responsibility, could scarcely sleep from thinking about it.

For days I came home from school early, took care of the horses most assiduously and waited for my chance to go to work. On Saturday it arrived. All day long I drew 124

dirt from the excavation for the hotel up the steep hill to the next terrace above the one where the new building was to be erected. At first it was very exciting, but in the early part of the afternoon I began to feel sorry for the horses; they seemed to have to work so hard, pulling those heavy loads up the hill, that just looking at them made me feel weary. I would get off the load and push. That made me really tired. Presently it seemed as though the afternoon would never end. I knew that everybody worked until six o'clock and I began to wonder how any one ever stood it-all day long, day after day, shoveling dirt, using a pickaxe, carrying stone, hauling brick, piling lumber, mixing mortar. Why was life so cruel that people had to do that? 

Holy Moses! It was awful! 

At five o'clock Father saw me driving the horse wearily down the hill to get another load. I was slumped down on the wagon seat and the reins hung listlessly in my hands.

It seemed as though I had been working, working, driving horses, drawing dirt, for at least a solid week. When I saw anybody walking along the street or standing around watching the progress of the work I thought: "What lucky people they are; they're not working! No doubt about it, the greatest pleasure in life is not to have to work." But then as I noticed my father looking at me I tried to sit up and to look brave. He grinned, but above that grin his eyes were full of sympathy. "Well, son, I guess you'd better take the team home now . . . they've had enough for today, and I guess you have yourself. Haven't you? Besides, by the time you get them unhitched and taken care of it'll be pretty close to six o'clock anyway. Run along now, and don't forget to let 'em cool off before you give 'em much water." ... ... 

I had intended to go in swimming that evening after 125

work, but when I finished taking care of the horses I went into the kitchen and sat down on a chair. Mother brought me a slice of freshly baked bread which was still so warm that it melted the butter. I sat there leaning my chair back against the wall, eating my bread and butter, feeling a sense of ease, of restfulness, of satisfaction such as I had never felt before. There was so much happiness in just sitting down, resting; and being contented not to do anything else -not even to wonder, or think, or wish for a different state from the one I was in. Peace. The peace which comes after hard work, the peace which follows strain and struggle. 

When I went to school on Monday I felt perfectly willing to hurry home that afternoon, hitch up the team and work on the job until five o'clock. But in English class Miss 

Darrel asked me if I wouldn't stop a moment after school to go over my theme with her. "At last," I murmured to myself, "she has read it; and now she's going to tell me how wonderful she thinks it is."

The prospect of working that afternoon immediately lost a great deal of its attractiveness. Even when I would stick my hand into my pocket to feel the dollar bill which Father had paid me on Saturday night, I still felt more eager to stay after school. 

Miss Darrel looked particularly beautiful that afternoon.

She wore a shirtwaist of silk which in itself (a very rare novelty in Morristown in those days) was unbelievably attractive. Her soft brown hair seemed to be smoother and softer and wispier than ever. A new perfume made her seem like a person too precious to touch; it set her so apart from every one else in the whole school. When I came into her room I was so excited that I could feel my heart keep bumping against my ribs. That she smiled and asked me to 126

take a chair beside her while she explained the criticisms she had made on my theme did not make me any calmer. 

I sat on the farthest edge of the chair and trembled with self-consciousness, not so much now because I thought she was going to praise me as because of the fact that I was so near her. It was not until she picked up my theme and her manner suddenly changed from warm friendliness to the impersonal air of the classroom teacher that I felt more at ease, though disappointed. 

Under the fire of criticism the story which I had written gradually appeared very childish and dull. Presently I was almost ashamed that I had ever handed it in; and as the hopes of my ever becoming a good English scholar were rapidly diminishing I sat staring at Miss Darrel's desk in self-pitying dejection. "It's no use," I sighed. "I'll never learn to write English! I'm absolutely hopeless!" "Why, Peter, of course you're not hopeless. You've done very well. This is a very good story. The only thing is: you've got to learn to punctuate, to use a period once in a while, and to start a new paragraph when you start a new idea or change your point of view."

Miss Darrel's voice had become personal again and her manner sympathetic. In the midst of her comments she had placed her arm around my shoulders. She turned her face toward me, and tried to get me to look at her. A lump came in my throat and if I had tried to speak I would have burst into tears. Yet I felt so happy that I wanted that moment to continue forever.

In a daze I kept looking at a page of my story which lay on the desk before me, and from the conglomeration of words scrawled in my clumsy hand the name Dorothy stood out like a phrase written in raised letters. And suddenly-though my eyes remained open-there appeared be- 127

tween the sensitive curtain of my retina and the sheet of paper before me a vision of Doria: before the white colonial doorway of her house in Potsdam she was standing, looking like a soft yellow rose momentarily and meditatively held in front of a miniature Dorian façade of white marble. 

And in a flash the vision changed and against the background of the lacy, green elm trees of the Normal School yard she stood once more in her organdie dress with her hair tumbling down her back like waves of glistening cornsilk caught by a golden barrette. I closed my eyes for a second, and in that second time leaped from summer to autumn and along the orchard path behind our old house in Potsdam Doria was hurrying in the pursuit of a kitten -there in the midst of the rich russets of a frosty October her hair was flying in the wind like spun molasses taffy. 

When I opened my eyes that hair still appeared before them, but the hair now was definitely brown, and suddenly I realized that Miss Darrel's cheek was touching my lips. My heart went thumping and my knees trembled, but despite everything else my instinct triumphed; I pursed my lips and gently pressed them against her cheek. Miss Darrel's arm slid from my shoulders to around my neck. In another instant her lips were brushing mine-gently at first and then very madly. Then in a flash I felt a stab of pain. She withdrew her arm and suddenly stood up. As I ran my tongue over my lips I felt a wound and tasted blood. Miss Darrel was hurrying toward the door. I sat staring at the paper on the desk. Abruptly she turned and walked slowly back to where I was sitting. "I'm sorry!" she gasped. "You'd better go home now!" As I got up to go she grasped my arm hard and said: "Remember, Peter, I have a great deal of faith in you!"

When I had left the room and closed the door I walked 128

over to my seat in the main study hall and sat there for several minutes in a glorious daze. I felt myself to be the most important person in the whole world. Absentmindedly I took a few books from the desk and got up to go home, but instead of going toward the corridor door I walked across the room to Miss Darrel's. I wanted to go back to her! I wanted the feeling which now enveloped me never to end. Just as I reached her door it opened and she came out. I looked at her eagerly and tried to catch her eye, but she hurried past without even giving me a glance. 

My world of joy vanished from the universe.

At home that evening I could not speak. The single thought which came into my mind I felt instinctively I should not utter. Yet how I wanted to talk to somebodysomebody who would understand! If Mark were only at home! I could talk to him. Whatever he heard in confidence he never repeated, even in secrecy. But now there was no one to whom I could talk.

There was one thing, however, for which I could be thankful. My mother was sure that she understood my silence. I had got home in time to hitch up the team and draw one load of cement, and I had come back from work covered with its fine gray dust. My mother very strangely attributed my glumness to overwork. "Papa," she said, “I don't think it's a good thing at all for him to be liftin' those heavy bags of cement. No wonder he's silent: he's worn out. That's a job for a man. And just 'cause he's big for his age is no reason why he should be doin' a man's work."

Mother was not usually indulgent or oversolicitous in the matter of my work or physical welfare. Oftentimes she would say: "There's two things I'm sure I'll never have to about: and that is that Peter will never kill himself worry 129

working or get sick from worrying about it. Land sakes alive! I never saw nobody slower to move or who took things easier." 

The truth was that she knew I was lazy. Thus it seemed very extraordinary that she should try to excuse me. (I didn't realize then how well she could read me: how clearly she understood that it was not overwork which kept me silent, but something deeper which she did her best to protect.) But the whole afternoon had been so extraordinary that this unusual gesture of Mother's seemed wholly in keeping. Indeed, the only thing about it that bothered me at the moment was that though it quieted the curiosity concerning my silence it made more difficult the problem of how to get out of the house after supper with some other excuse than going for a walk or bedding the horses. If I were so very tired I obviously should not want to do either. Yet nothing seemed more important that evening than to stroll by the house where Miss Darrel lived, to get a glimpse of her.

In the end, after an hour's pretense of studying, I went out the back door with the pretext of going to the "bathroom." It was nearly ten o'clock before I returned home. 

Mother looked disappointed and Father scolded; but after what had happened that afternoon I knew that such things would no longer be a punishment. My life had become involved in the more serious matters of the world. 130

CHAPTER X 

For days during the early part of the winter the river would be steaming; and then one morning I would awake and going to the window-as was my unvarying habit-to look across to the shores of Canada, now white with their blanket of snow, I would discover that the old St. Lawrence was scaled with ice almost out to the middle. It never seemed to be less than magic that such a large body of water-over a mile and a half wide and so very, very deep-could change overnight from a mass of moving waves to a glassy sheet of immobile ice.

Even now, despite the frequency with which I have seen the gigantic changes of Nature take place, and despite familiarity with the laws which they inevitably follow, there is nothing which strains my credulity more than the faith which I have that they will happen again. In the winter, when the snows are deep, the trees are barren and the rivers mute; how hard it is to imagine that within a few months that frosty whiteness will have changed to a warm green, that those barren twigs will be covered with leaves, that that icy solidity will be a flowing stream. I find it no more difficult to believe that yesterday we were fish and that tomorrow we'll be supermen. 

Winter sports on the river thrilled me, but not enough.

Besides those hours of skating and coasting there were others painfully empty. Hours dulled with unfulfilled desirelonging for companionship and the excitement of social activity. Those precious moments when some startling, wonderful change of Nature would capture my eye and 131

envelope me in a misty delight were all too few. They did not help me much in my struggles with algebra; nor did they wholly soothe the sadness caused by Miss Darrel's neglect; and though they would calm my passions for a while those passions would return to rule me, more powerful, more stimulated than before-driving me to paroxysms of forbidden pleasure and leaving me at last in perspiring moods of miserable remorse. My body was growing and my appetites grew with it. I hungered for excitement, for daring, for heroism. Working for my father had grown dull and irksome—a monotonous round of hauling lumber and hardware from the warehouse to the new building. At school I was behind in all my classes except English. I would sit in the study hall staring at the girls-waiting, hoping for those moments when they would cross their legs and unwittingly reveal those habitually concealed parts of their mysterious beings.

Day after day the pathos of Time gave more and more charm to the memories of Potsdam. 

At Christmas time a card came from Doria. In answer I wrote her a "long" letter of adoration and woe, filling two sheets of letter-paper-carefully written in ink-with elaborate complaints against the shortcomings of Morristown and ardent protestations of love and loyalty. For weeks I waited for an answer. None ever came. And just as all of us, suffering from neglect, seek to attract in some way the attention we desire, I sought out the most dangerous spots on the river-places where the ice was notoriously treacherousand called public attention to my bravery by skating across them. Soon I gained the reputation at school of accepting any dare. One day I got up from my seat and strode noisily out of chapel in the midst of prayer. When I returned to school Principal Benson sent for me immediately. Trem- 132

blingly, fearfully, I walked toward his office. Just as I was about to go in the door Art Barbour-one of the seniors who had dared me to do the flagrant act-brushed by me and whispered: "If he starts to give you hell, tell him you had to go to the toilet." Loudly I answered: "What's the difference?

I'll tell him the truth!" With fresh defiance I walked into the principal's office. 

Principal Benson taught a class in the Methodist Sunday

School and took an active and conspicuous part in all the didactic activities of the village which were respectable. 

Nothing made him happier than an opportunity to display his education (which in reality was a minute parcel of knowledge wrapped in swathes of misinformation, chauvinism, braggadocio, and solemn bombast). With the girls at school-especially if they were pretty-he was jolly, pragmatic, facetious and pompous. The girls liked him because he was susceptible to their teasing and cajolery; but not very many of them respected him. He was too unreliable. 

If he happened to be nice one day, the very next he might turn on one with sarcasm, animosity, and fierce punishment.

With few exceptions he was severe and tyrannical toward the boys and took particular delight in embarrassing them individually before other people, a pleasure which he mainly indulged by making very personal criticisms of them in front of the girls. If it so happened that he came to school with his nails freshly manicured he would make it a point. to snoop around looking for untidy hands and nails. Spying them he would swoop down spectacularly upon the poor individual who owned them and with a nasty "Ahem!" exclaim: "Mr. Barbour" (or Mr. Chadwick or Mr. Spink or any of the other boys whom he particularly liked to disparage at the moment) "Mr. Barbour, I wish that when 133

.. • you're in school at least you would try and keep yourself a little more tidy. Your fingernails, now just look at 'em!" In the act of calling attention to poor Barbour's dirty hands he would take great pains to display his own ostentatiously. "I'll bet your mother wouldn't let you go around home looking like that!" Then if his victim showed the proper amount of embarrassment he would leave him to his discomfort and walk away-proudly pleased with himself and chuckling with delight.

As I walked into Principal Benson's office I realized the conspicuousness of the rôle I was playing-for playing I was, showing off for the gratification of my vanity. In the office were Miss Darrel, another teacher, and Floyd Smith, a well-behaved senior. My knees began to tremble. "Well, Mr. Holland, perhaps you can explain why you have suddenly taken to deliberately walking out of chapel without permission-interrupting one of the most solemn periods of our whole day's work, without even so much as excusing yourself." (Professor Benson always had his defendant thoroughly convicted even before he would begin to ask him questions. With his phenomenal powers of penetration and his unique faculty of prognosis he usually knew “how,” “when,” “where," and "why" both God and man did everything. One of his most obstinate axioms was that high school boys were heavily endowed with "original sin.") "Come, now! I'll give you exactly one minute to think up some excuse for your indefensible act." He pulled out his watch, looked at it portentously, and then smilingly turned around to see if his audience were properly appreciating his magnificent tactics. "There ain't any excuse!" I blurted out. "I walked out of chapel 'cause I wanted to!"

This was hardly what Professor Benson had expected. 134

The smile on his face changed to an expression of deep bewilderment, of flabbergasted incredulity. He coughed nervously and his eyes blinked. For a moment my trembling ceased. Now that my danger was far greater than that of the preceding hour-and the fear I felt was correspondingly intense-my whole being changed to an attitude of reckless abandon. For one brief second I saw myself as a hero and sensed the elation of triumph. Then the principal reached out his heavy hand and seized me by the shoulder. 

Panic-stricken, I wrenched myself free and ran out the door. 

Alas! There is no more to tell of my academic career! I never returned to school again.

During the remainder of those two years in Morristown I worked for Father and went around with girls. One was as unpleasant as the other was difficult. To drive the team from eight in the morning to five at night and take care of the horses after hours made the days seem like months, and the months like centuries. While to be alone with a girl in the evening or on Sunday usually demanded the exercise of very plausible dissimulation—that is, of course, in addition to considerable cooperation. 

But it must not be supposed that I ever regretted leaving school-nor on the other hand that the school was greatly disquieted by my departure. Miss Darrel, alone, was excepted. It is curious how certain we are of another's shortcomings when we realize how ridiculously wrong they are in appraising ours. The consciousness of our own perspicacity is still more gratifying if it ingeniously discerns the benightedness of others. Never for a moment did I see Mr.

Benson as a creature who had appetites, hopes, and desires like myself, but rather was I sure that he was a tyrant whose consuming passion was to torture his students. On the other hand, Miss Wright who "taught" me Latin, could not con- 135

ceive of me as an individual who might develop a love for the Classic languages, but must look upon me always as an incorrigible ox whose only pleasure was to resist her teaching. 

Since I was now the oldest boy at home my position in the household had gradually undergone the metamorphosis from "the difficult child" stage to that of the wayward son. 

In the parlor Father would await my multitudinous returns from dark excursions, holding in store for me salvos and volleys of parental explosions and paternal admonitions instead of the erstwhile perennial "beech-oil." It was as if the violent methods of Solomon having failed he was at last resigned to using moral suasion. “Peter, you're on the downward path," he would say. “And if you're not careful you're going to wake up some day and find yourself in a place you won't like a little bit." His voice seemed to be struggling to convey both patience and intimidation, striving for a control it could not attain. "A fool despiseth his father's instruction: but he that regardeth reproof is prudent. . . . The way of the fool is right in his own eyes: but he that hearkeneth unto counsel is wise.''

Then if I came in particularly late, that is to say any time between ten-thirty and twelve, Father would be especially exasperated. Seemingly forgetting about Christian forbearance, he would cry: "Confound you, you're goin' to try my patience once too often! I hate like poison to put you in a place where they'll make you act the way you ought to, but I want to tell you if you drive me to it I'll do it! I tell you I'll do it! . . . My boy, my patience is just about worn out with you!" 

Toward the end of these harangues my father's voice would have risen from an ill-controlled barytone to a hysterical pitch, terrifying and pitiable. I remember that I 136

would then decide secretly to mend my ways and cause him no more trouble; not because of the threats he had uttered, but because the spectacle of his fanatical passion had made me feel guilty of driving him mad. That is, I was influenced in the end to try to govern my conduct according to his wishes not because I considered his wishes superior to mine, but because I did not want to hurt his feelings: not from fear and respect but from pity and kindness. And it is thus always that the workable ethics of the world are evolved. 2 During our last summer in Morristown there were-as there had been during previous summers-many visitors at our house. My grandmother would say: "True as you're livin', if you live by the St. Lawrence in the summertime you're bound to have company-more company than you want or know what to do with. Folks you never hear from and have nearly forgotten come to visit you. And they know what they're doin', all right, you can depend on that, for there's no better place in the country to spend the summer than on the old St. Lawrence. But you'll never catch 'em comin' around here in the winter, not by a long shot."

Nevertheless Grandmother was glad to see company. She loved visitors and she loved to see them enjoy themselves. "I think a picnic would be kind o' nice for all these people, Amos," she would say when the house was full of guests. 

And at seventy she was doing her share to make picnics a success. One never asked if she would be feeling in fit shape to take a boat ride and to eat lunch with cold tea on the shores of the river. Hers was the enthusiasm around which such excursions were built. Once the party was in progress, with the young people in swimming and “having a good 137

time," she was beaming all over, unpacking the lunch, posing for snapshots, and warning Grandfather, "For conscience' sakes, don't go and eat so many deviled eggs this time and get so sick like you did the last."

One Fourth of July Father came home from Potsdam with a surrey full of company. The hotel had been finished for over a year and since the village trustees had finally decided "not to build a Town Hall just yet" there had been no further work in Morristown. He had had to return to 

Potsdam to get a job. That year he was building a house for lawyer Cubbs, Potsdam attorney and president of the town board. And though times had been hard and Father had lost money in building the Morristown hotel, under the flattering patronage of lawyer Cubbs (who kept telling him how much Potsdam missed him and how great a mistake he had made in moving away, and how successful he would be if he followed his advice and came back and lived there in Potsdam) he was once more optimistic about the opportunities in the future and the success he would be making in another year.

So it was always with Father. He did not know how to be discouraged. At forty, at fifty and at seventy he accepted failure as a temporary setback: the hand of Providence momentarily chastening him for religious shortcomingsthe whole to be assuaged and eliminated by more work and deeper piety, if not in this world, certainly in the next. He was convinced that he had lost money in building the hotel because-like most hotels in those days-the plans and specifications had called for a bar. For a Methodist churchmember to build a bar was just about one step removed from directly abetting the Devil.

However, the episode of the hotel was now long past and presumably the sin had been expiated. Father once more 138

was, as we all would express it, beginning to get on his feet. Hence on this Fourth of July of which I have just written he was the genial host again, bringing home a surrey full of company. True to his character, of course, his guests were all women. I should say girls, for not one in the company was over sixteen. And hence for two days it was not necessary for me to go rowing alone or to hope for a storm, a shipwreck, or a fire to make things exciting. Grandmother-dear Grandmother!-suggested a picnic. A picnic, of course, was the ideal thing. Not only did it take one down to the shores of the river, where one most wanted to be, but it also avoided cluttering up the house. If all of us stayed at home there were enormous meals to prepare, dishes to wash, entertainment to be furnished, and furniture to polish; while playing croquet was the only form of amusement. At the river one could go in swimming, play in the rowboat, run through the woods, build campfires, make improvised rafts, and eat quantities of lunch. And always there was the possibility of some adventure with the girls.

On this particular day and in this last matter I was extraordinarily fortunate. Girls had come to mean so much in my life that if the thoughts of them were not immediately upon the surface of my consciousness they lurked but a little ways beneath, always ready to be summoned by a glimpse of limbs and lips, the swish of a skirt, the odor of lilacs or wild roses, the variegated visions which came before my closed eyes shortly after I had gone to bed at night. So when Father brought home four girls (two cousins and two friends of Ruth's from Potsdam) I was particularly happy.

There was Violet Perry, whom I had not seen since I was very small and whom I had remembered as a gawky, 139

freckled girl who cried so easily when you teased her. Now her dignity, her reserve, the enticing way in which her freckles decorated the deep valleys of her misty blue eyes, the superior way in which she spoke-all made me very proud that she was my cousin, and fearful that because I hadn't properly appreciated her before she would, if she remembered it now, take revenge by ignoring me. Yet there was something about the kindness of her expression (when she was not trying to be so very dignified) and the abundant yellowness of her hair which suggested that she could never be cruelly unwilling. Moreover, if she were, there was another cousin, Eva Vroman, who, if not as pretty, certainly appeared to be very easy to mold to my wishes. If I were nice to her I might therefore make Violet jealous. Somehow or other, vivid demonstrations of interest such as unmistakable jealousy, or an eagerness to be alone with me in the parlor, behind the barn, in the woods gathering flowers, were necessary in order to convince me that a girl "liked" me or was the least concerned about my feelings for her. 

Yet, terrible as I felt at that time when I was uncertain of the regard which one girl or another held for me, that very suspense was exciting—and hence alluring. The assurance of their devotion was pleasant enough (though once possessed it was sometimes awkward to know what to do with), but the precariousness of not knowing how they felt was far more provocative. So the elation I felt during the hours succeeding the moment of that Fourth of July afternoon when I kissed Violet and found a responsiveness and a surrender in her lips for which I had not even dared hope, could not compare in richness, agitation or tensity with the tantalizing suspense of the hours preceding it. 

We had gone down along the river two miles, to the shores of our old farm, to have our picnic. It was a territory 140

which I loved and with which I was very familiar. I had fished there often in the early morning and late at night. 

Among the groves of cedar and hickory which dotted the pasture from the old farmhouse down to the river I had hunted rabbits and elusive partridges. In the log-cabin boathouse on the shore I had frequently sought shelter from fierce downpours of rain and fiercer bands of imagined Indians. Recently the primeval ruggedness of the territory had been polluted by the erection of a "modern" cottage and an elaborate boathouse for housing a motor-boat, a circumstance which I then resented primarily because the occupants of the cottage (who rented the place from Father and came there during the summer) resented my going in swimming without a bathing-suit on. 

Fortunately on that day they had gone off to the Thousand Islands. Thus the girls could appropriate the boathouse for a place to undress and change to their bathing-suits. I suggested that they do so, with a very definite purpose; for, being familiar with hidden ways of swimming underwater to the dark recesses of the piers which supported the boathouse and thence, unseen, commanding a view of its interior, I artfully plotted to satisfy my restless and everpresent curiosity to see what girls looked like without their clothes on.

Getting into my bathing-suit in the old log-cabin boathouse I looked out through the crannies between the logs and could see the silver sheen of the river, calm in the warmth of the still July day. Its violet coolness invited me and made me happy. I thought of the girls walking toward the new boathouse to get undressed. The vision of their imagined bodies came before my eyes, floating and swimming like pale ivory nymphs over the surface of the violet water. For a fleeting second I was seized with the desire to 141

leap out into the river without my bathing-suit on. I wanted to defy the sensibilities of every one about me. But the very thought immediately started me to trembling with fear. My younger brother Johnny came into the cabin to get undressed. I slipped into my bathing-suit and plunged into the water.

Silently swimming along the shore I reached the piers of the precious retreat in which the girls were now getting ready for bathing. Father was walking up through the pasture to the spring to get water for the lemonade: Mother was taking care of baby Jenny; Grandmother was unpacking the lunch; Grandfather had gone off, cane in hand, to search for suitable thorn-apple branches out of which to make more canes. I was unobserved on my watery journey toward forbidden beauty.

In another moment I was opposite one of the crevices in the boards which surrounded the little motor-boat harbor. 

With heart beating so violently that it seemed it must surely make ripples in the calm water, I slowly, guiltily, raised my head. For some moments I lingered in pleasurable anticipation of the scene which was to appear before me, while into the background of my consciousness a terrifying command kept insinuating itself: "Thou shalt not uncover the nakedness of a woman .. the nakedness of thy sister thou shalt not uncover." Presently I lifted my eyes and saw the full contours of a girl's body. My glance touched the smooth, white firmness of her hips, the graceful architecture of her legs as she lightly moved them to slide into her bathing-suit; embraced the subtle swelling of her breasts, swinging with their elastic virginity; and came to rest on the tanned sweetness of her face. Then in recognition of the familiar features I was struck with awe! The girl was my sister Ruth. 142

I glided away from the crevice in violent consternation. But the chatter and laughter of feminine voices soothed my agitation and I floated back to peer once more at the frieze of beautiful figures. This time my glance fell upon the dark loveliness of one of the other girls. Her name was Elizabeth.

Unknowingly she laughed in the adoration of my sight, her eyes sparkling with the pleasure of the faint cooling air on her unclothed body, her small red lips opening joyously over the even pearls of her glistening teeth. 

I was startled by the call of my name, coming across the smooth water. "Peter! Peter! Where are you?"

Johnny had leaped into the river and was looking for me. 

There was terror in his voice, the terror of a ten-year-old who, not seeing me, thought perhaps that I had been drowned. I wanted to answer him, to reassure him, but fear of discovery was greater than compassion. Quickly I dived under the edge of the boathouse and swam underwater as long as my breath held out. I came to the surface several yards from shore. Johnny's voice was still screeching through the air, alarming everybody into a speculation as to my whereabouts. There were shrieks of hysterical laughter from the girls. Alas, I thought, I have been discovered! 

But I hadn't, for Johnny suddenly saw me and stopped his shouting. I learned later that when he had suspected me he, shunning the stigma of tale-bearing more vigorously than any other sin of childhood, had tried to shield me.

The voices of the girls had returned to their normal pitch and I swam more swiftly away from their boathouse, tingling all over with a magic excitement, as one who has come from his first baptism of forbidden and sacred beauty. 

For the rest of that day and on into the night, after I had gone to bed, the spell of enchantment hung over me. Each 143

time as those lovely images-now enriched and infinitely varied by the play of my imagination—would reappear upon the curtain of my memory my head would reel with pleasure and my body shiver with sweet pain and yearning.

Even the recollection of Violet's kiss grew dim as I thought of those figures standing there on the platform, like a lovely and immortal American frieze waiting to be sculptured for the delectation of coming generations. Naturally I did not then realize why the image of beauty, elusive and unpossessed, returned to me more vividly, more poignantly provoking, than a loveliness already made my own. I had not learned the philosophy of “unfinished pleasure." I was not yet aware that a kiss once taken—a goal once won-loses the magic of infinite anticipation.
