On the following Sunday morning the surrey was made ready, and Father started back to Potsdam with the bevy of girls. Now, save for one, they were all apparently my enemies. I had gone from one to another with shameless inconstancy, winning their kisses and then retiring. The "unattainable" Elizabeth had been the last, her dark, proud and prepossessing beauty having resisted conquest until just the evening before. As I watched her leave, a sadness filled my heart. I had been seized at last with an incurable love.

I had become inspired with great ambitions, determined to go back to school and make a name for myself. Elizabeth was very brilliant and had talked of going to college. If my life were to have a great tragedy, it would be my inability to marry her. 144

CHAPTER XI 

My mother was an incurable devotee of proverbs and platitudes, sayings and superstitions. If her right ear rang she was bright and happy, awaiting the good news which she knew it foretold. If on another day some curious disturbance in her left ear produced a similar sort of ringing, her expression became downcast and her manner sober: one never knew how serious a portent might be lurking in such a sign. "Pride comes before a fall!" she would say when any of us went about the house boasting of what we could do, or when her sponge cake "fell" in the oven shortly after she had proudly peeked at its promising fluffiness.

During the summer the possibility of "thirteen at table" frequently threatened, and on those particular occasions she considered it very fortunate that Jenny was still a baby, for with small protest and little ado one person could thus be added to or subtracted from the dinner-table as the emergency demanded. (If Jenny was hardly a full "person,” in so far as matters of social precedence were concerned, she was at least a numerical entity when it came to outwitting the evil that lurked in "thirteen.")

It often happened that Mother's proverbs would conveniently cancel each other. Thus when Father began to talk of moving again—this time to Ogdensburg—a real city which boasted a population of eighteen thousand, such sayings as "A rolling stone gathers no moss!" or "Nothing ventured, nothing won!" or "Far-away pastures always look green!" or "A new house, a new heart!" applied according 145

to whether at the moment Mother was washing lampchimneys and thinking of the advantages of gaslight in Ogdensburg, or whether she was entertaining the Ladies' Aid and ruminating about the "stuck-up" women who, she had heard, often belonged to city churches. As it became more and more certain that we were to move to Ogdensburg the proverbs in favor of a semi-nomadic existence began to bravely multiply until when the last load was on the wagon and Mother finally settled in the buggy, there was an outbreak of tears and muffled sobbing. The real feeling of her heart was to have its moment of expression, after all.

As for me, there had never before been anything so exciting, so wonderful, so full of promise as living in a citythe city of Ogdensburg. It seemed as though life, tame, ordinary, dull and monotonous, had suddenly changed into an endlessly fascinating round of novelty and adventure. 

The streetcar which ran from one end of the city to the other guaranteed me a pleasure (whenever I had five cents) which I knew I would never tire of. The joy of going to the moving picture theater was so intensely rich and keen that it needs must be accompanied with the painful feeling of sin. The stores, filled with so many fascinating articlesmany of which I had never seen before except in pictures in the Sears and Roebuck catalogue-were glittering emporia of unceasing delight. On Saturday afternoons there were baseball games at the Fair Grounds. On Sunday afternoons even the semi-pious stole off to Sandy Beach, where there was always a crowd of gay people in swimming, picnicking, riding the "shoot-the-chutes," devouring icecream cones, roller-skating, "spooning," taking boat rides, laughing, shouting. To me, they were living-living as people in 

Morristown never did. 146

Father had traded the farm for a huge brick house, whose magnitude, splendor and pretentious grounds seemed to indicate a richness of living, a height of luxury to which our family could never attain, let alone rightfully belong. There were quarters for servants on the basement floor-and heaven knows we had never had servants and wouldn't have known what to do with them if we had. The lawn was elevated at least three feet above the level of the street and held in its elevation by means of a retaining wall. As though this were not enough to make the place look exclusive, this wall was surmounted with a fence of iron pickets, while around the borders of the lawn there were beds of cultivated flowers, running back to an immense grape arbor elaborately and extravagantly constructed to provide shade and shelter on sunny summer afternoons. "Why, great God Almighty!" Grandfather would say. "It'll take all of a man's time just to take care of this place! 

To say nothin' of keepin' it a-goin' or findin' any time to enjoy it."

But I liked the house as I liked Ogdensburg. The big rooms with their high ceilings and lavish woodwork, the brilliant gaslights and marble fireplaces, the arabesque of grillwork, and the big heavy doors, represented to me the paramount forms of grandness and excellence toward which I had aspired since the days I had first known Doria Sessions. It was as though if I couldn't be very grand in myself (and I somehow seemed to keep postponing the process of becoming so indefinitely and in toto) I must call upon the aid of external arts and entrenchments to imbue me with a sense of importance for which I so passionately yearned.

At this time I was floundering around in a base and paltry life of worldly pleasure, putting off all efforts to 147

pursue serious ambitions always until tomorrow when conditions would be more ideal. When school opened, and in a fever of good intentions, brought on by the memory of dark-eyed Elizabeth, I went the first day to the Ogdensburg Academy to present myself once more for scholastic instruction, I became appalled and embarrassed by the comparative youthfulness of my classmates. I decided that some other time would be better for continuing my education. 

Then, seeking respite for my embarrassment and self-disgust for not carrying out my intention, I went home, dug up some builder's hardware which Father had stored in the barn, took it over to the junk-dealer and sold it, and went on an excursion to Sandy Beach with the proceeds. The roots of my conduct were imbedded in desire for immediate pleasure with as little effort as possible. When the day at last arrived for a serious reckoning I would give myself over to that not unattractive process of being redeemed and devote myself thereafter to the sober duties of a mature Christian. As long as you're going to be saved-I argued to myself—you might as well be saved from a whole lot.

That the imposing brick "mansion" into which we had moved was situated in a neighborhood which was no longer fashionable, indeed hardly respectable, did not substantially lessen the feeling of pride which it gave me. For though its impressiveness did not place me on speaking terms with sons of flour manufacturers, department store owners, bankers and wholesale coal-dealers, it at least gave me a sense of somewhat more than equality with the young people in our immediate vicinity. Meanwhile Mother was helping with the eagerness which was ever hers—to aid in the material welfare of the family by taking in boarders and roomers. In the beginning most of the boarders were 148

girls who worked in the silk factory. "I don't care how they make an honest living," Mother would say, "as long as they're decent and respectable." But while virtue was the essential upon which my mother insisted, I was keenly disappointed because "her girls" were not more fashionable or at least better-looking. Plainness and chastity from the hillsides of Canada and the farms of St. Lawrence County were qualities which I could scarcely appreciate. 

Into this plainness a vision of beauty came; and touching a spark to new desires, aroused me from the shallow pleasures of early adolescence and spurred me on to taste the moving and cataclysmic experiences of young manhood. 

Coming home one evening from the State Hospital, where I had recently got a job as an attendant with the hope of being able to play on the baseball team, the sounds from our piano came vibrating through the parlor windows to meet me as I passed through the iron gate and up the walk to the side door. Since our piano had long been silent it must be company. I wondered excitedly who could be playing. Perhaps Mathew had come home for a visit following his recent marriage in Rochester! A girl friend of Ruth's might have come unexpectedly from Potsdam. 

Through the dining-room and on through the sittingroom I hurried, expecting any second to encounter some one who would rush up to me with news such as: “Elizabeth has come for a little visit!” or “Mathew and his wife have just arrived from Rochester!" But no one appeared. I reached the parlor door unannounced, and curious. And then-O marvelous quality of beauty which loads each fresh appearance with a new delight. O truth at once eternal and felicitous-while the apparition on the piano-stool continued her melodic and rhythmic way, unwitting of my 149

presence, I stared enchanted at her evanescent being. Beneath the linen of her dress there was a special something, magic and alluring, which could not in substantial words be defined. Like a wave of unseen magnetism, infinite and enveloping, emanating from the wavy blackness of her hair, the delicate profile of her face and neck, the fragile grace of her hands as they moved across the keys producing subtle harmonies, it embraced and drenched me with a tenuous spell. At once I fell in love with this girl whom I had never before seen.

It seemed as though I stood there for a long time before she turned around and saw me. It was probably only a few seconds, although all the memories of my past life, ignoble, trivial and empty as it appeared to me before, came rushing through my mind pregnant now with meaning and importance. Just as years before my whole existence had acquired for a few days a real and serious significance, following a kiss which Miss Darrel had given me after an 

English conference in her classroom in Morristown High

School, so once again my inmost nature had discovered the key to the universe, and abstruse ontology contained no more enigmas.

When the girl finally noticed me there was an abrupt hiatus in her playing, as though she had to catch a sudden breath, and when she picked up the composition again after the merest second of faltering there quickly appeared an error here and there in the harmony of her rendition, as though self-consciousness had seized and robbed her of her skill and memory. I could not imagine that such a wonderful person would notice me. I fully expected her to go on playing, ignoring me completely; but, luckily at times, musical compositions have endings, and at last the one she was playing came to its final tonic cadence. She turned 150

toward me suddenly, her face flaming with the vivid scarlet of embarrassment. Yet her lips were upturned in a curious smile of amusement. "Please don't let me interrupt you!" I said in my bestmannered voice, for I was eager to let her know that I knew something about politeness. "I hope you'll go on playing. I enjoyed hearing you so much that I couldn't help but just stand here and listen."

She appeared wholly unaware that I had been talking. 

She stared at me for a moment with extraordinary frankness, her large dark eyes seeming to examine me minutely, her senses preoccupied as though she were unaware of what 

I had said. "You're Peter, aren't you?" she finally blurted out, "Your mother told me about you. I'm a new boarder. My name is 

René Picard."

I had apparently passed her examination successfully, for her smile broadened into a warm, inviting acceptance of my presence. It seemed almost that she was trying to soften her unusual and disarming prepossession. But the effect upon me of her rapidly changing manner was an even greater mystification over the nature of women. In the brief space during which I had known her she had, like an extremely versatile actress, portrayed for my confusion and wonder, fragility and sensitiveness, blushing embarrassment, a bold, defiant candor, and now at last a wholly unexpected friendliness. Indeed, I had not had a whole moment to decide just how to take her. "You're quite a big boy, for only sixteen years old. Your mother said you were big for your age, but I hadn't expected to see a grown-up man. Really, you know, if it weren't for those innocent eyes of yours I'd take you for a regular man of the world." She laughed slyly, with the assurance of an 151

omniscient woman. Alas, she must understand how complete was my adoration, tempting and ineluctable as she knew herself to be. Nothing is hidden from her, I thought; she undoubtedly knows the audacious thoughts that have been running through my head as I have stood here letting my gaze leap from her limbs to her lips, and wandering from there to her hair and her hips. Yet as I stammered a word here and there, in reply to her comments and questioning glances, a feeling within me kept gradually growing that she also liked me and was willing to show it. 

In my heart at that moment I realized that there was nothing which contributed more to my happiness than that feeling. Though I might be bashful, inarticulate, and awkward, I nevertheless tingled all over with pleasure; and though I had reached that age in this strange mixed affair we call Life when I thought of myself as having got off to a very bad start yet I must needs have hope again in the thought that René Picard liked me. For to youth the fact that another person likes one implies that that person has faith in one. I needed some one to have faith in me.

If you are miserable and wretched, slopping along in a cold wet street, with soaking feet and thoughts suicidal; if you are hopelessly sad and your soul is sunken, weighed with the sense of your own impotence-and then you suddenly spy a silk-clad ankle which lifts your eyes from melancholy depths to dancing hips: a swaying form beneath, perhaps, a bright hued raincoat . . . thence to a dewy face of rosy beauty ... and eyes that smile approval-then your whole world changes: the sun shines again and life has a purpose. So I felt when this girl in the parlor of our brick house in Ogdensburg smiled at me. And though it was far more pleasant to have the rays of her attention directed 152

toward me than toward the piano, I asked her again—for the want of something to say as I regained my composure following the elation which had come to me-if she would not go on playing. "Playing?" she said. "I'd much rather talk. I can play when I'm alone, or when you've gone back to work. What time do you have to go back? Do you like your job down there with all those crazy people? I should think it would be interesting. What are they like?”

Though she did not give me a chance to answer her questions immediately after she asked them, it must not be supposed that they were uttered perfunctorily or wholly for the purpose of putting me at ease. She was curiousintensely so concerning every question she asked. 

I told her of the strange people I encountered at the hospital, who were like apples which are perfect except in one spot; who possessed many fascinating qualities, but lacked the essential one of balance; whose commonest weakness, according to the doctors, was their constant propensity for thinking aloud. I told her of how I was gradually acquiring my old skill in pitching baseball and that I hoped before long to become a regular member of the hospital's baseball team. I told her of how, being sent one day to watch a gang of the patients as they unloaded a carload of coal, I had gone to sleep and had suddenly awakened to find that my charges had vanished. They had heard the twelve o'clock whistle and gone to dinner without me. . . .

I talked on and on. René still listened. Mother came into the parlor and went through the ridiculous formula of introducing us to each other. We talked some more and then 

René played the piano. After considerable urging she got me to sing. I had wanted to sing all the while, but I had accounted it the proper procedure to consent to such request 153

with the most modest reluctance. We played and sang until the rest of the boarders complained to Mother that they would like to get to sleep. Then we went out on the side porch, which led into the grape arbor, and sat there talking, inhaling the aphrodisiac odors of an early May evening. 

That night I was late in getting back to the hospital. The dormitory was locked at midnight and it was a quarter to one when I arrived. I took off my shoes and climbed up the fire escape. I stepped through an open window into a room occupied by three other attendants. Noiselessly I crossed the floor into the corridor and made my own room without discovery. I lay awake thinking of the girl I had just met; of a feeling which she gave me which I had never experienced before; of the invigorating candor with which she approached all of life; of the astounding manner with which she had made sin appear virtuous; of her big dark eyes and husky voice. I went off to sleep thinking of how 

I would make myself worthy of her.

To this day when spring comes around and with it the immemorial practice of seasonal cleaning, when the carpets are torn up and taken outside for a beating, the woodwork is scrubbed and the floors polished, the yard is raked and the rubbish burned, the closets are aired and fortified against moths, there appears in the atmosphere an intermingling of odors which, passing through that strange olfactory door of my memory, recreates there a vision of baseball. 

Ah, baseball! But a word; yet with a world of associations. A velvet green diamond on a sunny May afternoon! 

An exuberance of bodily energy which one cannot-try as he may-wholly expend! A brand-new baseball with its 154

gleaming white cover, its aroma and "feel" which make one shiver with delight, its spherical beauty and its irresistible invitation to be thrown through the air, to be smacked resoundingly with a right-balanced bat, to be sailed through the heavens in long graceful trajectories! Ah, the lure and delight in a pungent-smelling catcher's mitt!! The satisfaction of the sound as the ball socks into it! The thrill of elation from a line drive caught and held! The paroxysm of pleasure from a slashing hit when the bases are full! All of those sensations come whisking through my mind-now heightened and colored with the glamour of vicariousness, bathed and embellished with the pathos of Time-when the door of my memory is opened with the scent . . . sometimes, perhaps, of burning dead grass, sometimes again of redolent floor oil which we used in our youth on our newly bought baseballs. 

Happy indeed was I when I could play baseball. The game was a glorious floodgate for my animal spirits. With an appreciative grandstand and many opportunities to become an athletic hero it almost wholly promised me emotional fulfilment. That spring I had wonderful opportunities to play baseball. When the weather was fine there was practice every afternoon, the members of the squad being excused from their other duties at the hospital. On 

Saturday afternoons there was usually a game. Then to be out on the diamond in the warm sunshine, playing the game I loved while other fellows of my age were cooped up in schoolrooms, boning on their Latin and memorizing their English poets—and to be paid for my playing at the rate of forty dollars a month, with room and board-it all seemed more and more to be an unusually lucky circumstance. 

Father had gone to Erie, Pennsylvania, to superintend 155

a construction job, hence I was almost free to do whatever I pleased. Off duty in the evenings, I went up to the city to see René, and the hours with her were crowded with pleasure and enlightenment.

Having for the first time in my life pocket money enough to buy icecream sodas whenever I liked, to take René to the moving pictures, to go to Sandy Beach and excursions up the river, I began to feel at last that I had come into my own. I looked forward to the future with a keen sense of happiness. I meant to make of myself a famous baseball player and thus be able to travel around the country in ease and security with René. Each day on the athletic field 

I rampaged about with eagerness and energy, despite-on many occasions-having been out very late the evening before. Though my pitching was still wild and mostly a matter of sheer physical strength, it was pleasant to overhear remarks about my speed and my curves. "If he ever calms down and uses his head," the coach would say, "he'll make a great pitcher." For my part, however, I was content at the moment with the gifts which I had and the praise which they brought me. Very comfortably my feeling of self-importance grew, and I awaited my chance to appear in some critical and prominent game. 156

CHAPTER XII 

YES, at sixteen my being was infused with the ambition of becoming a baseball hero. What better path I could have followed at the time, with my aversion to work and the suspended animation of my mind, has not yet been revealed to me. The church had had its chance to make of me a theological student or an anti-saloon league snooper, but had got no farther with the job than to make a conscious sinner. In school I'd been exposed to the sundry arts of pedagogy ostensibly to mold me into a scholar or a bond salesman, but even there the effect was a case of mental dyspepsia. Baseball, alas, was not only the height of my interest, but also, alack, the level of my intelligence. 

The day came along with a chance to become a hero. 

It was the hardest game of the season, which we scarcely hoped to win. The famous "Reds" of Ogdensburg had journeyed down to play us. Rooters from the city and the "less-demented" from the hospital crowded the grandstand. 

René had come down with Johnny and Ruth, bringing a lunch for a picnic after the game. It seemed more and more true as I glanced at their eager faces in the crowd that events of importance were revolving around me. Glowing with pride and happiness, I decided that I could ask no more of life than such moments as these. I was not even sure that I would get a chance to play in the game, but my confidence consoled me that if I did pitch we would win. 

That confidence was shaken with terrifying results when to my tremendous surprise I was selected to start the game. 157

My knees trembled ungovernably and my heart-beat was vehement, until finally my mind was able to conjure up a vision of Grandmother smiling calmly at danger. For four innings I pitched with the determination of a demon, following assiduously the signals from the coach. Having held the Ogdensburg "Reds" scoreless, my confidence returned in conceited proportions. 

The firmament was never brighter nor a June day more glorious. My pride was as buoyant as the light, magic air. 

Every cheer from the grandstand seemed meant for my ears, which sucked in praise till my head must expand. To strike out as many men as I might happen to wish seemed not very difficult, and attractive. I decided to ignore the coach's signals for a few innings just to prove to myself that I could do as well without him. But to my great consternation plans immediately went wrong: two men got hits whom I had intended to strike out, and I had given a base on balls to a third in my eagerness to fan him. Something was wrong, but I couldn't believe it was I. My bewilderment increased with my incredulity. Three men on base and not a single out!

The next man up was a notably weak batter and the coach signaled: "Force him to hit." But now that I had gone so far in my plight I determined to strike him out if it was the last thing I did. My widest curves grew wider and wilder until presently the batter went to base on balls, forcing a run in—and forcing me out.

I walked toward the bench ingloriously defeated. The coach glared at me with anger and disgust. "I guess ya sprained your wrist so ya couldn't throw nothin' but curves -huh?" His voice was merciless and choking with sarcasm. The state of wretchedness which I entered was abysmal. Not, if you please, because I considered myself 158

hopeless, but because of the glory I had foolishly lost. For just as the unfailing frustrations of the universe are not nearly so hard to bear as provincial little upsets which might have been avoided, so the thought of lost fame which -save for an error of judgment-would surely have been mine, was far more depressing than any suspicion that I might not, after all, be a great baseball player.

But when, after the game, I had taken a shower and changed my clothes and gone down to the river to join

René and my brother and sister in their picnic, and there observed that every one else seemed to be bubbling with joy and insouciance; that Ruth had apparently forgotten there had ever been a ball game; that the sight of René's white linen dress clinging closely to her athletic hips as she bent over the lunch-basket was as ravishing to me as evermy pulses began to throb with the beat of happiness once more, my wretchedness suddenly vanished like night before a sudden dawn.

This period of rediscovered joy existed until René was gone, and I had gone back to my eight o'clock duty to be alone with my self-accusing thoughts.

So the summer sauntered on, to come at length to those days drawn indelibly in my memory—vivid, beautiful and full of sentient wonder. Father had come home for a few days from Erie, Pennsylvania. Sometime before his arrival

I had got into the habit of occasionally staying at home all night instead of going back to the hospital, awakening early in the morning and returning in time for duty. A few times, not heeding my alarm clock, I had been late and threatened with dismissal. When Father heard of this he stormed about such a wicked and wilful practice and threatened to take very drastic steps if I couldn't stop staying up till all hours of the night with girls and begin to 159

pay attention to business. "After this, the doors of the house are going to be locked at twelve o'clock and you're going back to the hospital."

Once I was with René exhaustion alone could break the spell of her magnetism. No laws, no morals, no parental threats had any power over me in the sovereign domain of her presence. There, momentarily, I was inviolate. Her presence transformed those usually formidable foes of my impulses into their positive, petty proportions. For in the flights of my imagination, in the intoxication of discoveries undreamed, in the first minute squirmings of my powers of ratiocination, she appeared to me as something much greater than a woman. Not that she was at all celestial or unearthly: the mystery of her nature was wholly human; but those human qualities were arranged in such uncommon proportions, with such ineffable design and magnificence, that the fascinating ensemble, though borrowing nothing from Heaven, appeared nevertheless to be superhuman. (A Titaness, I called her many years later-a 

Titaness in spirit with the fragile body of a girl.)

This Titaness worked in an Ogdensburg department store. Born of French and Scotch parents in a Canadian village just outside of Montreal, she had left home at seventeen to taste "life in the city." In the weeks I had known her she had told me very little of her past life, except indirectly as some immediate experience reminded her of it. I knew that she had studied dancing and worked as a waitress; that she had learned to play the piano and to draw, without a teacher. And beneath the grape arbor on Sunday afternoons when I was off duty she had read to me from a book which I had never heard of before: a volume of verse by a man named Omar Khayyam. That she apparently understood it made her appear more than 160

ever to be prodigiously wise; for, like playing the piano or drawing pictures, understanding and enjoying poetry signified to me at that time the unmistakable and mysterious mark of a genius. Such superhuman activities were beyond my accomplishment.

I had come up to the city the second night after my father's arrival with the real purpose of spending the evening with René. We would take the ferryboat to Prescott and buy icecream sodas. But shortly after I arrived and my intention was suspected, Mother said to me: "Why, Peter, you're not going out during the few evenings that Papa's here, I hope! Land sakes, I should think you'd have that much respect for your father." Because she said that I suddenly felt guilty and sheepish. I knew if I stayed at home it would be the same old story: Father would talk for a little while about the job down in Erie, ask me embarrassing questions about how I spent my money, and when I last went to church; and at length, after listening to my lies, would go to sleep in his chair-awakening in time to lock the house and send me back to the hospital. And since under these circumstances I was ashamed to ask René to join the family circle it meant that I would go the whole evening long scarcely seeing her. I would have been able to bear such an evening with difficulty, though the thought of the alternative which I would sacrifice made me miserable and angry.

As usual I had not counted on the extraordinariness of René, who, having known Father only two days, understood most of my difficulties. She came downstairs and started to play the piano-not if you please the Merry

Widow waltzes or gems from the Chocolate Soldier, but Methodist hymns! In a moment the atmosphere of the sitting-room changed. Father smiled grudgingly and wan- 161

dered into the parlor. Presently he was singing bass and beaming all over. When Mother and Ruth had finished washing the dishes they joined us around the piano in a regular Methodist song service. The strains of "How Firm a Foundation," "Love Divine, All Love Excelling," "A

Mighty Fortress is Our God," resounded through the old brick house-a primitive and vocal anodyne for pent-up emotions.

At ten o'clock Grandfather, coming home from an evening spent at the Norman House bar, his breath redolent with the odor of brandy and cloves, strode into the parlor. "Let's have 'All Hail the Power of Jesus' Name'!" he cried in a stentorian voice. "There's a hymn for you to put the fear o' God in any sinner!" His eyes had a diabolical twinkle which Father refused to notice. In a few moments the two of them were competing for the thunderous honors of the contra-bass.

In the midst of the commotion Mother motioned me aside, and pressing a dollar in my hand, sent me out to the store for some icecream. The evening at home had assumed the proportions of a party. 

The turn of events had so diametrically changed my feelings, disrupting anger and supplanting it with pleasure, that I had vaguely begun to wonder if by serving God in singing His hymns we had not all been rewarded with a visitation of His Grace. This dim speculation made me momentarily uneasy, for though I had not at that time wholly finished with God, I had recently been neglecting Him in a life of indulgence. This sudden happiness, which I could not otherwise account for, must have been sent, I suspected, as a tantalizing reminder of the joy of religion which I had been neglecting.

My most uneasy perplexity was concerned with René. 162

Was it possible that despite all she had said she might be religious? Had she denied her heritage of Catholicism during all the weeks I had known her out of deference to the faith which I so indifferently embraced?

Outside the night was quiet and heavy with the rich scents of August-of the summer flowers dying and the raspberries ripening, of fall flowers awakening, and sweetsmelling honeysuckle. Before we had finished eating our icecream and cake in the dining-room René and Ruth, taking their collation with them, wandered out on to the side porch to look at the moon. Presently I "guessed" that I would take a little walk; and in a few moments the three of us were leisurely strolling about the lawn. 

I found such a sense of happiness in the unexpected flow of events, so relieved in my mind of the obsession of sin which normally accompanied my every pleasure, so full of righteousness from having made Mother and Father happy (I did not for one moment think of my inadvertence in so doing) that I began very consciously to rhapsodize over the "wonders of God's universe." To all my exuberance

René said nothing, but gazed at the firmament, smiling enigmatically. Then, after Ruth had gone into the house, her voice broke the night's silence with soft, restrained laughter. "You feel quite religious tonight, don't you?" she observed. Her voice was not mocking, nor critical, nor curious, but rather inflected with a feeling of sympathy. 

Suddenly there came over me a mood of unwonted introspection. I wanted to answer her honestly as to the nature of my uncommon sentiments. And though unfamiliar then, of course, with the fascinating labyrinths of psychology, I nevertheless sensed that my rhapsodizing had not been wholly sincere; that is to say, not knowing that its real purpose was to ingratiate myself with a God in 163

whose existence I had but recently had a renewal of suspicion, I nevertheless realized that it had not come from the bottom of my soul.

So I said to René, in answer to her remark about my religious feeling: "Why, no, not especially; I just feel very grateful." "Grateful for what?" "Why, grateful to you for doing what you did this evening!"

She grasped my hand and drew herself close to me. But what might have followed was brusquely interrupted. 

Father came out on the side porch. "Peter," he called, “isn't it time for you to be starting back?" 

My punishment! I have denied my religion, I thought. 

But I answered: "Yes, I'll be going soon. I have one of the fellow's motorcycles, so I can make it in no time.” "Now, don't go depending too much upon any motorcycle," he scolded. “Get started early! There might something happen to it, and then where'd you be?" And with one last hesitating look toward us he turned reluctantly, as if it were a struggle with his conscience to leave us by ourselves, and walked back into the house. 

The light went out in the dining-room and presently through the windows in the front of the house we could see him reading his newspaper. In a few moments he would be asleep in his chair. Then when he awakened he would make his rounds of the house, lock the doors, and go to bed. If the motorcycle didn't happen to be out front when he bestirred himself he would probably conclude that I had started back to the hospital. Jubilantly I announced to 

René that I had a scheme for fooling him-assuming that she felt as I did about the necessity of staying up later. 

When Father awakened, René and I were already down 164

by the river, sitting on one of the old abandoned docks at the foot of our street, listening to the deep bass whistles of the "lake-boats" as they churned their watery ways up the St. Lawrence, and alternately and intoxicatedly staring at the moon and each other.

But somehow it seemed as though I must always feel guilty. I began to think now of the risks which René was taking. "If we're locked out," I presently assured her, “I can surely crawl in through a window and let you in. Only, I hope Father doesn't try to look in your room." And I added guiltily: "We might go back before twelve, if you'd rather not take a chance."

She was silent, looking thoughtfully at the silver-green water. The prospect of being locked out seemed not to disturb her, as walking through the streets at a late hour at night had not; nor the scandalous possibility of being discovered at such an hour alone with a man. To all of these unorthodox practices she had consented without a protest. 

But now her silence began to disquiet me, like the disturbing voicelessness of impenetrable darkness.

Mother had once told me that some of the other girls considered René very brazen. She had added, defendingly: "I don't agree with 'em. She's always treated me courteously. I guess all's the trouble, she's got more 'n her share of spunk." I, having been frequently startled at some of the unconventional things that she did and said-such as lifting her skirts above her knees while doing a dance, or discussing camisoles and child-birth in front of me without blushing-had gradually come to suspect, from the example of her indifference to them, that customs and conventions were not always unchangeably sound. Even frankness, which years of churchly upbringing had taught me to distrust, had been made attractive to me by the charm 165

which René gave to it. Why was she so very reticent now? 

Was it perhaps that the wisdom of her twenty-one years had suddenly grown weary of unsophisticated youth? Or had the singing of hymns started her pondering deeply over religious problems? 

Timidly I asked her: "What are you thinking about? 

Would you rather go back?" Then suddenly, in a mood of solicitude, I said: "I suppose a girl's got to think of her reputation." 

At the word "reputation" she quickly looked up, searching quizzically the expression on my face. Pity and amusement shone in her questioning eyes. "You're a funny boy, Peter," she said at length. “But I like you nevertheless! I feel like a mother to you-like protecting you from your funny notions!" "Funny notions?" I protested. "What's funny about me?" "Well, for one thing, you rarely like to show what you really think or feel. . . . I mean, dear, when you're happy you always seem to be trying to discover some reason why you should be sad, and when you're miserable you're forever endeavoring to cover it up with a smile . . . as though you liked it." "When did I ever do that? I smile when I'm with you, don't I... and you make me happy." "Yes, but somehow you can't seem to get rid of the idea that there's something naughty about liking me. Tonight, for instance, when we were singing and I would smile at you, you'd give me a tiny little grin as though you were ashamed to show that you were enjoying yourself. And now that we're down here by the river in such a heavenly moonlight you seem to want to keep thinking of being locked out, or what people will say, or what your father 166

will think." She paused for a moment and sighed yearningly. "My goodness! to hear me talk you'd think I was a fellow and you were a very nice girl! Of course I'm older, but I don't care about that. What I mean is, that if you're going to enjoy a thing, enjoy it; or if you don't like a thing, hate it.”

Seizing my arm she slid over onto my lap. I refused to look at her, and kept staring down at the water. But she took my face in her hands and laughingly sought my glance. .. • "Look at me, my great big man, look at me! How do you like to hear your grandmother scold you?"

I had no disposition at that moment to look into the eyes of any one, for I had suddenly become hypnotized by the recognition of a gigantic Truth. The last curtain of concealment had been torn from my soul and I was face to face with the fatuousness of its self-abasement. Doubtless my mind had long been preparing for the discovery and the words which René had uttered, coming at precisely the right moment, had at last revealed, in its complete wondrous form, the eternal necessity for a soul's independence the final accountability which an individual has toward himself. 

René had said: if you want to enjoy a thing, enjoy it! I thought to myself: if you want to do a thing, do it! Don't ask whether God or mankind approve! Dare, then, a little, though you go to Hell for it!

But clear Truth is light which is dangerous to stand in. At first it's intoxicating and then it befuddles; and at length, like the sun, drives mere mortals to shade. So one must not think that the seering effulgence, which I at sixteen had so fortuitously stood in, could thereafter remain 167

my unobscured beacon. Ludicrous indeed-and tragic-the spectacle, were youth withered and seered with the transcendency of Truth.

But that evening, intoxicated, I swore resolutely that henceforth I'd do what my impulses dictated. I'd be done with thinking “if this is sinful" and planning my life so to squeeze into Heaven! Conventions be damned! I'd become independent. Parents and morals could not make a man. 

And so in that moment of fierce resolution my longcherished wish was realized, namely: a glorious escape from eternally imposed restraint, the first victory in my desire for individual supremacy. For I suddenly realized that now 

I was free. The liberty to do anything I wished had at last been achieved simply by unearthing the courage to face the consequences.

And I immediately set out to try my new philosophy. 

I told René that she had been right in gaging my conduct, but from that moment her criticism would never be true again. "To begin with," I said, "I'll tell you what I have never had the courage to say before: I'm crazy in love with you and I'd marry you tomorrow. I'm nearly seventeen and if you'd marry me now, I'd have so much ambition that I could accomplish anything you asked me.” 

Her first reply was to put her arms round my neck very tightly. And in the midst of the pleasure which that reassuring and ever so delightful sort of an answer gave me I pondered the first fruits of my philosophy with satisfaction. 66 '... Dear," she was saying, "you're wonderful, wonderful! . . . I know you could do anything that I might ever ask you. . . . You're brave to even think of marrying me at your age. . . . But you know, darling, I really don't want you to marry me at least now. . . . Someday, perhaps, 168

when you're a little older, and have met more girls and seen more of the world. . . . But as far as loving you is con- 

You were so sweet to 

cerned, I'd marry you tonight. 

ask me. . . It made me tingle all over." "If you really love me, then why not get married tomorrow?" I protested. "I could get a better job if I went to a big city. Mathew's in Rochester; we could go there and try. You know Bob David, the baseball umpire, told me if I'd get a lot of experience, in a year or so I might be able to get into the State League." 

Her expression grew more wistful, and her arms tightened around my neck. "Don't tempt me, dear!" (She punctuated her words with another embrace.) "If you only knew how I'd love to do it! ... But I can't! I can't! . . . It's hard to explain." Her smile became roguish and her manner facetious. “I just simply couldn't go robbing a cradle, now could I? 

You wouldn't want me to do that, now, would you? It wouldn't be fair. You go out and know some other girls first, and then see how you like me.” "Well," I grumbled, "I could hardly have expected you to marry me, anyway. I'm young and don't amount to anything. But, by God, I'll show you that I can! I'll leave 

Ogdensburg tomorrow! And not come back till I make good!" 169

CHAPTER XIII 

WITH vehement words I announced my "great" decision.

Curiously enough the announcement was as great a surprise to me as it must have been to René, for less than a second before I made it, the idea of leaving Ogdensburg had scarcely entered my mind. 

To be sure, I didn't leave on the following day. Decisions still had their practical affairs which needed attending to. 

But a day later I did set sail from home-embarked on my first independent adventure.

Before I go any further with the telling of my own adventures, I had better set down here what had happened meanwhile to the rest of the Holland family. Ruth, of course, had remained in Ogdensburg, patiently helping Mother with the work of the boarding-house-and goodnaturedly taking care of little Jenny who was then nearly five years old. Having had to leave high school before graduating, her great hope now was that she might soon be married.

Johnny was twelve and would be entering the eighth grade in the fall. Having recently joined the Boy Scouts he was absorbed in "Being Prepared"-against Indians, against robbers, against drowning, and snake bites, and finally against all enemies of Home and the Republic. 

Mark was married and living in Binghamton. Since 171

graduating from business school he had been working in a bank and what with a phrenetic, religious and very loquacious wife, an innate generosity, and a bank clerk's salary, his life had become a complicated problem of “making both ends meet" and paying church pledges. 

At the same time Mathew had a very different difficulty. 

Successful in Rochester, he had reached a proud height from which relations with the rest of the family appeared more inconvenient than welcome. Married to a woman with a passion for praise and attention which amounted to madness in periods of frustration, his problem consisted of how to acknowledge his family and still remain on terms of peace with his wife. Meanwhile he worked-teaching school, playing in a theater orchestra, and, during the summer, tooting a cornet in the Municipal Band. When Father, made bold by his success in the city of Erie, had hinted that he might move the family to Rochester, Mathew had been strenuous in advising him against it, rehearsing the difficulties to be encountered in big cities. "Better stay in the small towns," Mathew warned, "with men of your own calibre, and where competition is not so keen and people not so well educated."

Thus far had each member of the Holland family traveled, along diverse roads to their ultimate destinies-thus far when I began my independent struggle away from my home in a stepmother world. 3 

On the day following my "great" determination I resigned my job and received fifteen dollars pay. I went back to the city to decide where I would go, what train I would take, and when I would leave. Father was leaving that day to 172

return to Erie. For some time I entertained the thought of asking him if I could go with him. He could certainly give me a job where he was working. But against such a prospect stood the main purpose of my departure. I must be free from all restraints and authority. If I wanted independence, complete and unconditional, why not begin at once in my struggle to achieve it!

In the end I went down to the Rutland depot to inquire about trains to Montreal. The city now held an added fascination for me, since René had once lived there and had often spoken about it. But before I could enter the station an unforeseen influence suddenly and ineluctably decided my exodus in a different direction. For there at one of the docks, tied up like a captured sea-monster, was a “lakeboat" with all hands on deck getting ready to leave port.

I knew not where she was going, but I knew that I wanted to be on her. It would be a fitting beginning for my journey into an unexplored world-the fulfilment of a long-held yearning which till that moment had always seemed too daring, too crowded with thrills to be innocent of evil. 

The name of the boat was the S.S. Crocus. I went on board and asked for the captain. A tall, wiry man with steel grayish hair answered my inquiries. "Aft on the quarter deck, my lad, there's where you'll find him.” But when I found the captain I didn't know what to ask him, and finally had to simply blurt out that I was looking for a job. "A job?" he exclaimed. "What kind of a job? A sailor, an engineer, a stoker, a quartermaster," (he grinned) "or a cook? What experience have you had?" "I never done any of those things," I answered, “but I think I could be a sailor. I've been on most every kind of a boat that runs on this river, and I know how to sail." 173

The captain laughed with very evident amusement. "Ha! my lad," he cried, "so you know how to sail a boat, do you? That's fine! That's fine! But I guess that wouldn't help us very much, for we ain't got a yard o' sail on the boat." Turning around, he pointed to a smokestack. “There! 

You see! You see that pipe up there? That's the only sail we got; and the only breeze that fills it is coal: dirty black coal, and a hell of a lot of it! How about it now, would you like a job shoveling coal? Fifty dollars a month, twelve hour watches! It's not very much like handling the tiller of a sailboat. What about it, eh?” "I'll take it," I said. "Where does the boat go?" "Hold on a minute there! Are you just aiming to go some place? You can't do that, you know. You got to stick with the ship until she comes back to this port."

I protested my innocence of such regulations and told him that I just wanted to work on her until she reached her farthermost port, and then get off. "So you got the wanderlust, have you! You just want to go sailing. Well, at least you're honest about it. But I'm afraid I can't do anything for you. I'm sorry." "Thank you, sir," I said, and turned to go, bitterly disappointed. But upon reaching the companionway, he called to me again. “Avast! there lad," he cried laughingly; “heave to a minute, will you!" And walking over in my direction, leaned against the taff-rail and began to examine me with curious intentness. "So you want to go sailing, eh? You look pretty husky, but you don't look very old. How old are you?” "Nearly seventeen." "Seventeen, eh! Well, do you know how to fry an egg?" "Certainly, sir!" I said, wondering what he was driving at. 174

"Well, then, my lad, I might be able to help you out.

I don't know what you're trying to run away from, but I was young once myself. I'll give you a job as third cook, and pay you ten dollars when you leave the ship at Cleveland. 

You want to see the world? Then maybe that'll do you for a while." “I'll take it!” I said, thanked him again, and asked when the boat left. "Tomorrow morning, early. Be on board at eight bells."

After signing the ship's papers I hurried down the ladder, elated beyond all that I could silently bear. Away from the dock, I shouted with excitement. "Cleveland!" I hollered. "A sailor! A sailor! On the Great Lakes!" I felt a great awe at my own unprecedented daring.

But my daring had not yet reached the point of boldness which would enable me to break the news to Father. 

The main cause of my fear was the probability that, knowing my plans, he would prevent me from following them. 

Luckily he was leaving that evening and I would have only Mother to deal with. I' packed my clothes and made ready to leave. My straw suitcase bulged with things which 

I thought I would need: raincoat, sneakers, white flannel pants, baseball shoes, gloves, straw hat and sweater, a jackknife and underwear, and, hesitantly, at last, mainly because I did not want Mother to discover that I had left it behind me, a Bible which she had given me on my thirteenth birthday. 

With everything ready I could not wait until René returned, but must needs hurry up to the store before she had finished work. Now, thought I, she'll be impressed all right, and sorry enough too that I'm really going to leave.

But then, unawares, my mind began to dwell upon the good times I was leaving-the jolly little excursions with 175

René, the baseball games at the hospital, the hundred pleasant moments, so familiar and taken-for-granted, that until now, knowing I must leave them, had seemed so insignificant. Suddenly I perceived as all of us must, when we set out on new adventures away from our comfortable and familiar refuges, whether they be refuges of body, mind or spirit—that the real stuff of pioneering is made of something more than fireside dreams and gentle yearning. As suddenly, I determined that from somewhere within me I'd unearth that stuff. So by the time I met René I had acquired a new pride: the pride of a Perseus, unblenching and indomitable.

When Father had gone I made known the news to Mother. At first she pleaded and endeavored to discourage, and at length became sad and tearfully accepted. “Papa's gone," she wept, "and so are Mathew and Mark. It won't be long now before I haven't got no family left. . . .” She wiped her eyes and straightened her face. “But if you think you got to go, I suppose you'd better; but, for heaven's sake, take care of yourself and don't forget to write!"

Thus in effect I said good-by to my mother. Late that evening after lofty farewells and bathetic embracing, I dragged myself from the arms of René, whose pleasures, alas, had been so poignantly heightened by the sad awareness that they must soon be foregone. 4 

From the arms of a girl who embodies all loveliness; from beneath a grape arbor on an August night, where the blended scents of femininity and late summer roses fill one's nostrils with odorous delight; from the familiar shadows and friendly sounds of a moon-bathed house which I knew 176

as home from these sweet things to a ship's strange forecastle, with its acrid smells and pungent forbiddingness, its unknown faces and repulsive bunks-there was a transition to keep me awake!

And awake I stayed for the rest of the night. The hours passed, but my memories lingered, comparing what I had left with what I had got into: a comparison indeed which sadly depressed me. Aloft on the deck the forecastle watch rang the bell at every half-hour. Two of my shipmates breathed a duet of snores. The dim electric light which hung over by the hatch was the cynosure of eel-flies and hungry mosquitoes. I thought of my bedroom back at the brick house, with the window which looked down on the top of the grape arbor, and of the door into the hallway which I would carefully leave open so that from my position in bed before finally going to sleep I could catch a glimpse of René on her last trip to the bathroom.

The swells from a boat which had just gone down the river were rhythmically lapping our starboard side. The lines which held us fast to the dock were regularly answering with their inhuman wails. A bedbug started a predatory journey up my arm.

A step announced another presence on the deck, and soon the figure of a man appeared in the dim opening. 

It was just past three o'clock and very dark. "All hands stand by for muster!" cried a voice, whose owner began a noisy clatter with a club against the side of the hatch. 

Quickly the crew began to bestir themselves. I put on what few clothes I had taken off, went up on deck and reported to the steward. Before the hour was up the propeller was churning, the hawsers were free and we were gliding out into the serene St. Lawrence.

But just as I had started romanticizing to myself while I 177

leaned over the bulwarks, staring back toward the dock and the rapidly approaching dawn, I was abruptly reminded that I was not a tourist setting out on a lazy voyage, but a member of the crew of a coal-hauling freighter. A tourist, indeed! I was an abysmal third cook! "Ho! there, you dock walloper! Stand by in the galley!" I "stood by" in the galley, washing pots and pans, peeling potatoes and scrubbing vegetables, swabbing the deck and polishing the range, descending into the hold for stores and serving the captain his coffee, waiting on the crew's mess and running errands for the first cook.

Time out of mind they have been sung and written-the lure of the ship, the glory of sailing, the romantic and robust life of the waves, the jolly and rollicking verve of the forecastle. What, then, can I say of the lowliest scullion, who makes things shipshape in a greasy dishpan, who views 

Castor and Pollux through a grimy porthole! Very little, I fear, of either. In the bowels of the ship, I was at last "a-sailin'" and my thoughts at least were aloft in the foretruck.

There were some moments I could spend on deck, away from the stenches of forecastle and galley. Then I would go foreward and lean over the bow, staring ahead into the foam and the future. Before the close of the first day we were out in Lake Ontario with Cape Vincent behind us and our bow plunging straight into the setting sun! And for a few moments the limpid green of Ontario's waters changed to the red-gold of heaven's fire.

For the first time in my life I was out of sight of land. 

Face to face with such a momentous experience my heart began to palpitate wildly. With chest heaving in the enchanted air, throat choking with a strange, unaccountable 178

emotion, fists clenched, muscles tense, I stood erect all set for a fight. But who my assailant would be it was hard to imagine. There was nothing in sight more threatening than the azure sky or the ever-brimming waves which billowed from our side. I was alone on deck and no one was approaching—unless . . . unless perhaps . . . prophetically

I was preparing for all the sorrows and vicissitudes of this vain and wilful world. No small task that! And who's to know, when sinew leaves the field, and heart must carry on, or soul, or mind, what woe will then assail us, what new horrors loom above the plunging bow of this wrecked hull you call your life. Oh, Peter! what? .. • 

What matter? Up helm! Brace forward! You have seen the decks awash; and more-you've seen those fibrous walls mount in your very ship-incarcerating; leaving no escape save through that fragile exit of the mind: Imagination.

I went below to bank the galley fire, then forward to my springless bunk in the pungent-smelling forecastle. 5 Two nights later the S.S. Crocus looped her hawsers round the mooring hooks of a dock in Buffalo. I went ashore piloted by an amiable quartermaster. Krater was his name, a lusty German whose shoreward mission was to "catch" some beer. I'd never drunk. I'd signed the pledge at twelve.

In those days the swinging doors could not allure me. (Drinking by itself has no real appeal to youth; but mix it with the seductions of Prohibition: daring, smartness, and a challenge to its right to choose-then you have bewitchment thrice confounded.) I drank that night to keep the quartermaster company. The beer was bitter and went 179

down very slowly. I chewed sandwiches and pickles in between to take the taste out of my mouth. The quartermaster talked of many things. "Had a woman, lad?" he asked, with the same casualness which he might have used in saying: "Pass the mustard." "Not exactly. . . no." I hesitated, hating to admit it, for

I momentarily felt that without that accomplishment I could not really say I was a man. I wanted wholly and indubitably to be a man, particularly then, upon the occasion of my first glass of beer and my third day of proud independence. "No, you say?” he grinned. "Well then, it's time. How 'bout a piece of tail tonight? I'll fix you up . . . I know some sports; good-lookin' too they are. . . . Cost three dollars, but they're worth it."

Attentively I listened, blood afire, my mind a flaming screen of lurid scenes concocted from an ardent imagination. I heard, but yet could not believe that anything so much desired, so unsurpassably exciting, could actually happen to me. The supreme lure! The highest enticement which Sin possessed in its long list of seducements! Offered to me? I could not believe it! The beer was befuddling me.

Still, it was so. We left the saloon, walked a few blocks, then into a hallway next to a second-hand furniture store and up some stairs. The door was opened by an untidylooking girl, blonde, pudgy, and unctuous. When she recognized the quartermaster she smiled professionally, her brown eyes squinting with a greedy look. He spoke to her familiarly. "Wie gehts, Rachel? Wie gehts it by you?” 

The girl stared at me with a bold appraising eye. I stood very awkwardly, waiting to be introduced. "A friend of mine from the boat," explained the quartermaster. "Is Edith around?” 180

"Sure," she answered, and her professional smile broadened. "Come in! Come in!"

Edith was dark, with eyes that had a dull and shallow gleam like dark-brown buttons, hiding a soul of fear. With difficulty she managed a tense, half-hearted smile. "Come with me, honey!” she whined. "I'll show you a good time."

The light from the worn-out gas mantle shone on her face and gave it an old and dismal look. The chalky powder could not hide the wrinkles. She reached a scrawny arm mechanically around my waist. Her faded crêpe kimono opened in front. A cloud of sickening perfume choked my nostrils, strenuously overdoing its deodorizing task. She led me into a tawdry, garish bedroom. A moth-eaten banner of Niagara Falls decorated a conspicuous spot in the blotchy wallpaper opposite the bed. The ugly dresser was covered with cheap jewelry and gaudy souvenirs. A dowdy corduroy curtain slovenly hung around a makeshift wardrobe.

A wave of nausea swept over me. An acrid stench of disinfectant, medicinal, carbolic, vied with the cheap perfume for supremacy. Edith released her arm, walked toward the bed, turned and held out her palm. "Got something for me?" she whined, her ghastly voice striving to assume a childish plaintiveness.

I stared, bewildered, trying to avoid those beady eyes. 

My blood was still aflame, though I could not imagine myself going ahead with the rôle I had contemplated. Revolted and yet cravenly confused I stood there, not knowing what to do. "Got five dollars, honey?" she whined again.

I reached in my pocket and drew out a bill. She took it greedily, stuck it in her cotton stocking and then began to slip off her kimono. 181

"You're a nice fellow; not like the lousy guy that was here the other day. When I put my hand out for money, he spit in it. God! did I tell him how to go to hell, though."

Thus I was introduced to the so-called “real thing”: that supreme, ineffable experience for which years of Puritan instruction had so perfectly, so inevitably prepared me. I walked back to the ship depressed and heartsick, despising the quartermaster who accompanied me; but most of all loathing myself. "A fine way to start off making good! A swell way to make myself worthy of René!"

But the next morning I awoke feeling strangely refreshed. 

The S.S. Crocus was plowing along through the lingering swells of Lake Erie. The day was clear and serenely blue, with a transparent, soft and feminine air.

As I walked on deck I felt curiously happy. "Perhaps," 

I said to myself, "it would be all right if the woman were clean and not so terrible-looking." But the feeling of revulsion still remained. It was as though I had thirsted and waited and prepared for a drink at a clear, cool spring and had drunk at last of foul, stinking water. The water had quenched my thirst but the bitter taste remained.

Late the next afternoon we docked in Cleveland. All hands "turned to" to relieve the Crocus of her cargo. Supper was late, so the task of making the galley shipshape kept me busy far into the evening. When the work was finally done I willingly stayed on board. Stretching myself supinely on the deck planks of the forwardmost part of the bow, I gazed upward to the innocent sky. The gentle breeze off the lake was cooling and soothing. The lofty firmament spread its blue and star-specked mantle over all, impartially blessing lovers bowered in princely gardens and scullions 182

bunked on decks of grimy coal-boats. The enchanted air seemed for a moment to dispel the cankerous memory of the night in Buffalo. My mind went roving among the stars, dreaming of the old nights at Potsdam, when first I'd lain enraptured by their infinite, impenetrable mystery. 

And other nights besides, when in the frosty crystal air I'd stood outside the church on Christmas Eve, looking and looking for that Eastern Star.

For the first time in my life I caught a glimpse of the world as a whole: a very tiny glimpse in the rôle of a spectator. 183

CHAPTER XIV 

THE illumination of Cleveland was much too strong for my rustic eyes. The little I had seen of Buffalo counted for nothing in the way of preparing me for the next breathtaking city. I stood confused, and my judgment completely left me. I craved the splendor of the “city life," and felt the lure of being rich and "sporty." With nearly twenty dollars in the pocket of my white flannel pants, I decided that I could well afford to take a vacation for a couple days before looking for work.

So after I had got a room for three dollars a week I set out assiduously to discover how it felt to live a life of leisure in a strange, big, and brilliant city. The enormous buildings, rich resplendent stores, the magnificent, luxurious hotels of the downtown section, were an unending spectacle of delight to my awe-struck senses. Hour after hour I would wander around the streets, going from one hotel lobby to another, writing glowing letters to René on the Hollenden's stationery, visiting and revisiting department stores. Then, feeling a desire to rest my legs and travel in elegance for a while, I would ride out to one of the parks and back again on one of the big streetcars, which charged the princely fare of three whole cents. In the evening I haunted the moving picture shows, poring over the billboard posters of William 

Anderson and Florence Turner, John Bunny and Clara

Kimball Young. Before a week was up I had spent nearly ten dollars on meals and a "good time." I abruptly realized 185

that I had better try to find a job. The next day, I decided, I would look for one.

But there were apparently no jobs of any magnificence in Cleveland that September for inexperienced country boys from New York State. If there were I didn't know how to find them. At the end of another week I was washing dishes in a Waldorf Lunch for a dollar a day and meals.

Cleveland had gradually become very sordid and forbidding. I wanted to leave it. 

Remembering a place called the "Seamen's Union" of which the quartermaster had told me before I left the Crocus, I went down there in search of a job. At the address given I ascended a long flight of stairs to a shabby office on the second floor. The room was crowded with sullen, weather-beaten men. I had come there conspicuously arrayed in white flannel trousers, a brand-new starched collar and a showy straw hat. As I walked up to the desk their conversation virtually ceased and too late did I realize my ludicrous appearance. The man at the desk smiled at me derisively. There were hardly any jobs, he told me, even for experienced sailors, to say nothing of school boys who hadn't yet learned to swim.

I walked out of the place burning with shame and disappointment. I had counted so much on getting a job which would take me back East. Down on the street I was suddenly overwhelmed with the sense of my unimportance. 

There was no one now to whom I could turn for either help or encouragement. A word of friendly conversation, just the smell of Mother's kitchen, or a glimpse of a familiar street-corner in Ogdensburg, would have delighted me so that I would have burst into tears. "God," I said to myself, "what a fool I was ever to leave home! Now, I guess I'll 186

have to wash dishes in a restaurant until I save enough money to pay my way back . . . and at two dollars a week that'll take me darn near till Christmas." I walked along in a melancholy daze, alternately bedeviling and pitying myself.

Back at the Waldorf a new fellow was working in the kitchen-young, red-headed and jolly; I was eagerly drawn into his congenial conversation. We exchanged confidences concerning our adventures and our backgrounds. Learning that he came from Syracuse, New York, I immediately felt as attached to him as I would have to a relative. He was twenty years old and had left home at seventeen "to escape"-as he put it-"the tyranny of his father." Since then he had been working and traveling from place to place -San Francisco, Chicago, New Orleans, and even Mexico.

Except in the winter he had never stayed longer on any one job than to earn sufficient money to travel a little farther. "Not," so he explained, "that I ever donate any of my cash to the railroads. But I save till I get enough to go where I want to, then I blow it all in, having a grand and glorious time. . . . Then I hop a freight and get there just the same! I got in town yesterday from Fort Wayne, Indiana. I'm headed now for Erie, Pennsylvania, 'cause I heard the town was booming." "Erie?" I exclaimed. "Why, my father is working in 

Erie! Gee, but I wish I could go along with you!" "Come along, 'bo! We ought to make it in a day.” "How can I do it?" I asked. "I got only two dollars to my name." "Boy, you're rich! I got a quarter, and I can make it! 

Did you never ride the rods?" "No," I answered, "but I'm willing to try it." 187

My new acquaintance's name was Tim O'Connor. Upon his advice I had spent most of my remaining two dollars in shipping my suitcase to Erie-addressing it to myself, general delivery, not knowing my father's address. Innocently I had changed into a light gray suit and, having no other, I wore my straw hat. Late that evening, after finishing our tour of duty at the Waldorf Lunch, and having eaten as much as we could possibly hold, we took a streetcar to the eastern suburbs of the city. Thence to the New York 

Central railroad tracks! Eastward into the cloudy night!

It was at least two hours before a freight came along, going slowly enough for us to board it. But luckily the one we caught had several empty box-cars and was moving so moderately that we were able to push open the door of one of them and crawl in. Once inside we discovered the luxury of excelsior.

Despite all the excitement I was finally able to go to sleep. But the fears which I had thus far suppressed had their innings in my uneasy dreams. 

Whang! bang! crash! The freight had come to a sudden halt. Inside the car the darkness was heavy. A noisy tattoo on the roof told us that it was raining torrents outside. 

Slowly we felt our way around the wall and pushed open the door. A few dim lights were just visible ahead of us, and through the rain we heard a distant shouting. Tim listened intently for a moment, squeezing my arm. "I don't know where the hell we are," he said, "but I guess it's a yard and we'd better beat it. Those lights might be bulls, coming down here to look in these empties. . . . 

I'm taking no chances. . . . Let's go!"

He hopped down to the ground; but I, alarmed at what 188

the rain would do to my suit and hat, stood in the door of the car hesitating. "It's raining pitchforks," I protested. "Do you think we really have to get out in it?" 

Tim snorted. "Rain? Boy!" he rasped. "Rain's a damn sight better than spending thirty days in the hoosegow! 

Come on! We'll head for that strongest light up the track . . . it may be a station."

It was a station, and under its wide overhanging roof we found protection from the rain-but not until we had both got thoroughly soaked. Standing there in the darkness, shivering with cold, we saw the telltale flashlights making their way up and down the length of the train. 

Presently one of them stopped and there was a clamor of violent shouts. Hurriedly the lights congregated and in a few moments they were all making their way toward the station. “The bulls, all right!" muttered Tim. "And they got their prey, so now we don't have to worry. I pity those poor devils, though. But they should have known better. 

Come on!"

We started back toward the train, the pitch-darkness forcing us virtually to feel our way. Before we could reach any of the empty box-cars the engine gave a short, staccato whistle and, with a raucous thunder of ear-splitting puffs, started on its way. Now it was as obviously impossible to crawl inside of an empty as it was nearly impossible even to see the train. In desperation we had to clutch at the iron rods at the end of one of the cars, and finally crawl to the top. There, back to back, we sat through the remainder of the night. The rain kept up a heavy downpour and the engine steadily befogged us with its burning, choking smoke. I continued to wear my straw hat. 

Just a little before dawn the rain stopped. The sun came 189

up with a promising warm brilliance. I have never since welcomed it with more eagerness or gratitude. It was like the visit of a friend when one is spending his fifth or sixth month in the ward of a charity hospital-before the cold, wet, indifference of fair-weather companions has been taken for granted, or one has steeled himself to the reality of being forgotten.

In the middle of the forenoon the train slowly rattled and banged into the freight-yard at Erie. Jubilantly we swung off and headed for a restaurant. For the moment it seemed as though Paradise could offer nothing more wonderful than bright September sun and ham and eggs with coffee. The lowly lunch-car in which we ate became a veritable heaven of hospitality and delight. When we had finished eating I could have very willingly lain down on its greasy counter and slept for countless, peaceful hours, without even a worry about my soot-covered gray suit, or a single misgiving over my poor straw hat. I had been so proud of that hat. It had been the bulwark of confidence and the hallmark of my urbanity during those few days of magnificence upon my entrance into Cleveland.

I encountered Father in the most coincidental fashion. 

After leaving Tim while he looked around for a job, I went to the post-office to get my suitcase. There, to my great surprise, I met Father coming out of the doorway. It was just a little after twelve o'clock. He had come down for his mail at the general delivery window. 

If I was surprised he must have been thunderstruck, for he looked squarely at me and failed to recognize me. When 

I finally spoke, a look of astonishment came into his face sufficient to betray his lack of faith in modern miracles. His eyes, his mouth and his Adam's apple were all agape with 190

unutterable amazement. It took him nearly a minute to partially recover himself. "For heaven's sakes, Peter! What-what in the name of mercy have you been doing? Where in the world have you been?"

It was then I fully realized how very bad I must look. 

When I had first seen Father a very strange feeling had come over me. My throat had begun to tighten with a flood of sudden emotion. I perceived for the first time during all my years of adolescence that despite all resentment I loved him deeply. As I stood there on the post-office steps 

I looked upon him more welcomely than I ever had done before. So now, at his almost forbidding greeting, becoming suddenly upset and very self-conscious, I answered him facetiously in an attempt at retaliation. "I just rained down," 

I said, trying very hard to force a smile on my face, though 

I felt much more like crying. "Rained down!" he shouted angrily. "I guess you did, all right-and in a cloud of coal dust! How in heaven's name did you ever get in that condition? I'm ashamed of you! 

Let's get out of here quickly, for I don't want anybody to see me talking to such a looking person on the street." 

He motioned me to come with him, and we hurriedly left the vicinity of the post-office.

Several hours later, after having smuggled me into the bathroom of his boarding-house and leaving me in a hurry in order to get back to work, he returned to discover me asleep in the bathtub. That was more than an already outraged father could possibly stand. Seizing his razor strap he began to swing it viciously at my wet and unclothed body. "I'll teach you, my lad," he cried, "to try and disgrace your father!" 191

"But, Father!" I protested, while struggling to get hold of the strap. "I just came down here to try to get a job! 

I rode on a freight-train," I went on to explain (meanwhile successfully disarming him physically), “simply because I didn't have any money." "It's a prison offense," he shouted, "and I've a good notion to have them put you in jail for it!"

His anger had increased at my refusal to let him go on striking me. But loath to lose entirely his position as paternal dictator, he stepped away from the bathtub and, fixing his fiercest glance upon me, commanded that I be dressed in five minutes and present in his room. "There," as he expressed it, “I'll tend to you! . . . And don't you forget it!" 

But the storm blew over. And when I came to him he was apparently a changed man. The next day he got me a job on the building where he was working and before two weeks were up he was showing in many ways—as we walked out in the evening together to a movie or to church -that he was proud of me. "It's a shame," he said once, "that you're such a wild and foolish boy, for you have everything else in your favor. . . . If you'd only settle down and make a Christian man out of yourself." 

Shortly after that remark (which was more of a sigh or a prayer than a very optimistic hope) a circumstance came about which must have made him think momentarily that his prayer was going to be answered. One morning his newspaper carried the announcement that the great Billy Sunday was coming to town. Father's eyes lighted up with a glow of anticipation. "There, now," he said, as we walked to work that morning, "we shall have something in Erie that will make the agents of the Devil take counsel with themselves. And this city will be a cleaner, better place in a few weeks, or I miss my guess." 192

By the time the evangelist arrived my curiosity had been aroused by Father's enthusiasm. Starting out early the first night, so as to be sure to get seats in the huge tabernacle, I felt somewhat the same sensation I had experienced years earlier in attending my first circus. 

Outside the doors we joined a mad throng, all curious and eager to see the great miracle-worker. Inside, the tabernacle was decorated gaily with American flags and bunting. 

The night was unusually warm. The resinous smell of the sawdust mingled with the odor of perspiring humanity. 

An immense choir made up of the best-looking church girls in town, all chastely dressed in white, impressively occupied a conspicuous place on the platform. A blaring, blatting conglomeration of brass instruments, slightly out of tune in the manner of an augmented Salvation Army band, began to play the first hymn: "Throw Out the Life Line.”

The huge crowd of nearly eight thousand people lifted their voices in an orgy of shrieking, wailing, howling, bellowing, roaring, moaning, and singing. The net effect was undeniably impressive.

When the congregation had finally sat down the great evangelist entered and walked dramatically to the front center of the platform. Raising his arm grandiloquently, he started to pray: asking the assistance of God in saving "the many sinners in this devil-ridden sink of iniquity. . . . And help us, O God," he prayed, "to close up all the saloons and the dives and the sneaking Sunday theaters . . . and to open up the hearts and the pockets of these people in the great work of making this campaign a huge success." Some more hymns were sung, hymnbooks were offered for sale at a dollar apiece, and then the incomparable Billy Sunday commenced his sermon. 

At first his loud voice maintained a fairly even, moderate 193

tempo. Gradually growing in rapidity and fire, to the accompaniment of weird contortions and wild gestures, it became hysterical, maniacal in pitch and pace. Seizing a chair he smashed it into kindling wood as a gesture of emphasis to the fact that all there assembled were bound straight to hell if they did not give their hearts to Jesus that very night.
