Off came his coat and vest! Collar! Tie! And finally, his shirt! With bare arms swinging wildly he hopped upon the top of the pulpit! With a frenzy untamed by his precarious position he called down anathema upon the brewery owners, the brothel proprietors and the saloonkeepers, "those foul mouthed, lanterned-jawed sons of iniquity who are dragging this fair city down into the very depths of Hell!"

Down off the pulpit he leaped, fists clenched; and dashing furiously to the very edge of the platform he shrieked like a madman: “Will you people stand by and calmly watch your homes, your children, your dear ones, swept by a conflagration worse than fire? Will you? WILL YOU? 

Now, now is the time to stop it! . . . Give your heart to 

Jesus!... Come with me now!" ...

The motley collection of brass instruments began softly to bray: "Jesus, Saviour, Pilot Me." "Hit the sawdust trail!" he pleaded. "Come forward! 

Come forward, and acknowledge your sins! Lay your hearts bare before the Saviour!" Then one final threat: "If you don't, I tell you, you're on the road to Hell: burning, agonizing, torturing Hell! . . . And if you don't believe it, I'll prove it to you!"

Crowds of dull-witted people started for the altar. Father turned to look at me triumphantly. But seeing written on my face more curiosity than penitence, his expression 194

changed to a sad sort of hopefulness. "Are you going up, son?" he asked, pleadingly.

I had been very impressed and had felt considerably moved. My heart had ached and my stomach had been faint. But only during those moments when the evangelist's voice had been sad and plaintive. In the midst of his threats and violent denunciations my mood had changed to an intense defiance. And now at the end of the sermon my defiance had won over my other feelings. "No!" I retorted. "There are too many sheep up there already." 

He eyed me angrily; then, remembering his Christian duty, covered his anger with a sickly smile. “You simply can't get rid of the Devil, can you, son?" he sneered. "But whether you can or not," he went on, with his voice quivering with rage, "I want to tell you one thing: don't you dare blaspheme any more in my presence!" Then, leaving me in my seat, he went up to the altar to be "saved."

After this episode at the tabernacle the atmosphere of congeniality and cooperation which had existed between us for nearly two weeks vanished; and a quarrelsome, grating, suspicious dissension took its place. Each night Father would go to the meetings alone, after having hinted, and asked, and threatened, in his efforts to get me to join him. 

In the beginning I excused myself with a variety of disgusting lies, but at length I flared up with frank and wounding refusals. But these did not deter him upon his return from rehearsing for my benefit tiresome and irritating accounts of the sermons. 

One night in high dudgeon I decided that I'd been a fool and a weakling to ever come to Erie: that in so doing I had defeated the very object of my exodus from home. The 195

only thing to do now was to leave as soon as possible. The facts: that I had a few dollars in my pocket and would be getting eighteen more on Saturday, and that René had written me that she was leaving Ogdensburg for Syracuse, where she had just succeeded in getting a better job, no doubt influenced me.

I was painfully eager to see René again. Instead of diminishing, the memory of her embraces had seemed to grow more acute during the two months which I had been away from her. Such period of time might be rather short for that vague and indefinite task of making a man out of oneself. I felt very uncertain as to whether anything along that line had been accomplished one way or the other, notwithstanding the very consciously adventurous life I had been living. The time seemed abundantly long enough to assure me of her welcome.

So, remembering-very conveniently-my resolution to follow my impulses and do the things that I wanted to do without worrying about the consequences to myself, I determined that not only would I leave Erie, but I would go to Syracuse despite all the protests of my father. And just as a person, feeling himself unjustly admonished will, in a fit of pique, "be damned" if he doesn't give his reprover something to "really" complain about, I swore to my father that I was parting from his company and that I never expected to see him again until I was twenty-one and married.

But while packing my grip I had all I could do to keep back my tears. 196

CHAPTER XV 

BUT if at sixteen I thought that I was through with my father until such time as I should become a man-or at least attain to the legal age thereof-I reckoned very little with life's vicissitudes, and not at all with its capriciousness. 

Indeed, after I had been in Syracuse for a year, living happily with René, working in a factory for $10.00 a week, playing semi-professional baseball, one of those lamentable accidents often called blessings-overtook me; and suddenly disabusing my confidence of its naive independence, sent me timorously scurrying homeward for paternal assistance. René had become pregnant! So we must get married; and being only seventeen I needed my parents' consent.

I would have to go home to Father to ask for it. 

Some instinct told me (or perhaps it was the accumulation of another year's experience) that on such a mission, humility and contriteness were far more effective than defiance. So I went to Father and very humbly asked him for his aid and consent.

On this occasion, I am sure, he did not first think of his Christian duty but greeted me warmly from the bottom of his heart. After having accepted me, he very happily bethought himself of the Prodigal Son. "Peter," he said, when he had become fully aware of my predicament, "bring the girl home and let her receive our blessing. You can be married here at the house by the Methodist minister and start your married life off in Christian surroundings. It's a shame that what has happened has happened, but in the 197

end it may be the best thing for you. It may make you settle down and realize the responsibilities of life. But," and then he heaved a long and woeful sigh, "... you're only seventeen ... seventeen years old! And starting off life with a wife and a child! O Peter! Peter! when will you give up your wickedness, forsake the Devil and let the 

Church save you from this awful way you're going!"

Silently, reverently, I had listened, hanging my head in a mood of self-reproach. For once again terrible uneasiness had come over me. Was I being punished, after all, for the sins I had committed, for my lack of faith and failure to attend church? I hadn't for a moment thought that there was no God, but I had hardly believed that He punished one on earth. Even now I couldn't be sure, for an instinct quickened within me to warn me against such a belief. The question remained unanswered, and was soon forgotten, returning to annoy me later when I had got into other difficulties.

That was the way with my religious life. When in hot water I regretted my neglect of it, but when the water had cooled nothing seemed more arduous and useless than the pious confinements of the "straight and narrow path." Not only arduous, but manifestly impossible; more than useless, they were positively absurd.

So after René and I were married, and had made a honeymoon trip out to South Park and back on the streetcar, we had comfortably settled in Rochester (where Father had moved the family that year notwithstanding the warnings of Mathew and the outspoken protests of his wife). 

The consolations of the Church and the assurances of paternal authority ceased for the time being to have any attractiveness. I had got a job in the Eastman Kodak factory which paid me sixteen dollars a week, and we had 198

secured a comfortable furnished room with light housekeeping privileges for twenty-four dollars a month. On the weekly balance of ten dollars we managed to “get along.”"

I knew nothing at that time about obstetricians or accouchements. But whatever the expenses to be entailed at the advent of a baby might be, I was sure I could meet them by the time they arrived. According to calculations they were due in May, and a full month before that time-I was confident-I would be playing baseball and adding to my wages.

Meanwhile it was fall and Rochester was beautiful. The gorgeous colors of the leaves along the tree-lined streets even concealed the ugliness of the squalid houses. The famous parks were abloom with flowers, yellow chrysanthemums and wine-red dahlias, golden zinnias and varicolored asters. Markets were overflowing with fruits and produce. The air was redolent with the homey odors of canning tomatoes and spices boiling for appetizing ketchup, peaches stewing for delicious preserves.

The season had opened at the Lyceum Theater and Mathew, playing in the orchestra, invited me to sit in a corner of the pit one night to see a show. René was not invited.

The inside of a legitimate theater had been up to then a mysterious region wholly unexplored by me. In Syracuse 

René and I had never ventured into them because we had considered them far too expensive. So I looked forward to the experience with tremendous excitement. 

The play happened to be The Round-Up with Maclyn Arbuckle. What an impression the whole evening made upon me! The melodrama seemed dazzling! Breathtakingly wonderful! My heart pounded madly all the way through it. And the theater itself, with its fascinating sug- 199

gestions of a life incredibly charming and irresistibly lovely, captivated me completely. I had to walk across the stage to get to the orchestra pit, and the excitement I felt during those moments is hardly describable. The magic enticement of make-up and scenery! The subtle lure of that backstage odor!-perfumes and powders mingling with the fireproof sizing; and, in the case of The Round-Up, the smell of horses. 

And besides the actors and actresses, who intentionally transported me into a world of make-believe, there were the people out front, who, no less effectively, through no conscious effort I am sure, pictured for my delight a world hardly more real or familiar than that which I saw on the stage. Ladies in evening gowns, the equal of which I had never before seen in real life! Women with beautiful coiffures, and vivid complexions, and soft and elegant accents-a world so different from the drab and pedestrian atmosphere which I had known all my life, that it seemed scarcely to be subject to the same natural laws. From the dark corner of the orchestra pit I looked out upon them, enviously, wonderingly. . . trying to imagine any of the persons whom I had ever known fitting comfortably into such splendor and brilliancy. Doria?—perhaps! Mathew? 

No! Finally?—no one! It was a world set apart from that which I knew, not only in riches, but in culture and intelligence. A world, unconquerable by ordinary mortals, where conversation was so scintillating, tastes so exquisite, intelligence so subtle, that any one whom I knew would be both speechless and lost in it. 

Not until many years later did I meet some of the people from that world. And then-after several courses in skepticism and disillusionment-the revelation that they, after all, were most ordinary creatures, superior in nothing save in 200

the glamour created by my imagination, distressed me more poignantly than any previous disenchantment. I looked upon those representatives of the world which I had almost wholly imagined, as mountebanks twice guilty, for not only had they not lived the recondite lives which I had ascribed to them, but they had not even dreamed of them. 

After the theater Mathew took me to the Odenbach restaurant, and there over our glasses of beer we talked of my "future." Or rather, he talked. I listened-listened with varying feelings of respect and fear and awe and a little resentment at this strange brother of mine who since the years I had lived with him in Potsdam had grown into a person whom I hardly knew. Gently at first he hinted at my shortcomings; then seeming to grow angered at the reminder of their multiplicity he upbraided me very frankly about my ignorance and incorrigibility. “Look at yourself now!" he commanded. "Just look at the mess you are in!

Married at seventeen and working in a factory! No education! No training of any sort . . . and precious little chance now of ever improving yourself! What do you suppose is ever going to happen to you? Married, and going to have a baby, when you're hardly able to support yourself! Do you ever stop to think about those things, or don't you even give a damn? My God, boy, if you don't think, or don't do something pretty soon, where are you going to be? Working in a factory and raising a family! You're a disgrace, a disgrace! That's what you are! You haven't any backbone, and not an ounce of pride!"

I stared into the dark-brown depths of my beer and struggled to find an answer to his tirade. But all was confusion and bitterness-the bitterness of beer before it has become palatable-the confusion of life when youth first tastes its bitterness-the bitterness of life when one's im- 201

pulses fall athwart the patterns of respectability-the disappointment and confusion when the brother to whom one looks for encouragement and help replies with harsh criticism and annihilating predictions. Everything around me seemed suddenly to disappear into the vast penumbra of vague, unfigured things. 

But out of the bewilderment some words finally came, quickened by an instinct subtle and hostile, fostered by an antagonism to the pattern of Mathew's life, rooted in a resentment towards an older brother's vaunting success. "Whatever happens to me," I retaliated, “I'm willing to take the consequences." "You'll take the consequences!" he snorted, sarcastically. "You'll take them? What do you mean? You haven't even learned to take care of yourself yet! You've run away from everything you've come up against-school, the job in Ogdensburg, the job in Erie, the job in Syracuse! And now you come to Rochester and get married. and Father gets you a position at the Eastman Kodak ... and the 

Lord only knows how long you'll keep that! Until you get tired of it, I suppose! And then you'll come to me and want me to help you! But, boy, you got another think coming if you think I'm going to have anything to do with you.

From now on you're going to get from me just a good letting alone!"

Mathew got up from the table and signaled for the check. His cheeks and eyes were flaming with anger, and he glared at me much as he would have at a skunk. 

The joys of earlier in the evening had long ago collapsed -the beauty and excitement of the theater, and the elation I had felt there; the warm glow of happiness which his invitation had given me, the great expectations I had had of his friendship! All gone, all of the encouragement which 202

his cruel subterfuge had first given me. But the image of that evening had passed into my soul forever. 

René had never seemed more wonderful than she was that night, when I came back to our little furnished room, where, uninvited but unprotesting, she had waited so hopefully, so expectantly, for me to return with glowing tales of Mathew's blessings and encouragement. I threw myself on my knees before her and thrust my head into her lap, trying—and succeeding-in keeping the tears from falling. 

She asked no questions, but she knew what had happened. "You poor kid," she soothed, pressing my head tightly to her body. "Never mind, never mind; they're just trying to break you!... But they can't do that, can they?...

They don't understand you and they're trying to make you over.... But you'll show 'em! You'll show 'em that you're 

Peter Holland and not anybody else." 203

XVI So I won a measure of independence from the limitations. of Mathew's ideas of what a young man ought to do and be by depending on René. Winter came on and we kept very much to ourselves. At Christmas Johnny brought over a bookrack which he had made in manual training class in high school. Mother followed in the afternoon with little 

Jenny, carrying cakes and cookies, a knitted jacket for René, clothes for the expected baby, and, as always and forever, her warm, radiant hominess.

Father's wages had been cut to twenty-one dollars a week, hardly enough money for the family to exist on. 

Ruth was working at Eastman's and getting six dollars a week. And Johnny, in order to earn a little money, was arising at four o'clock every morning to deliver newspapers. 

In the midst of their various struggles they tried valiantly to make a merry Christmas. As for Mathew, he had become a serious apostle of the modern idea. To him the celebration of Christmas was "just a lot of poppycock," and he considered it the sober duty of his newly acquired atheism to discourage it. To be sure, the theater had given him a turkey and he had accepted it, but they weren't even going to prepare it for Christmas day. Too much bother, what with the matinée and everything-they'd wait until 

Sunday, when they had more time.

The New Year ushered in a "slight economic depression."

At the Eastman Kodak factories men were being laid 205

off every day. Wages were sinking, and the winter growing more severe. I lost my job.

Out of sixteen dollars a week we had been able to save nine dollars. After fifteen days of unemployment we had one dollar left. My efforts to get work had all been in vain. 

One morning I left the house with five cents carfare in my pocket. Our balance of twenty cents René retained for food. Late that afternoon I pawned my five-dollar watch for fifty cents and hurriedly returned to the room for a lunch of graham crackers and milk. Much to my disappointment

René was not home, but I expected that she would return at any minute. A whole hour passed and still no sign of 

René. I went outside again into the cold gray street, and walked up and down, looking for a sight of her, meanwhile trying to stifle a sickening faintness in my stomach. 

The old fly-specked clock in the grocery-store which I could see through the little aperture in the frost-covered window as I passed back and forth in front of it, finally registered ten after six. I had at last made a decision. I would go and ask Mathew for help! I must. The situation demanded it. Our money and food were virtually gone. 

Damn it! ... I didn't mind starving (but of course I knew

I wouldn't I knew I could get along some way), but how about René? That was a different thing. Pride and independence and everything else could go to hell! She had to be taken care of. Father couldn't help; so Mathew would simply have to. . . . ...

I started up the street with my head buried in the collar of my overcoat. The air was damp and cold and seemed to penetrate everywhere. But I determined to walk to Mathew's flat just to impress him with the fact that we had no money even for carfare. Then if he loaned me some, I would take a streetcar and be back at the room in an hour. God! if 206

he would really loan me some money! We could have a hot supper, some soup and some beef stew! It would 

· · seem like a banquet . . . and René would be so jubilant!

Why, she could make a festive occasion out of a weak cup of tea!

The chill wind swept around the corner with additional fierceness. I lunged forward blindly to take it "head-on.” A streetcar had stopped to let off some passengers, and now the wheels were shuddering and grinding against the cold steel rail as it started on its way. A faint voice was calling in the midst of the noise. "Petey! Petey!"

I finally heard it, and turned to spy René, hurrying to catch up with me. "Where are you going, dear?" she cried, as she came up to me.

I hated to tell her before my mission was completed. But now that she had seen me I knew that I must. "I'm going over to Mathew's to borrow some money," I said, as calmly as I could, for I wanted her to feel that it was a perfectly simple and natural thing to do.

The smile with which she had greeted me changed to a look of disappointment. “Oh, please don't," she cried. 

And then, as though suddenly remembering something pleasant which she had momentarily forgotten, her smile returned. "Dear!" she exclaimed eagerly. "Wait till I tell you! . . . You won't have to go!" "Why not?" I retorted. "Of course I've got to! We can't just starve." "Well, we won't have to do that either," she eagerly assured me. "But let's not talk here. Come on back to the room and I'll tell you all about it."

René had got a job!-a job in a shoe factory, at seven 207

dollars a week! And, as she put it, “I vamped the foreman into paying me daily until you could get work. Wasn't he sweet? Look! He gave me a whole dollar just for working a little while this afternoon!" 

That night I pondered the fruits of my impulsiveness:

René, pregnant and working in a shoe factory! Me, out of a job and depending upon her for food! And nothing, nothing apparently, that I could do to help it! Oh, God! I must have been born wrong, I thought. Nobody else could possibly cause so much misery when they tried to do the opposite. I didn't want to hurt anybody, or cause any one to suffer-Father or Mathew or God or anybody else! . . . least of all René! And see what had happened. I just couldn't do right! . . . that was the whole trouble! There was just something in me that made me cause evil. . . make people unhappy! I was being punished . . . God was punishing me! ... O God, O God! help me to do right! 

I'll do anything You ask me. . . I'll work night and day. . . . I'll go to church . . . I'll give my life. . . . ... 

Ah, that was an idea, to commit suicide! . . . and then I couldn't cause anybody any more trouble! But what would 

René do? Damn it, I can't leave her! I couldn't! I wouldn't! ... I'll do something for her! I'll do it, no matter what it takes!... I'll steal! By Jesus, I'll rob some of these rich bastards that ride around in big automobiles. I'll do something! . . . I'm not licked yet! . . . I'll show 'em! O God, help me!-if there is a God-I don't want to do anything bad. ... But I got to help René, no matter what happens. ...

René snuggled closer to me in bed, and when I moved my arm to put it around her she realized that I was awake. "Oh, Petey, did I wake you?" she whispered huskily. 208

"No, I been awake." "What's the matter, sweetheart? Can't you go to sleep? 

Are you worrying?" 

I was silent. "What are you worrying about... hope not about me.

You know, I'm going to be much better off doing something. I never did like to sit around here all day, just waiting for you. I'm so glad . . . I wish I'd done it sooner."

She withdrew her hand from beneath the bedclothes and gently began to smooth my forehead. “Go to sleep, darling; everything's going to be swell . . . nothing to worry about ... go to sleep."

The next morning we were up before seven o'clock. Outside it seemed darker than the middle of the night; inside the gaslight hissed its hideous death-like breath into the gray and ghastly hour-the hour when even the least of humanity deserves the refuge of sleep's forgetfulness—the dismal hour before dawn of a winter morning in the city. 

René had to be to work at eight. We left the house together. 

First there was the newspaper want ads to be gone overthose columns with their perennial and unspeakable annoying "come-ons" for salesmen and agents. . . . Eastman 

Kodak wants an experienced chemist, preferably a research worker. carpenters, bricklayers, plasterers long job, three dollars a day strong man, not afraid of work, handle oil barrels, Vacuum Oil Company. 

Ah, there! there's a job I might get. ... ... "Well," said the superintendent, "you're the huskiestlooking fellow that's been around this morning. I think we might give you the job. Ten dollars a week. Pretty good wages for these times, too, I'll tell you.. But what made you so late in getting around? You nearly missed out. We 209 ...

start here at seven, you know. Can you be to work this afternoon? You'll need overalls or old clothes." 

I have a job! Boy! Oh, boy! How different the world looks! It's always darkest before the dawn, all right! But what a dawn! More glorious because the darkness had been so intense, as victory is sweetest after the severest strife.

The streetcar could hardly carry me home fast enough. I had decided to go there first, change my clothes, get my overalls, eat a little lunch and then be at the shoe factory promptly at twelve to break the news to René. From there 

I could get to the oil company's warehouse by one o'clock easily.

On the table in the hall in the rooming-house, I found a package addressed to me in Mathew's handwriting. Rushing up the stairs, I waited until I got into the room to open it, meanwhile wondering excitedly what it could possibly be. And when the wrappers were finally off I stared perplexedly at a large book and a little paper folder. The book's title was, "The Power of Success," and its author's name was Haddock. The folder was entitled "Forging Ahead,” and appeared to be an advertisement for a correspondence school.

From seven in the morning until six at night I pushed, rolled, lifted, heaved, and handled heavy barrels of oil in a cold, dank warehouse. At first my muscles ached during the night, and the days seemed weary and never to end. The aches I soon got over, but the weariness became more aggravating. Yet the evenings with René compensated for everything. At the end of a month she gave up her job. 

And once more I looked toward the future with a certain satisfaction, not quite so naive, but still youthfully confident. 210

It had been arranged that Mother should nurse René through her confinement. And as soon as the event became imminent we were to get larger quarters-two rooms probably, or at least a room and an alcove. I had already talked with some of the managers of the semi-professional baseball teams and they had promised me weekly engagements if I could make good, so I had no doubt that we would be able to afford two rooms easily. 

One of the "baseball men" to whom I spoke, Bill O'Donnell, asked me where I was working, and when I told him he seemed very surprised. "You can do better than that," he assured me. And handing me a card with the name and address of a large trucking company on it he said: “Here, take this down there whenever you get the chance, and tell the manager I sent you." A week went by before I was able to see the man.

I took the card over one noon hour and got a job on one of the carting company's trucks. Nothing was said about wages, but at the end of a week's work I discovered eighteen dollars in my pay envelope. 

It was the last Saturday in March and unseasonably mild. 

Nature seemed to be genially compensating for an extraordinarily severe winter, and the memory of the dark cold days which had so nearly overwhelmed us grew dimmer and dimmer in the happiness and good fortune which at last seemed to be ours. Full of the light-heartedness that comes from the release of a long oppression we gaily trotted downtown and bought René a new dress to more suitably clothe her swelling figure. And just as life always becomes colored with a new and unbelievable fascination after a 211

victory won, that day seemed to be the omen of a spring which would be unprecedently beautiful. We celebrated it by taking chickens, ham and eggs, and flowers over to Mother's for an Easter dinner. In the evening René played the piano and we all sang hymns. That is, all except Johnny, who having just recently started to study 'cello, contributed in lieu of his voice a struggling tenor on his instrument.

Every one was so happy that it seemed like old times-those wondrous, distant happy old times in Potsdam, when the Sunday School orchestra used to rehearse in our parlor. 

We stayed all night and went to church in the morning— Mother beaming with joy at having so many of her family around her again, all sitting together in the church pew on a glorious Easter Sunday. Poverty might threaten, but her children were well and most of them were near her. She asked no more than that to make her happiness complete. 

April came, and with it a baseball tryout. Meanwhile I had been gently "breaking my arm in" during noon hours at work. My muscles felt like steel and it seemed to me as though I had never had better control. At the try-out Mr. 

O'Donnell appeared to be very favorably impressed. He asked me to report again on the following Saturday. When

Saturday arrived he took me out to the International League baseball park, and there, to my amazement, asked me to pitch for some of the batting practice of the Rochester team.

Bewildered and trembling I somehow managed to change to my baseball shoes, slip on a jersey which he handed me and walk out to the pitcher's box. He's got a lot of nerve, I protested to myself, asking me to pitch to these professional players, just to try out for a little semi-professional, home team . . . why, some of these fellows have been in the big leagues! And the Rochester team won the International 

League pennant last year. It isn't fair. . . and if he hadn't 212

been so good to me before I'd almost believe he was trying to make a fool out of me. 

But faced with the batters my mind concentrated on baseball, and I suddenly became filled with a new eagerness -a tremendous curiosity to see what I could do against veteran players. I started off throwing balls which I knew they could easily hit, and then began to mix in curves which according to my hopes and intentions they ought not to be able to hit. No one was more surprised than I was when many of the batters swung and missed. More batters came out and still I was successful-not always, to be sure, but in a fair number of cases.

Presently Mr. O'Donnell called me over to him and introduced me to a Mr. Hanzel, who, he explained, was one of the Rochester coaches. Mr. Hanzel asked me if I would mind "throwing a few" to him. I eagerly accepted. A little while later he sent me out to the pitcher's box again, this time to pitch to one of the regular Rochester catchers, whose simple signals in regard to the kind of balls I should throw he asked me to follow. Five men came to bat and I struck out three of them; the other two hit balls that would have been easy put-outs.

Again Mr. O'Donnell motioned for me to come over to him. "Say, boy," he said, when I had reached his side, “you got some pretty good stuff there if you want to make use of it. How'd you like to come out here Monday morning and stay for a few days? The Hustlers are opening their season

Monday and Mr. Hanzel would like to see some more of you. .. I'll speak to Sam down at the trucking company and he'll let you off. There might be something for you out here, if you'd like to give it a try. Hanzel likes you, and he knows pitchers. What do you say?" ... 

I was struck dumb with amazement. "What what ... 213

... what do you mean?" I finally managed to stammer, wondering if it could really be possible that he meant for me to practice with the Hustlers. 

He was amused at my surprise. "Why, boy, I mean that

Hanzel wants to look you over some more . . . to see how good you are . . . and let the manager see you in action.

He thinks in another year, if you get plenty of practice, you might make the Hustler team. Now, how do you like that?" 

I couldn't get out of that baseball park fast enough. I ran for the streetcar and raced when I got off it-up two flights of stairs and into René's arms. I squeezed her, lifted her up on my shoulders and carried her around the room; meanwhile conveying to her between excited cries that I might have a chance to play on the Rochester baseball team. 

That night we indulged in another celebration: ate at a restaurant and had glasses of wine. The most difficult thing 

I did throughout the whole evening was to keep myself from going to a telephone and calling up Mathew. Exuberant with joy, I felt that I must tell everybody of my good luck. But René calmed me down and persuaded me to wait at least until Monday.

By the middle of the next week I had signed a contract with the Hustlers. Not for a full season, but up till the middle of June! And for playing baseball! Just for playing the game that I loved I was to receive the incredible fortune of forty-five dollars a week! I did not tell Mathew, but 

René and I rushed immediately with the news to Mother. 

The look on her face I shall never forget.

Since I would be out of town a great deal with the team and since the last few weeks of pregnancy would be precarious ones to spend alone, René went to live with Mother. 

When I returned to Rochester after my first trip, we went 214

on an orgy of shopping, buying more things, it seemed, than we had previously purchased in our whole lives: babies' clothing, a carriage, and perfume, and a hot-water bottle, new clothes for ourselves, and a tablecloth for Mother, a tennis racket for Johnny, and a new vase for the piano in the parlor-candy, peanuts, fruit, and some new music.

While I had been away Mathew had heard the news and now he had come down to see me. Full of advice and half beaming with restrained smiles he expressed the hope that I had not taken all that he had said last fall too seriously. Had I read “The Power of Will," he wanted to know. Had I made any plans about saving money? Had 

I thought of any business that I would like to go into during the winter months when I wouldn't be playing baseball?

To all of his questions I could only answer No, but it pleased me tremendously to realize that he was at least interested. I forgave him in my heart for all that he had said; indeed, I even was willing to think that there was nothing to forgive. And when he invited René and me to have dinner with him the next night down at the lake, explaining that he would hire a car and take us down so that René could make the trip more easily, I was overcome with sadness at the thought that I had ever displeased him. 3 I hadn't yet had a chance to pitch a regular game with the Hustlers. Relief work in two exhibition contests had thus far been my only experience "under fire.” In those two games, against easy opposition, I had done very well. Now 

I was eager and yet a little fearful-to get a chance in a "real" game. Hanzel had told me that I might be used in

Rochester. We were scheduled to be there for eight days 215

and I had got tickets for René for every game. I was determined that if I did pitch she should be there to see me. 

And she was. Toward the end of our stay Montreal, which was trailing in the league, came down for a fourgame series, of which Rochester won the first two. Then in the fourth inning of the third game, with both teams scoreless, our pitcher stopped a fierce line drive with his bare hand. The ball bruised his fingers painfully and caused such a contusion on one of them that he had to leave the game. Hanzel, after a brief conference with the manager, asked me to “warm up." At last my chance had arrived!

And under the most proud and fortunate circumstances, for not only was René in the grandstand, but Mother and Johnny were there also. Moreover, in a few days I would be eighteen years old.

There was no one out and a man on first base. Crowley, my catcher, signaled for a wide, fast ball in order to catch the runner if he should try to steal second base. I threw the ball, fast and straight, but not quite wide enough. The batter reached out for it and drove a terrific hit into the outfield. Excellent fielding alone prevented the runner from reaching home.

For a moment I stood dazed, not realizing what had hit me, so great was the shock, the shame and disappointment. 

The Montreal coaches began an uproar of derision and gibes. My knees began to tremble with the thought of what might happen to me. Two men on base and not a single out! 

And the first ball I had pitched-hit for a two-bagger! 

Crowley walked out to me and told me not to worry. "You'll fan this next guy out easy, kid!" he encouraged. "And then we'll be all set for a nice double play! Take your time now and don't let them birds get your goat!"

Not very often, perhaps, does it happen that the plans 216

which one makes, whether in the fortuitousness of baseball or the capriciousness of life, unfold themselves precisely according to our intentions. But occasionally they do; and on the strength of those occasions we go on renewing our faith in the magic of concentration. Indeed, sometimes we make a religion out of it. If you think a thing hard enough, we say, you are surely bound to realize it. And: "There is no life, truth, intelligence or substance in matter; all is infinite mind and its infinite manifestation, for . . . etc." 

And thus do our most fantastic beliefs continue to be born out of coincidence.

And thus also in that critical fourth inning of that seemingly all-important baseball game in Rochester, when by the amazing combination of fickle circumstances, the next batter in front of me struck out, and the following hit into a double play, so great was the impression made upon me, so fervent was my gratitude, that I immediately decided, in the throes of deep emotion, that thenceforth and forever I would place my faith in the power of the mind. And with that decision there came an illusion of progress, selfsatisfying and high-flown-as though I had discovered quite independently the one last truth necessary to insure me of happiness, permanent and inevitable. 

However, before the end of the game that "truth" seemed far less infallible than it had at the moment I had discovered it. For after applying all my powers of concentration to the task of hitting a home run, I was chagrined to learn that I could not even hit the ball to say nothing of walloping it out of the infield. And though our team finally won the game, all of my mental focusing and straining had not prevented Montreal from scoring four runs though it had been my most determined intention to keep them from scoring. 

But the praise I received! The elation I felt, at the end 217

of that game! In the clubhouse the manager patted me on the back and said, "Nice work! You're going to come along fine." The other members of the team, who had previously ignored me, now smiled sincerely and warmly shook my hand. And when René looked up at me as I met her outside, a new light shone in her eyes and her voice choked with pride, while for the first time in my life Mathew appeared to greet me as an equal. And Johnny, little Johnny! struggling with the recent dignity of his thirteenth birthday, seized my hand speechlessly and hugged it against his cheek. All the way home I heard nothing but praise. Life,

I thought, could never again be unhappy. 

Nobody recalls that baseball game today except meafter all that praise, and that supreme awareness of feeling that I was a hero. I wonder today as I listen over the radio to the roar of the crowds and the screams of approval if those football fans or baseball fans realize how an athlete feels after he has left the limelight of "heroism"-especially if that blinding light has been of short enough duration that he has not grown tired of it! 

"Vanity of vanities . . . all is vanity." When the light has faded it is like the sun gone down. And darkness, anonymity, insignificance and neglect settle down on that man like a black, unending night. 218

CHAPTER XVII 

THAT evening there was more celebration. Johnny brought around three of his school companions after supper, and before it grew dark I had played "catch" with each one of them in turn. People whom I did not know and some whom I had never seen before stopped as they came along the street to congratulate me. Johnny and his companions were very careful to see that no likely baseball fan above the age of ten should pass by without knowing who I was and what I had done that afternoon.

Later that night I could not sleep from the sheer excitement and joy which lingered in my memory. The light of a full moon came pouring in through our window. Weary but happy I lay awake listening to René breathing-counting her breaths and meditating upon the happiness she had brought me.

Then, just as the intoxication of the night was beginning to subside René suddenly awakened with a cry of surprise and lifted herself on her elbow with a startled look on her face. "What's the matter, darling?" I asked. “Oh, it's nothing, I guess. I just thought I had a pain.”

A little while later she was sure of it, and I went into Mother's room and asked her what to do. In the drowsiness of such a very early awakening Mother still smiled reassuringly, and followed me into our bedroom. She leaned over 

René and tenderly smoothed her forehead, as though she felt in this hour that René needed more kindness and love 219

than anything else. Although René was a French Canadian and consequently a "foreigner" whom Mother had hoped I would not marry, she was now to bear her first child, and the first grandchild in the family-away from her own people, her church, her friends, her mother. In Mother's eyes such an event made all others appear insignificant. She began methodically and cheerfully to prepare for it. Along toward morning she sent me for the doctor.

By the time I returned Father had awakened, and was now smiling happily at the prospect of a grandson. Ruth was in the kitchen cheerily getting breakfast, singing over the bubbling oatmeal, glad that for one day, at least, she would not have to go to the factory, but could stay home and help with things in such an atmosphere of expectancy. 

The doctor examined René and said: "Well, young man, it will be several hours yet. I'll leave a list of things which you should get and I'll be back around noon." 

At ten o'clock I made a hurried trip out to the baseball park and told Hanzel the news. He smiled very warmheartedly and squeezed my hand with friendly grasp. "That's fine, my boy!" he exclaimed. "We'll all congratulate you this evening when it's all over. Now you run right back before that first baseman of yours is born! We won't need you here today. But we're leaving town tonight, you know, and you will have to go along with us." He grinned encouragingly. "Everything will be O.K. by that time, though. Report down at the hotel at six o'clock." As I was leaving he shouted, "Don't forget the cigars!"

When I got back to the house the doctor was there. 

René's pains had been so frequent and intense that Mother had had to call him before noon. As I was hurrying upstairs to see her an ominous, stifled cry came from the bedroom. 

René crying! It hardly seemed possible! The strangeness, 220

the portentousness, the agony of that voice sent icy shivers down my spine. What a terrible pain it must be, to make

René cry out-she who normally suffered the worst torments in silence. I rushed into her room without knocking on the door. The doctor, bending over the bed with viciouslooking forceps in his hands, looked up at me annoyed. "All right," he said; "now that you're in here, you can stay a few minutes; but right now this room is no place for a husband." 

Mother was holding René's hand and soothing her fevered brow with cologne water. I seized her other hand and kissed her flushed lips. Painfully I felt the need of doing something more. As her fingers dug into mine in the spasms of pain I asked myself: "Why, oh, why can't I share some of her torture-I who am so strong and who have known so little physical pain?" But my question was as futile as it was simple. Those slim fingers again drove themselves excruciatingly into the palm of my hand and perspiration poured from her slender convulsed body. And I was helpless. The doctor motioned for me to go. René protested. "Don't leave me, Peter! Please don't leave me!" There was an unwonted tone of fear in her voice that struck terror into my whole being. God! Suppose something should happen. suppose she should die! Suddenly I knew that despite anything the doctor might say I wouldn't leave her side. The doctor seemed to divine my resolution, for with a look of resignation he proceeded with his brutal instrument-pulling and perspiring with a very human sort of powerlessness. René writhed and very visibly weakened. Presently the doctor laid down his forceps and gave her a hypodermic. Then he looked at me and forced a smile on his face. "Well, young man," he said, "you're looking kind of pale. 221

Don't you think you'd better leave the room before I have another patient on my hands?" "No," I replied; "I shall stay here."

He patted me on the shoulder and went back to his forceps. Mother looked haggard and glanced at me uneasily, but of all of us she concealed best the presentiment that we all felt.

Long before the baby was born its life had silently been despaired of. When it was finally delivered, scarred and inanimate, the ghastly reality was partially mitigated by a certain sense of relief. A short-lived relief, for it immediately became apparent that René was suffering from a very severe hemorrhage. And once again the doctor struggled futilely this time in an effort to stem the flowing blood. 

It was late in the afternoon before he would dare leave her. Calling me aside he spoke with unusual gentleness. "She'll probably pull through," he said, "if nothing else develops. But stick by her," he added, "for she needs you right now. I'm going to my office, and I'll be back in two hours."

When the doctor returned he brought another physician with him. But despite consultations René gradually grew weaker. Her face became more pale and peaceful. When she opened her eyes they were luminous and calm. The doctor had asked her not to talk, but when those beautiful eyes looked at me there was no need of words. They were pouring out their love-before she had to say good-by. Presently she summoned a brave smile and shaped her lips for a kiss. 

I leaned over and kissed her and she pressed my hand weakly.

Father had come home and I heard him talking to some one out in the hall. Johnny, innocent of the true state of 

René's condition, began to practice on his 'cello down in the 222

sitting-room, and then suddenly stopped. Mother got up from her chair at the other side of the bed and went out into the hall. Presently she came back bringing Father along with her. Coming over toward me and putting his arm around my shoulder, he whisperingly asked me if I didn't want him to call the minister. Then, bending over the bed, he kissed René on the forehead.

After he had gone I told René what he had said. She forced another smile to her lips and shook her head. Mother, who had been calm until that moment, finally started to cry, but each fresh sob seemed to be accompanied by a determination that it would be the last. We left the side of the bed for a moment while the doctors again examined her. 

There was the sound of more footsteps out in the hall and Mother looked apprehensively toward the door. Her crying had stopped now and she calmly asked me if I didn't think René might still get some comfort from a priest. But René again shook her head. Closing her big eyes she seemed to go to sleep. Mother knelt down beside the bed and began to pray.

Hoping that there was a God I, too, started to pray silently. A few moments later René began to choke and I took her in my arms. Weakly her head leaned against my shoulder and she breathed her last breath. I closed my eyes and tried to stop thinking. Presently the doctor came over and patted me on the shoulder. "Go away!" I exclaimed bitterly. "She doesn't need you any more." "I know, I know, my son. But in our profession it is always necessary to make a last examination."

I walked into Mother's room and threw myself upon the bed. I could not think. I could only cry. Nothing else mat- 223

tered! Nothing could have mattered! When Mother came into the room and gently told me to be brave, a feeling came over me almost akin to wonderment-as to who she was and what she was saying. During those recurring seconds in which some instinct would come welling up within me, endeavoring time after time to start me back on the road to emotional equilibrium, my wretchedness would have none of it, but would plunge more hopelessly into unrestrained weeping. 

Suddenly I stopped. The tears would come no more!

There was momentary relief until a new emotion overpowered me. This time, as before, I was scarcely aware of anything around me. I got up from the bed and walked out into the hall and went downstairs. Though I passed several people they meant nothing to me. I took my hat from the hall rack and went out into the May evening. The street which I had known so well seemed strange and foreign. 

All the world was remote. I kept on walking. Presently some one came alongside of me and put his arm through mine. I did not even look to see who it was. We walked along silently for a long time, coming at last to Maplewood Park. There the odor of lilacs penetrated my senses and suddenly wrested me from my state of apathy. The memory of the yard in Ogdensburg with its lilac bushes came rushing into my consciousness, and I turned to see who it was who was grasping my arm so affectionately. 

It was Mathew! For nearly an hour he had not said a word. Now as we walked along he started to talk about baseball, interrupting but once to say: "I'm sorry, Peter, about what has happened; but no matter what happens we simply must go on." "I don't want to go on!" I exclaimed bitterly. "I don't give a goddam about going on!" 224

Nevertheless after the funeral I joined the baseball team in Providence. Two days after my return I was knocked out of the pitcher's box during my first inning of relief duty. There followed several games which were equally disastrous. But back in Rochester several weeks later, just before the expiration of my contract, I pitched five innings and our team won the game. However, by the middle of 

June the manager seemed quite willing to let me go.

Mr. O'Donnell came around and offered me a position on his team, reassuring me that next year I would get another chance with the Hustlers. But suddenly it seemed to me as though I must leave Rochester for a long, long time. The city was filled with phantoms reminding me of René: stores where we had shopped together; the baseball park where on that unforgettable day she had looked up at me with that special glance of pride; the living-room at Mother's house where she had played the piano so gaily-and had lain in a flower-covered casket on a certain May afternoon; the dismal street of our rooming-house along which I would now find myself occasionally walking, for no other purpose than the hope that by so doing I might bring back more vividly the memory of our happy hours there together. 

I had to get away from Rochester! A dim instinct echoed within me that life must go on.

It was the middle of June and, like an old friend, undying, loyal and sympathetic, the St. Lawrence beckoned. I hoped that I might find some town near the Thousand Islands where I could get a job on a baseball team.

In the end I took a train for Watertown. 

And there, quite magically, I seemed to enter a new world. The town was only a few miles from the St. Law- 225

rence, somnolent, pleasant, inviting. I went to see the backers of the town baseball team, an unpretentious organization, having a brief summer schedule calling for two games a week (on Saturday and Sunday), and was received with flattering eagerness after I had shown them the newspaper clippings of the games played with Rochester. They hired me, and for a few days I wandered about the beautiful town alone, peacefully and lazily absorbing its summer loveliness. The past gradually vanished into a world aparta world in which I had once lived but had now put behind me. I wrote to no one. No one back in Rochester knew where I was. The pleasure of playing baseball again became an anodyne of forgetfulness.

After the first two games, which the Watertown team won, and for which I received an undue amount of credit, there were many eager and congenial companions who took it upon themselves to see that I did not suffer from loneliness. Although I received but ten dollars each week, I still had enough money left after paying funeral expenses in Rochester to make it unnecessary for me to get a supplementary job at least for a few weeks. So my days were spent in fishing trips down to Sacketts' Harbor; games and idle talk at the poolroom of the Woodruff House; pleasant evenings at the bar in the Leroy Hotel (where I finally learned to like beer); occasional poker games with some of the town's petty politicians; dances at the Lodge Halls and several indifferent flirtations. The weeks flew by pleasantly enough, though I was conscious of feeling very meagerly; life indeed seemed to go by in an insensitive sort of daze. 

One Sunday afternoon in the midst of a very difficult game I felt a severe twinge of pain in my left knee which greatly hampered my pitching. Between our innings in the field one of the fellows rubbed it thoroughly with liniment, 226

but the pain still persisted and I finally had to leave the game.

That evening I went to a dance and became more conscious of the irritation. The next morning I consulted a doctor, who diagnosed the trouble very vaguely as rheumatism, assuring me that exercise would not aggravate it, but adding the warning that dampness and exposure would. 

Leaving his office I felt considerably better. 

But on the following Saturday when I started to warm up for the afternoon game the sharp pain returned, and nothing seemed to alleviate it. After the first inning I reluctantly retired to the bench. The next day was a repetition of the first; and as a result that evening I received no money. Finding myself nearly broke and apparently unable to play baseball for a while, the difficulty of my situation suddenly forced itself into my consciousness, and for the first time in weeks the daze of my pleasant indolence vanished like a charming illusion.

Vanity would not permit me to seek another job in Watertown, where I had, during all those weeks, been more or less an idol of the town sports. But on the other hand not enough money remained to take me very far away. The possibility of borrowing some was equally precluded by my vanity, since I had made no effort to correct the impression-made when I had first come to town—that 

I possessed all the money I needed. That very evening I accepted an invitation to play poker in order to forestall any suspicion that I had suddenly become broke, and when in the game I had lost all that I had left I excused myself quietly on the pretext that I wanted to go to bed. Back at the hotel I became tangled and dejected in the toils of my dilemma. Not enough money to buy breakfast and a week's hotel bill to pay! 227

The next morning I was up early and started wandering aimlessly around the public square. A placard, advertising for enlistments in the Marine Corps attracted my attention, suddenly seeming to offer the only solution to my difficulty. 

I stopped to ask questions of the recruiting sergeant, who luringly convinced me of the charm and excitement of life in the Marines. I was just about to accompany him to his headquarters for an examination when one of my companions of the evening before came along and hailed me. 

Quickly leaving the sergeant with a promise to return later, I joined my companion, who presently revealed that he was on his way to breakfast. Upon learning that I had not yet eaten he asked me to join him. I accepted very casually, concealing an eagerness I was ashamed of. 

During breakfast an inspiration came to me to tell my acquaintance that I was leaving town-". . . going back to 

Rochester to consult my own doctor about my knee. . ." 

I glibly explained.

When we had separated I realized that now I had to leave town, for within a few hours the news would have spread among the rest of my friends.

It was a morning of strange coincidences. On my way back to see the Marine Corps sergeant I passed by the farmers' sheds, and there on another placard I saw an advertisement for "farm laborers to help with harvesting." 

And suddenly there flashed upon me the thought that if

I could only work for a few weeks and earn some money, my knee might by that time be all right and I could come back to Watertown to finish the season. 

I went into the sheds and hired myself out to the first farmer for two dollars a day and board. Then hurrying back to the hotel I quickly packed my suitcase, explaining to the clerk, whom I had grown to know very well, that 228

I was going to Rochester for a few days' visit and would be back before the end of the week. 

The farmer for whom I worked lived about six miles from Watertown. There in seclusion and agony I labored for two weeks. The pain in my knee continued spasmodically; but when it visited me there was no doubt as to its severity. At the end of the two weeks I received twentyfour dollars, packed my suitcase again, caught a ride over to Adams and took a train to Buffalo. I had planned vaguely to get some sort of a job there, and to visit a doctor as soon as possible. 229

CHAPTER XVIII 

BUFFALO was a hideous city that winter-a city of gloomy clouds and black coal-smoke, of dismal furnished rooms. and men out of work, of weekly visits to a doctor and of pain not only unalleviated but spreading into my other knee and finally into my ankles.

My first jobs were in the kitchens of restaurants. I graduated at length into the long hours of agony of being a streetcar conductor, limping through crowded cars collecting fares... wondering self-accusingly if God was punishing me for my sins. 

Weary at night, the pain in my knees would still awaken me; and in the daze of first awakening I would wonder where René was. Why wasn't she in bed with me? ... where had she gone? . . . at first not believing that she would not soon be back in my arms. And then the shock of realization! . . . My stomach seemed filled with lead, and cold perspiration poured out of my body. The lurid stale air of the furnished room was permeated with bitterness and depression. It was impossible to cry, for there was not a soul to give me sympathy. Tomorrow, I would say to myself, I'll take the first train back to Rochester.

But with the next day there came work, and a fresh determination to stick it out a little longer.

One evening I went into a restaurant where I had never been before. As I entered the door a waitress flashed by me, leaving only time for a glimpse at her profile. My heart beat wildly and for a second I was breathless! In that 231

instant of her passing I had caught a fleeting suggestion of René! Bewildered, I stood there waiting for her to come into view again. And when she did, I followed her and sat down at her table. Upon closer scrutiny the illusion quickly vanished, but a certain magic still attached itself to the girl, and a mad, irresistible desire impelled me to talk to her. When she came up to take my order I spoke to her bluntly. “When you get through work,” I said, “will you go to a movie with me?" "I'm sorry, but I couldn't," she answered, with a cool politeness which discouraged any further entreaty. 

Throughout the rest of the meal I kept staring at her, hoping that she might see in my countenance, or divine from some sort of mental telepathy, the yearning which I had, just to talk to her a while. . . the need in my loneliness to tell some one my troubles. But if she looked at me at all it was with a queer sort of expression as though she thought that I was half mad. Oh, why, I complained mentally, can't she understand what I want! If I could only explain to her that she has reminded me of René! 

Feeling more depressed than ever I reluctantly left the restaurant and wandered "home" to my furnished room. 

Along the dark gray street a prostitute accosted me. Hungrily I stood, listening to her professional words of love. "Come along, honey, and I'll give you a good time.” 

I stared at her wonderingly and then gently asked her if she would come to my room. "Not on your life, dearie; I couldn't do that." "But I'm lonesome," I said. "I just want some one to talk to."

In the murky light from the street-lamp I tried hard to look into her face, to see if there were any sign of sympathy. 232

But her eyes seemed opaque and her countenance quite expressionless, giving a general impression of damp, coarse hair, and a horrible streak of red across a bluishly powdered face. 

I tried to explain to her: "You see, my wife died a few months ago, and it's terribly lonely without her. I don't know a soul in the city. I'll pay you just the same," 

I pleaded, "if you'll only come up to my room." .. • 

Her eyes surveyed me coldly and her voice suddenly became hard. "Say, what do you take me for? Do you think 

I look that dumb?" And backing away from me she hurried timorously down the street. 2 The doctor finally warned me that I would have to stay off my feet for a while. "Haven't you got some place you could go for a couple months, until this irritation goes out of your joints?" he asked.

That was the excuse I had been looking for for so long. 

Weary of fighting, struggling against I knew not what, I had wanted to go back to Rochester, but had been waiting for some authority, higher than that of my own desire, that would impel me to go-an authority which would serve as an excuse for my wishes, and thereby absolve me from the feeling that I had given in to a weakness. For somewhere, in the dimmest regions of my consciousness, the notion had again possessed me that all the disasters which had been pouring down upon my life were the result of that arrogant resolution made on that evening in Ogdensburg: the resolution to do whatever I wished and willingly take the consequences. Now, having found the consequences so bitter, 233

I had at last become eager to capitulate. The craven philosophy of religious penitence had again laid hold of me, and I must look for other excuses than my wishes to justify every action. 234

CHAPTER XIX 

ARRIVING in Rochester late one night I found that the family had moved, and having no idea of their new address, and feeling completely exhausted from the increasing insistence of the pain in my knees, I went to a cheap hotel and slept until noon the next day. When, after many inquiries I finally found my parents running a shabby restaurant on Exchange Street, I listened to a recital of poverty and discouragement. Father had lost his job many months before. 

Mathew had taken Johnny to live with him in order that he might be able to continue in school. Ruth was waiting on table in the restaurant, while Mother was working in the kitchen. Father, who had spent his lifetime outdoors in the free and ennobling activities of farming and building, had been forced by poverty to put on a white apron and stand in virtual confinement behind a scurvy lunchcounter. Somehow all of them were just managing to make a living and to keep little Jenny in school. 

And I had come into the midst of such a scene crippled with rheumatism, looking for help, expecting to stay with

Mother and Father-to live on them until my knees got better! 

Yet Mother welcomed me and tried to keep me in bed.

Father listened sympathetically to my difficulties and sent me to a doctor. And when the doctor recommended an operation he took me to the hospital. 

Father had gone and the nurse had finally left me. I was alone. Alone in a small white room which smelled 235

rather comfortingly at first of reassuring antiseptics—an odor so pregnant of promises and miracles to the innocent. 

The room was bare and depressingly chaste, and the quietness that surrounded it seemed to accentuate my isolation from the rest of the world. The pain, as though suddenly embodied with human intelligence, began to relent in its severity in the face of an imminent operation. (Perhaps my mental suffering had forced it into the background.) 

Thoughts kept gnawing at me with an insuppressible stubbornness: thoughts of Mother and Father and Ruth working in a restaurant-the wretchedness they had gone through, to which I had undoubtedly added during those months of silence and apparent indifference; thoughts of the future, fearfully wondering if they would have to go on doing such work for the rest of their lives. . . if I would ever be able to play baseball again . . . how I could possibly endure to be cooped up for two weeks in this tiresomely white room, and the bitter helplessness of my position. And then above the comparative stillness of the hospital, the occasional faint rattle of dishes, a closing of a door, the subdued voices along the corridors, there came the sound of a violin, playing a soft lament which seemed to completely express the mood I was in. A sense of ease slowly, imperceptibly, stole over my whole being. It was as though some one else had penetrated into the depths of my misery, as though some one had experienced and grown sad from suffering like my own, and had then emerged to express it in music. Neither knowing, nor caring, why I should suddenly feel transported from unrest to contentment, I gave up my senses to the enchantment of the moment. Some one had known! Some one had 236

felt! And sensing that, made life at once seem less bitter and cruel. The awareness visited me without permission or explanation; and like all art which exercises its magic over us it soothed and invigorated without asking to be analyzed.

The daughter of Doctor Low, in whose private hospital I was staying, had never studied psychology, but she liked to play on the violin. She had never had pointed out to her in any academic way the effects which various kinds of music have upon especial moods. But with the instinct of an artist she responded to her environment. The voice of her violin began by expressing the pervading spirit of the hospital, then slowly and gently it modulated into a major harmony, while the chords of its piano accompaniment gradually vibrated with suggestions of calm courage. The mood of the hospital changed, rising from self-pity and despair to acceptance and serenity.

The doctor's daughter was slowly dying from multiple sclerosis; the doctor himself suffered from asthma. I thought it strange then that doctors, who know so much about the mysteries of life, could still fall victims to any of its diseases. 

I went to sleep and dreamed-dreamed of being pursued by a whole army of Marine sergeants, who were laughing at me cruelly while they brandished their fixed bayonets. 

Limping along desperately I finally fell exhausted, bumping my knees against a jagged rock. God! how they hurt! I couldn't get up! The sergeants were upon me, running their bayonets through my knees and kicking me in the shoulder! I screamed and shouted in horror! And suddenly

I awakened. A nurse was shaking my shoulder and telling me that she had come to prepare me for my operation. 237

My mouth and throat were parched. My brain was burning. My legs seemed to be enveloped with fiery hot lead -and a sickening pain gnawed at my secret flesh. Jesus! 

Jesus! What are they doing? Killing me by inches? ... burning me up? I opened my eyes. The nurse was rubbing my forehead with alcohol. "A drink, please,” I murmured. But she shook her head. "Not just yet, sonny, but here's some cracked ice." I held the ice in my mouth and my head began to clear. But the burning in my knees seemed to grow worse as my senses slowly awakened. It was as though gas-jets had been lighted under them and were now being turned on full force. "I can't stand it!" I moaned, rolling my head from side to side. 

The nurse stopped soothing my forehead and began to rub alcohol on my arm. "Oh, you'll be all right," she said softly. "Just try to keep quiet for a little while." She left the bed for a moment and came back with a needle. "Hold still for a moment, please. . . ." And she jabbed the needle into my arm. I kept on munching cracked ice and presently felt better.

After a while the nurse went to the door and Father, Mathew and Johnny came into the room, with boxes of flowers which Mother and Ruth had sent. Mathew had some cigars for me . . . "when you feel better and are able to smoke them," he explained. Johnny looked at me timidly, curiously, and asked how I felt. "Like hell!" I exclaimed, and Father glanced disapprovingly. "Did they cut you very much?" said Johnny as he stared at the mountainous contraption which surrounded my knees. "Jesus,” I said, looking at Father defiantly, "it feels as though they made hamburger of my legs and then lighted a fire under them." 238

The doctor came in and smiled ingratiatingly at my callers, felt of my pulse and examined the temperature chart which the nurse handed him. Presently every one left the room except the nurse, who gave me another hypodermic and held one of Mother's roses up close to my nose so I could smell it. "Give the flowers to some one else," I sighed; "they remind me of my wife's funeral." Unaware that I had been married, she considered my remark a very poorly attempted joke. "Honestly," I assured her, "I can't stand cut flowers."

She looked very displeased. "All right," she said patiently, "but don't try to kid me that you've ever been married."

I insisted that I had, and began to pour out to her all of the sadness of my feelings about René. She listened sympathetically and finally leaned over and brushed my cheek faintly and hurriedly with her lips. Then she left the room, taking the flowers with her.

Ten weeks later, on my nineteenth birthday, I left the hospital, walking with the aid of crutches, my knees being quite inflexible. Towards the end of that first wonderful week of freedom Johnny took me to a baseball game which his high school was playing on the university campus. It was early June and the sun shone with a caressing warmth.

The weather-beaten seats of the old wood stands seemed like old friends, comforting and welcoming-exuding mellow traditions of historic games and sentient years of happy scholastic rivalry. That day they were alive once more with the gay shouts of schoolboys. The atmosphere of the scene cast a spell of reminiscence over me-the crack of the bats, the smell of the warm earth, the movements of the players and the exhilaration of the game suffused me again with a bitter-sweet bliss. When, if ever, would I be playing base- 239

ball again? And if it were never, what use was there to living? Except to help Father and Mother get out of that damned restaurant. Thank God, I thought, I was through with hospitals; in a few weeks more I would be able to go to work.

I sat in the front of the restaurant behind an untidy counter, cluttered with boxes of nickel cigars and a dilapidated cash register, taking in quarters for the meals which 

Mother and Ruth served, smoking cigarettes and wondering if my knees were ever going to loosen up and if those occasional twinges of pain which I felt in my shoulders were due entirely to the strain of using crutches. 

Mathew had brought over some textbooks on bookkeeping, which I opened at first with hopeful curiosity-and then closed discouragedly after my first bewildered attempt at study. I had never learned to study, was incapable of intellectual application. Such things, alas, were for smart people like Mathew, a graduate of Normal School who even maintained that he got pleasure out of reading long and serious books.

Still, Johnny of late had seemed to enjoy reading; not only reading, but even studying Latin; and not only studying, but even practicing on his 'cello. It was a strange thing, I thought, that a boy of fourteen should be interested in such things and yet like to play baseball. On his way to and from his 'cello lessons he would stop in occasionally, proudly slip the instrument out of the case and play some new étude he had just learned. “How do you like this?" he would ask enthusiastically, and his fingers would run over the strings as though they were dancing with delight. 

His eyes sparkled with a gleam I could not understand.

He would add: "I'm studying the thumb position now and 240

playing in the high school orchestra!" What strange things,

I thought, to become excited about! Sometimes he carried along with him a book which he was in the midst of reading "The Deerslayer" or "Cousin Bette," "Oliver Twist" or "Tom Jones." Discussing them eagerly he would tell me I ought to read them. "Now that you can't move around very much," he would say, "I should think you would like to read."

I felt that under the circumstances perhaps I might like to read; although ever since I had left school I had never been able to see any attractiveness in reading, as long as I was still able to move around and enjoy other things. Reading was for old people and scholars and-now since it had been called to my attention—perhaps invalids. I tried to get interested, but I had to give it up. The books that Johnny left me were much too serious and difficult to follow. I turned to adventure and sensational magazines; they at least were things I could understand and enjoy. 

But in the hours at night and in the many hours sitting there by the front window of the restaurant there was nothing that could prevent my mind from teeming, struggling in a smoky haze of unrest and inactivity, turning over and over in its feeble, inchoate way the unwieldy problems which life had thrust upon me. 

I was lost! 

And conditions grew worse. A young farmer from Norfolk-a small paper-mill town not far from Potsdam-had been corresponding with Ruth, and now he came to Rochester for a visit and stayed for two weeks. Before he returned there was a wedding ceremony, hastily celebrated in the little rooms above the restaurant. A girl from Ruth's 

Sunday School class played the nuptial march on the battered piano that poor old piano once so new when it 241

entered our house so proudly on one of those glorious Christmas Eves in Potsdam, which had followed the family in its many movings and now at last was very sadly out of tune. The minister of the church was there with his sanctimonious smile and read the service with a funereal voice. There were sandwiches and coffee from the restaurant, and a brave fruit salad-trying to look festive in the midst of irremedial gloom. Mother "bore up" cheerfully until the very end, but when her tears finally came they revealed all her woe, the dreary bitterness of losing “another one of her children."

School was out and Johnny came over to help in the restaurant, washed the dishes and waited on table, mopped the floors and tried to find time to practice on his 'cello. 

But as the summer wore on business fell off, bills accumulated and disaster threatened. It became more and more difficult to walk with crutches and the faint twinges in my shoulders had developed into a persistent pain. The whole world seemed to be pressing down upon me in a determined effort to keep me from moving. And without physical movement I was sure I would grow mad, since for me there was no other form of activity. Meanwhile out at the baseball park the Hustlers were winning another pennant, but I didn't have the heart to ask Father for money to go and see them play. Restiveness and discontent, anxiety and dejection! My God!-I wondered-when is this going to end!

Into the midst of despair there came a little excitement, which helped us all to forget for a moment the trials of our own lives. Bold headlines in the newspapers announced that Germany had declared war. For weeks the sheer incredibility of it fascinated me to the exclusion of everything 242

else. While people all around deplored such a cataclysm I secretly hoped that the excitement would continue, for with innocent brutality I felt very grateful for anything at all that could take my mind away from myself. Indeed, deep within me an acute wish was born, too impossible, it seemed, ever to be realized, but tremendously enticing. 

If only our country could get into it! How much more thrilling it would be! Forced to the sidelines in my own little game of life, I craved, just so much more intensely, to witness a tumultuous show. But by the time school opened in the fall and Johnny went back to learn in his Cæsar class that: Omni populi Belga fortissimi sunt, I was feeling that my own battles in life were not so gigantic, after all, and that by reading of other people's courage my own had been immeasurably strengthened. 

Late that fall a letter from Ruth told Father of rumors of many new buildings to be erected in Norfolk, and added that though he had lost all of his money and was no longer able "to do contracting" he might "just possibly" get a job on one of them as a superintendent of construction.

Years before, Father had done work in Norfolk, where he was well known. So he wrote to some of the "parties" whom he knew, inquiring about the rumors, and after a few weeks of negotiations he had elicited the promise of a job. Once more the smile of triumph beamed upon Father's face. And the undying optimism, which through the years of poverty and hardship in Rochester had merely been slumbering, awakened once more with its original freshness and practically the same innocence. Off with the apron and on with the best suit, while he strutted proudly in front of the restaurant owner and told him that he would have to leave. "Yes, you understand, I just took this tem- 243

porarily. I'm a building contractor and I've got to get back to my work. There's a big contract in the northern part of the State, on which they're holding off work until I can get there. Yes, I've built a good many buildings in my life, I can tell you .. big ones too . . . and now that

I'm fifty years old I'm not done yet either . . . I expect to build a good many more. I could never git started in 

Rochester because here everything is politics. And that's one thing I won't get mixed up in if I never get a job." 

So the furniture was packed in the truck and we said farewell to Rochester. Johnny would remain at Mathew's until he finished high school. But as for myself, I was a dependent, crippled with rheumatism, who must needs follow Father, back to the neighborhood in which I had spent my early youth, and from which only a few years before I had struck out so vaingloriously. 244

CHAPTER XX 

THE brick house into which we moved in Norfolk sat up on a high ridge overlooking the Racquette River. Situated on the outskirts of the village it commanded a view of the surrounding country and looked down in a detached sort of way across the river toward the rest of the town. It was large and spacious, with a porch clear across the front, from which in the morning one could see the sun rise over the hills across the valley. From the rear of the main part, like the tail of a letter T, extended a small brick gable elongated by a capacious woodshed and summer kitchen at whose extreme end, of course, there was the customary outhouse. Along the north of the brick gable there was another porch, from which on summer evenings one could see the setting sun. Upon these porches, when the weather was pleasant, I began the day and ended it, greeting the sun in the morning shortly after it had climbed the eastern hills, and saying farewell to it in the evening as it sank its russet glow into the distant waters of the St. Lawrence. 

In that house I descended into the deepest depths of Hell, and within those same brick walls I somehow managed to emerge again.

That year I was confined at last to the wheelchair and bed.
