My physical environment was limited to the world of the living-room-where Mother had placed my cot so that I might have the company of the people and activities of downstairs—and the two porches. If I traveled outside of those worlds the journeys had to be made in my imagination. From my wheelchair on the front porch I could hear the sound of the hammers as they drove nail after nail 245

into the new "block" which Father was building down on the Main Street, and when Father would come up to the house for dinner and supper he would tell me of the progress which the work was making. The frame was up the roof was on . . . the floors were being laid ... the trim was being put on and the doors were being hung. 

Through it all I led the life of a vicarious builder. "Gee, Dad," I would say, "I only wish I was up on my feet, so 

I could be down there helping you." 

Never before had physical activity seemed so attractive to me. It had always been attractive-indeed, I had enjoyed doing things with my muscles to the exclusion of almost everything else, I could do things with my hands which other people found awkward or impossible, my coordination was excellent; but never had activity seemed so necessary, so consciously alluring, so maddeningly irresistible as it did now that it was denied me. The hope that 

I would soon be on my feet loaned encouragement for a while in the gloom of my confinement, but after months had dragged by with not a glimmer of improvement, that same hope had to borrow from other emotions in order to keep itself alive. It could no longer lend. The excitements of war, moving, Father's new job, new friends, vague prospects, had all at length to grow stale and wearisome. The dry fever of unrest was gnawing at my vitals. 

I must get interested in something! I must do something!

But there was nothing I could do-nothing that could hold my interest save the yearning (which became a mania) to get better and to get out of bed. 

At Easter vacation Johnny came up from Rochester for a visit. A great deal of his time he spent by the side of my bed or wheelchair. During another large part of it he practiced his 'cello close by, calling my attention to the “nice little phrases" and playing them over and over again— 246

because they always sounded so grand. Talking incessantly about the things he was studying, the things he was learning, his startling discoveries in the world of knowledge, he radiated an atmosphere of enthusiasm and eagerness which was both contagious and exhilarating. He would insist that he was going to teach me German-though he had studied it for scarcely a year himself. He would intersperse his conversation with the language in order that I might learn as much as possible before he had to leave. In the evening, when the family was all gathered in the living-room, and Father would begin to make inquirieswhich he supposed were very subtle-about the progress of Johnny's spiritual and moral welfare in his new home with the atheistic Mathew, Johnny would answer him with upsetting questions about the Bible in relation to history or certain dark and disgraceful episodes in the records of the Christian religion. The array of his knowledge filled me with wonder and I listened to him with pride and amazement. It would be great, I thought, to possess knowledge like that, to know things about history-and to be able to read German. Hell! Why couldn't I know things as he did? That certainly ought to be interesting! But there was no one to teach me. How could I learn anything without a teacher?

After Johnny had left, the house seemed desolate. He had brought such a spirit of gayety and excitement into it, talking enthusiastically about so many things; always bubbling over with energy and interest, he had surrounded our lives with an atmosphere of youth and expectancy. Now that he was gone Mother would talk about his "brightness," how quickly he had learned things, and how "smart" he had become. "The only thing is," she would say, “I think it's a turrible thing that he's drifting away from the Church, and Mathew encouraging him with all his 247

heathen ideas." She would sigh very sadly and then go on: "I don't know why it is that all my children don't seem to care about religion. Why, if people outside hear them talk and don't know any better, they'd take their parents for regular infidels. Yes, they would!" "It's all right," Father would add, “for children to be smart, but when they get to the point where they think they know more than their father and mother, then I think their education is a pretty sorry business. Pretty soon then they'll be believing that they know pretty near as much as God. And if they go much further they begin to say there ain't any God. Then if they get that far there's no help for them! They're lost! Educated fools-that's all they are!"

But as for me, Johnny had put a spark in my mind which kept on smoldering after he left. He had seemed to get so much excitement and fun out of just reading, and thinking about ideas, that I began to suspect that such things might even interest me. He had kept encouraging me to try to read some books and promised to send some in which I might be interested. Perhaps I might learn something if I had to stay in bed much longer. Johnny had mentioned so many events of history which had sounded very interesting. I could read history! Why not? I had liked it quite well when I was in school. Then, when he came up to see me again, I could talk with him about such things!

He sent me some books and urged me to start at once to read them: a Life of John Fiske, of whom I had never before heard; "Talks to Teachers on Psychology," and "Talks to Students on Life's Ideals," by William James; "Be Good To Yourself," by Orison Swett Marden; and an "Outline of Ancient History," by Webster. Included 248

with these were several copies of the American Magazine and Current Opinion. I looked at them with a new curiosity, not expecting that they would furnish me with enjoyment, but hoping that by studying them hard enough I could gain some of the knowledge which Johnny possessed and seemed to make so exciting. 

The magazines were all right, with their stories of success and heroic struggles against handicaps. I could have gone on reading them. They were interesting and inspiring; but the information which they gave me amounted to almost nothing. Once cast aside they were soon forgotten. 

Anodynes for the moment, they left me in the same state of ignorance with which I came to them. Still, they did this much: they showed me that it was possible to enjoy reading at least some things.

With the books there were complications; it was almost impossible to get interested in them. Going from one to the other before any of them was very well started-a fickleness of attention, caused by the vain hope that the "next one" would be more interesting-they all became confusion and an arduous task to continue. To make matters worse, my shoulders-irritated, I supposed, by the strain of holding the book in front of me as I lay in bed --began to pain again, and thus enforce me into further idleness.

Moreover, when I stopped reading the pain did not cease, but grew in intensity and spread to my elbows. Finally one day Father brought a doctor from Ogdensburg to see me, a famous surgeon who was universally worshiped in the community and considered a veritable miracle man. 

He talked with me for a while, asking questions about my past life: "When did you feel the first pain in your knees? 

Did you ever see Christy Mathewson pitch? What do you 249

think about the war? Did you run around with the girls very much? Ever had gonorrhea? How are your teeth? 

Ever had trouble with your tonsils?" And examining me carefully he asked about the operation on my knees. “Who did that butchery? Ye gods, what a job! Did he tell you 

Iwhat he did it for? Heavens! what could he have been thinking about? Absolutely unnecessary! Did you a great deal of harm and couldn't possibly have done you any good." He straightened up from the bed and looked at me with a sad sort of seriousness. "Well, son," he said, “I could tell you some bad news. Can you take it all right and not let it bother you? I think you can or I wouldn't have even mentioned it to you." 

My heart beat more rapidly, but not from disquietude.

Bad news was usually exciting, and for the moment I anticipated the excitement with pleasure. "Shoot," I said. "I guess I can stand anything you can tell me." "Well, my boy, you have what we doctors have long considered a practically incurable disease called arthritis. 

Now, don't misunderstand me—it's not always incurable.

You may be one of the lucky ones to get rid of it; but your chances are very small. This ankylosis which has taken place in your knees is now beginning in your shoulders, and from there it may go-and it often does-to every joint in your body, making them fibrous-freezing them so you can't move." The doctor continued: "I'm telling you this now, so you can prepare for what is before you.

Now, don't give up hope, because medical science doesn't know everything yet, by a long way. Next month, next week, even tomorrow, some one might discover a means of curing you completely. Always think of that. But meanwhile it's wise to prepare yourself for the worst. If you know the worst that can likely happen you can sometimes 250

make ready to get the best of it. In your case, for instance,”—the doctor smiled encouragingly-"if you can't use your body any more you can concentrate on your mind. 

And I wouldn't be surprised if you had a pretty fine organ there to work with." He gazed at the books on the chair beside the bed. "Fine books you're reading," he commented; "they'll help you develop your mind. Hm! William James! Now there's as good a book as you'll ever find. I get down his Psychology every year and read the chapter on Habit. So you're reading that, eh? Well, then, I'm not going to worry a bit about you. You'll get along. 

I'll make a bet on that.”

The doctor remembered his more customary professional routine and drew out his fountain-pen. "Now," he went on, "I'm going to prescribe a little morphine for you when the pain gets too annoying. You may not have much pain. In that case you won't have to bother about it. But if you do, this will fix you up while it lasts. I'll explain to your mother about using the hypodermic needle and she can give you half a grain at night during the periods that you have your pains. They won't last very long-and they may not come at all. Why, next summer who knows but what you may be up playing baseball! Keep a stiff upper lip, and I'll be back to see you before long."

It took me a long time to realize fully the import of what the doctor had told me. His words, his manner, his magnetic and invigorating personality, had so hypnotized me that for a long time after he had gone I felt tremendously stimulated rather than depressed. He had almost made me believe that I was about to undertake an heroic career.

But pain visited me again and like an obliterating curtain shut out the past with its hopes and resolutions, its 251

brave point of view and confident plans, binding me to the moment, the all-absorbing moment of physical sensibility. And once more I realized how effectively the stress of the struggle can kill the desire to become a hero. Courage is one thing when considered sentimentally, but something else when some practical occasion calls for the exercise of it. 

After the first use of the hypodermic needle I became very willing to resort to it again; at first only when the pain was severe, and then gradually when I felt the least uncomfortable. During the day I tried again reading books, but it went very slowly and seemed to grow less and less worth while. Finally I gave them up entirely and depended for my reading upon the ephemeral anodynes of the magazines. Morphine and magazines became the drugs of my forgetfulness: indulgences which were in the beginning accompanied by acute pangs of conscience, chosen as they were despite an inner warning, and in lieu of sufficient courage to accept a little suffering and to triumph over it.

Most people, as did I, look upon pain as something to be avoided at all costs, a condition wholly evil and willingly accepted only by masochists; not realizing that it is the safeguard of both our spiritual and our physical life, the impetus of most of our progress, as the ability to feel it is a good gage of our degree of civilization. Pain refines our senses and prepares them for ecstasy; it warns us of dross in both our body and our spirit. It challenges our mind, giving it the choice of subtilization or oblivion. But, like pleasure, it need be neither sought after nor indiscriminately accepted. It is not a panacea for salvation, but a warning that we must save ourselves. It cannot lead us into a ready-made Heaven, but it can-though it may not -endow us with a sense of tragedy. Its warnings will never bring us peace, but they may help us to discover beauty. 252

CHAPTER XXI 

In the middle hours of the night, when every one else was asleep, I would lie awake with every nerve of my body taut for action-tightened for an activity I could never engage in. 

Have you ever felt like smashing something . . . crashing a window or knocking over a skyscraper, for no other reason than that you simply must do it? Have your nerves ever been possessed with the mad desire to destroy everything, to jump with wild abandon and wave your arms fiercely in the harmless and impalpable air? Have you ever imagined how you would feel to itch all over and yet be unable to move to alleviate the torture? If you have, you know a slight intimation of the madness I felt. But

I could not even give vent to the annihilating impulse of pounding the side of the bed. I could not move! I could only lie there waiting in suspense for my body to explode and tear itself to pieces in madness.

The torture could be tranquillized by morphine-and it must be tranquillized. The only alternative was utter insanity. So at first desperately and reluctantly, though finally with no compunction-I would call to Mother and she would give me a hypodermic. First a quarter-grain ... then a half . . . and a whole! Until at length I was taking five or six grains a day. It ceased being a lure; it became an inexorable compulsion-an addiction no longer involving choice or reason-a necessity for which I would 253

unhesitatingly have murdered, or as unhesitatingly sacrificed my otherwise wretched life. 

Under Mother's ministration that needle of mercy poured heavenly release into the tortured nerves of my body. To that sensation there is nothing comparable in man's normal life. It was like anguish raised to the nth degree and then suddenly reduced to the infinity of nothing. It was like unendurable continency ending in divine consummation. 

And what a consummation! The tenuous insouciance of morphine! Losing one's body and becoming an exalted and superior intellect! Elevated to divinity and a cloudless serenity upon which no trouble of the world can possibly impinge. In this state of euphoria all problems of this world and the next are solved with instant despatch. There is no agony, no dread; just a paradise of elation, a superhuman activity of the mind, overruling all other feelings with a sense of superiority. And then there is sleep.

In the first hour of awakening I felt the torpor of indifference, an invulnerable lassitude. Then slowly, gradually, my nerves would awaken and within the fevered fibers of my muscles the desire to move again was born. 

It would mount to a passion: a passion which could never be fulfilled and which carried ever in its wake the acute sense of frustration. Still I vowed that I would never again ask for "the needle"; not because of the bliss which it brought me-indeed not-but because that bliss made the rest of life seem so unbearably hideous. I vowed-but before the all-conquering morphine no man-made vow can exist for more than a few hours. In the daytime I was crushed by the burden of idleness; at night the burden was the same, but it must be carried through the darkness. 

Slowly, relentlessly, the irritation would travel until at last my mind would succumb to the inevitable temptation. 254

"Mother! Mother!" I would call. "Will you please step here a minute?”

Mother would come and look at me sadly, her face gloomy with discouragement and pity. "I have a terrible pain. Will you give me some morphine, please?" "But, Peter," she would plead, "it's hardly four hours since you had the last. And the doctor, you know,—well, you remember what he said." "Please! Please!" I would cry impatiently. "It's terrible! 

I can't stand it! Give it to me now! The pain is terrible!” "All right." Her voice was muffled with discouragement, and she would leave forlornly to go into the kitchen. A couple of minutes would drag by like as many hours. "Jesus Christ! Mother! What are you doing! Hurry, for 

God's sake! I can't stand this pain!"

But there was no pain-save the agony of desire. No pain, that is, except the tension of my nerves, which must find release or suddenly shatter themselves, and the trembling of my stomach, which fluttered like a leaf.

Yet man has an instinct for self-preservation. When all else fails that instinct works alone, bare handed. Am I going to let myself die in the bliss of drugs? There had been -there still were many occasions when I would have welcomed death; but, like all other desires, even the yearning for death passes with the hours. If you try suicide at this moment and peradventure it fails, tomorrow or next week you may be searching for the secret of longevity. 

There are few things in heaven and earth more mutable than the desire for self-effacement. 

The great physician from Ogdensburg said: "If you give up morphine it will kill you!"

The modest country doctor who called on me each week 255

assured me that if I didn't give up morphine I would be as good as dead within a year. 

A dilemma with two horns-both apparently fatal. 

Am I going to let myself die in the bliss of drugs? What better death. "Could man be drunk forever 

With liquor, love, or fights.” 

Why not live in Paradise unto death; in that glorious chancery of dreams? Why not be intoxicated with morphine forever, and live in that ethereal domain “beyond life"? "But men at whiles are sober 

And think by fits and starts."

And when I thought (I do not know where those thoughts came from, unless they were distilled by that instinct of self-preservation; a perverse instinct, indeed, to sweep me back into a life of pain and agony in preference to a Paradise of bliss followed by an easy death) I thought solemnly and decided that I would face the torture of denial.

A curious thing, man, who works and struggles for Utopias and Paradise, which if realized would bore him to extinction; who strives and slaves for charm and easeand when they are achieved restlessly yearns for the primordial and savage; who in the midst of peace would be irked by the lack of intensity and danger; who thinks he wants reforms whereas his actual delight is in the struggle he makes toward effecting them. 

Away with artificial dreams and drunkenness; the good of all artificial schemes fades and pales! I'll give up mor- 256

phine! I'll intoxicate myself with Pain! Such was my lawless fancy.

But the all-conquering morphine made ludicrous my resolve. While it suffused my veins I was the Zeno of Stoics; but when its magic deserted me I became the most craven of mendicants.

Yet I had sworn in the presence of Mother, in one of those moments of morphinous fortitude, that I would take no more of the drug. I had commanded her not to give it to me. Thus the burden which I could not carry came to rest wholly upon her shoulders. She listened to my complaints, my threats, my abuse, my curses; and at length from sheer pity would give me infrequent hypodermics.

The months dragged on, bringing with them the faint suspicion of a curious change in my condition. The hope was even born that I might snatch victory from the very jaws of death.

Then, one day in the agony of unrest as I was begging shamelessly for the assuaging balm, Mother came to the side of my cot and gazed down at me with a curiously calm smile. Yet her calmness could not quite conceal a certain triumph and eagerness in her manner as though she had been keeping a secret for a long time, and now wanted to tell me. "Peter," she said with a sigh of relief, "I'm not going to give you another drop of morphine. You don't need it, and you've got along without it for three whole months." 

Got along without it for three months? My God! what was she talking about! "What do you mean?" I snarled. "Just what I said," she replied with the suggestion of amusement in her voice. "You may not believe it, but 257

it's the truth: unbeknown to you I have been substituting warm milk for morphine in all the injections I have been giving you for the last three months, and you've been just as well satisfied as though it had been morphine." 

At first I couldn't believe it; yet I knew that my mother would not deliberately lie to me. Then the thought that it might be true made me tremble with anger. In the mood I was in I was blind to the benefits I had derived from the subterfuge; I was sure of one thing: that I had been the victim of a cruel joke. "I don't believe it!" I cried. "You can't fool me! Give me another injection, for Christ's sake!" 

Mother remained immovable, and answered my abuse with gentle persuasion. "It's true, Peter," she said, “and you've never known the difference. Time after time you've said what a relief it was, when all I used was milk. So it can't be you need it. Now you just try for a little while and see if you can't get along without even the milk. 

Probably your greatest trouble now is your imagination." 258

CHAPTER XXII 

MOTHER was the heroine. She cured me of the morphine habit! 

Belatedly I, too, felt a certain sense of triumph. And because of that sense I became vastly invigorated, even getting up enough courage to make another attempt at reading. 

When a man cannot possibly occupy himself in any other capacity he is sometimes driven to contemplation. 

He may even be swept into the unnatural activity of thinking-that is to say, he may become guilty of that rare crime against Nature upon which the whole march of evolution depends. I was so moved-purely by that process of Nature which had reduced all other activities to an impossibility. This does not say much for the widespread potentialities of mental awakening. Indeed, it implies that the growth of the mind, the acquisition of wisdom, depend largely upon the experience of adversity. Let it be so! 

Adversity is the essential catalytic for mental fermentthough no insurance of it. For the hypersensitive organism the necessary degree of adversity is naturally less, for it requires less. Such an organism feels more. A true genius sees the world at a different angle from that of his fellows. 

That is his adversity-a tragedy which drives him to suicide or immortal expression.

For a whole year and a half I had not heard from Johnny, and I supposed that when he learned about my taking morphine he had lost all hope for me. But now that I had emerged from the addiction with the determination to seek respite in the acquisition of knowledge, my 259

mind inevitably returned to thoughts of him who had first attempted to arouse my interest in "learning." I wanted to write Johnny a letter, but a certain vain pride made me decide to wait-wait until I had made some progress with my fresh resolution.

Thus I began again-this time with a novel of Balzac's which turned out to be surprisingly interesting. Indeed, by the time I had finished the two novels which Johnny had left me I had become fascinated, feeling that I had become acquainted with a "new" people and a “new” country. Paris now seemed to exist familiarly and wonderfully in my imagination, and the life of Parisians rich, free, and exciting beyond anything that could exist in tiresome America. I was drawn to the glamour of life as 

Balzac described it, though I had, of course, not the slightest glimmer of the art which gave that life meaning and beauty. But there did begin to dawn upon me the faint suspicion of why other people enjoyed reading. 

Then one day the village doctor, proud and pleased at my triumph over morphine, sent me a copy of De Quincey's "Confessions of an Opium Eater." With a very natural curiosity I opened it and began to read. That a book could be so interesting I had never even dreamed! Yet my interest in the story did not seem nearly so novel and strange to me as the circumstance of my conscious concern and delight with the manner in which it was told. Never before had I been aware of such a thing as style, not to mention such a curious association as that of scholarship and enjoyment. In De Quincey I felt the first dim glow of pleasure in sheer writing-though of course I did not understand quite how the essay charmed me. I only knew that I felt it and had not the wit to ask why. With all of the unusual elements of the mere story I was personally and 260

physically familiar. But they alone could not lay claim to my captivation. But extraordinary as my feeling of appreciation was, it was just a faint spark-in comparison with the conflagration to follow. 

I picked up William James' "Talks to Teachers on Psychology" and turned to the chapter on the laws of habit: "The great thing in all education is to make our nervous system our ally instead of our enemy."

How peculiarly sentient I was to that idea after what I had gone through! The simplicity and sheer wisdom of it nearly lifted me from my cot in one grand ascent of exultation. But that was not all; one tremendous idea after another came flashing up to me from the pages of the essay. I came to the quotation from Darwin: "If I had to live my life again, I would have made a rule to read some poetry and listen to some music at least once every week, for perhaps the loss of these tastes is the loss of happiness, and may possibly be injurious to the intellect, and more probably to the moral character, by enfeebling the emotional part of our nature.” 

I immediately resolved that the very next day I would begin to cultivate an enjoyment of poetry and music. 

I was learning! I was learning! Now, past twenty, I had at last discovered what it meant to have an enthusiasm for knowledge. Naively, credulously, I had begun to burn with a studious zeal. 

And how fiercely I burned when I looked into the essay "On a Certain Blindness in Human Beings"! "Our judgments concerning the worth of things, big or little, depend on the feelings the things arouse in us." 261

God! How true that was! How many times had I felt inchoately the same thing, without being able to express it!

Many years have passed since I first read that sentence of William James'; but the magic still is in it-even as the magic is still in the sound of his name: a name that will ever be associated in my mind with my birth into a new life. 

Fascinated, I read further! But the sensations of those moments are incommunicable. I would stop for a moment intoxicated by the magic of some idea, tasting it, savoring it, drinking in the fluid joy of conscious enlightenment. 

Reading such things as I had never before dreamed, I wanted to shout to the whole world the discoveries I was making. I would call Mother from the kitchen and insist that she listen while I read to her one choice paragraph after another. I tried to tell her of the great things that I felt. "Ten times more wonderful than playing baseball!" 

I would exclaim. "And what is more, the pleasure lasts!

It accumulates, promising infinite and infinitely greater felicity!" 

Mother would smile, but not without appreciation. "My goodness, that's a pretty tall order," she would observe, and she would look at me with a faint expression of wonderment.

It was an Epoch in my life. A "Higher Vision" had been granted to me! 

After William James, I had to write Johnny a letter! I simply couldn't wait until his next visit to apprise him of the strange and magnificent things that were happening to me. I wrote to him in the rhapsody of elation which filled me, with all the biggest and superlative and flowery words at my command. And thus began a correspondence which acquainted me with new and wonderful delights. 262

CHAPTER XXIII 

JOHNNY had fallen under the spell of a Unitarian minister, a graduate of Harvard and a fearless scholar. Now, in his last year of high school, he had won honors in scholarship and distinguished himself as a 'cellist-all this in addition to earning a large part of his expenses by playing professionally for weddings, banquets and occasional dances. 

I was proud of his achievements, but I wondered, a little apprehensively, if with these main preoccupations he might not have forgotten me—or at least have no time to answer my letter. But my apprehensions were groundless. A few days after I had written him I received an answer. And such an answer! Page after page of enthusiasm and encouragement, words of advice and paragraphs of inspiration.

Occasionally in the midst of our greatest happiness we are on the verge of tears-tears of joy. Such an occasion had visited me. There was something in Johnny's letter, something not revealed in the words or the sentences, which yet communicated that joy to me. Perhaps it was that we had been drawn together in a common interest: the pursuit of knowledge. Perhaps, sharing with him the glorious zeal for learning, I felt for the first time an association which was unutterably wonderful. 

Johnny sent me a set of the Harvard Classics. When the books arrived it was like a great day of celebration. Mother opened the box and Jenny carefully unpacked them, reading the title of each volume as she withdrew it from the 263

case. I could not eat, so absorbed was I in that promised land of red and gold. Suddenly I wanted passionately to become a "cultivated man."

Previously, whenever I read a thing that I did not understand I willingly passed it by without pausing to discover its meaning. But now there had come over me the desire to understand everything I read; to be an assiduous student; to have no loose threads dangling, incomplete, from any subject once covered.

If there was the slightest doubt about a word, I looked it up, went into its etymology as far as the dictionary would carry me, in an endeavor to unearth its exact meaning. It was a long time before I came to distinguish between knowledge and understanding. But finally one day the distinction dawned upon me, while reading a paragraph in Gibbon's "Decline and Fall of the Roman Empire." I suddenly realized that beneath all the words, the facts, the opinions, which were coming up from that printed page, through that incredibly wonderful mechanism of the optic nerves, into my cerebrum, and, there encountering infinite patterns and associations, were being finally and almost instantly transmuted into ideas. The ideas were being enriched and elaborated by comparison with other ideas already there; beneath all that surface process of consciousness there was still an inner something: a subtle, intangible understanding which Gibbon possessed, but yet could not put into words. There was a meaning between the lines which, if it were to be grasped at all, must be evolved by the reader from the impalpable substance before himan understanding without which the highest element of anything written, painted, sculptured, built or sung, is lost: a tenuous attribute beneath the visible patina of any creation. It was impossible to understand anything by just look- 264

ing at it! The spectator's judgment was sure to miss the root of the matter and to possess little truth. "It is a wonderful achievement," I said to myself, ". . . to be able to distinguish between knowledge and understanding." 265

CHAPTER XXIV 

THUS my adventure proceeded-an adventure, thrilling, glorious, unutterably wonderful. For months I read eight hours a day: the Harvard Classics, Gibbon, Voltaire, Nietzsche, H. G. Wells, Shaw, Galsworthy, Royce and a host of other Olympians. I Promessi Sposi, of which I had expected so much, I could not read-nor the Divina Comedia, try as I would with a constrained sense of duty and fairness. The Koran disappointed me. But upon re-reading the Bible, especially the Psalms and Ecclesiastes, I discovered a wholly unexpected charm and beauty. Milton's prose I liked, but his poetry put me to sleep. And after Nietzsche it was almost impossible to read any other philosopher except Voltaire. As for Hazlitt and Huxley, I swallowed them voraciously, eternally hungering for knowledge and more knowledge-consumed with desire for that last Truth which would set me free. 

Early in my days—and nights—with the Harvard Classics I had read the "Autobiography of John Stuart Mill"read it and succumbed! Mill became my hero and prophet, a prophet toward whom my allegiance could not for a long time be shaken, despite my naïve fondness for undigested "Nietzsche." Mill's essay "On Liberty" had been a major event in my life. And a few years later when specious arguments in favor of the Eighteenth Amendment were sweeping over this "free" country like a tidal wave of distortion I took particular delight in epitomizing my opposition to them with those magnificent lines: ". . . The sole end for which mankind are warranted, individually or collectively, 267

in interfering with the liberty of action of any of their number, is self-protection . . . the only purpose for which power can be rightfully exercised over any member of a civilized community, against his will, is to prevent harm to others. His own good, either physical or moral, is not a sufficient warrant." I was very proud of that borrowed wisdom-that is, until I later became imbued with Socialism. 

Those glorious days! In the midst of the war boom, which gave color to a household that had so long been concerned with the drab struggle for a living, a building contract in Potsdam fell into Father's hands and the family moved back to the beloved town of my childhood. Johnny had at length joined the army and been made a lieutenant, but his new activities did not prevent him from writing me long letters from training camps, describing the strange new life of a soldier-and of how in the Camp Library at Fort Sill he had unearthed-of all things-copies of Nietzsche, Treitzsche, and even Bernhardi... and of how, in an army camp, he had personally discovered the poet of poets: Percy Bysshe Shelley. 

Mathew was in France leading an army band, discovering the virtues of Cognac, and developing a great appreciation for the French people and their habits. His character seemed to have changed curiously, to have developed an inspiring freshness, to have grown more human. He wrote letters to me which did not even mention Success or Economic Independence, but which were full of gaiety and adventure, hinting even at times of a timid, fledgling, tentative respect for poetry and romance. "These people," he wrote of the French, "have something that we don't possess-and it is wonderful!"

To all of us, indeed, the war seemed to bring a prodigious 268

invigoration. The atmosphere at home was imbued with fresh energy and excitement. Letters from France and faroff Oklahoma created an interest in worlds outside of our own-an interest which, once begun, carried us farther and farther toward new and larger horizons. The patterns of our lives became more complex, rich, and inclusive. 

That summer I lay on my cot on the porch of the house at Potsdam, breathing in the aroma of honeysuckle and listening to the cries of the youths as they played their games of baseball on that same field where years before I had felt the thrill of victory. I lay there, reading, reading, with a continual glow of enthusiasm and happiness—an enthusiasm which would carry itself way into the night, as I re-made the world, its morals, its religion, its education, its philosophy.

For I had become a philosopher-so I thought-and 

Johnny now headed my letters with "Dear Zarathustra."

Those years unfolded themselves happily, serenely. The war ended and I became twenty-six. Again, in search of a job, Father had moved the family-this time to Watertown, New York. Johnny had married and was living in 

Rochester. Mathew had returned from Europe to the staid, prudent routine of his professional career. President Wilson had come back, defeated yet unresigned, from the "Ver sailles Conference of Spoils." The Utopia of World Peace and Understanding, toward which I had looked forward as the great justification and outcome of the war, appeared more and more fantastic-a cruel delusion that had visited a dreamer immersed in the optimism of H. G. Wells.

The world was in confusion and would not listen to reason nor be guided by intelligence. Watertown itself seemed a conglomeration of war millionaires, parvenus, upstarts, greedy dentists and papermill owners of incredible vul- 269

garity, gossipy wives, girls whose minds were the quintessence of vacuity, boys who for the lack of knowledge as to how to amuse themselves inevitably turned to viciousness and sadism. No one read in Watertown. They bought automobiles and boats, built bizarre houses and filled them with gaudy furniture. If they had time from their restless search for pleasure they would pick up the newspaper and run over the comic strips and society news and then hastily drop the sheet for fear their minds would begin to smoke. 

Indeed, it seemed that if one removed jealousy and greed from the list of Watertown's ruling passions there would be nothing left.

Bitterly I tried to dismiss them with what I considered a superior Nietzschean gesture; pronouncing mankind a vast herd rushing stupidly to destruction. The country was in the throes of another economic depression. It would ever be concerned almost wholly with food and sex. When surfeited with both it turned to material aggrandizement, and often sheer greed. All right! . . . Let it go!!

Thus I tried to cast aside humanity's difficulties. Confident of my own sagacity, certain that a world made up of intelligent individuals sharing my wise point of view would automatically be a pleasant and peaceful Utopia, I endeavored to live in dignified and superior isolation. There in my room full of books in Watertown I sought to view the world in a disinterested way. 

But I couldn't! The blood of the Evangelists was upon me. The world had to be saved! And its salvation-I concluded could be accomplished only by Education. I was especially susceptible to the promise of Education because I had dated my true birth into this world with the discovery of its virtues. I had been redeemed by the process of study. 270

So, to do my share of bringing knowledge to the ignorant, I appointed myself the "Apostle of Enlightenment." Sadly enough my mission was not auspiciously successful at home. Both Mother and Father had finally to be despaired of. They simply would not see the Light. 

Despite all my preaching and exhortation they resolutely clung to Methodism, Capitalism and the Republican Party. 

Not only did they not see the light, but Father retaliated by bringing the Methodist minister to call on me; thereby establishing a precedent which soon developed into a weekly visit and prayer by my bedside. It went on, strangely enough, to a friendly and interesting companionship.

The minister was a curious anomaly for Methodisma witty and eloquent Irishman by the name of Shannon.

For many weeks Dr. Shannon's visits were marked by my cordial antagonism-an antagonism aroused by what I considered a presumptuous, stupid, condescending exercise of his pastoral duties. I wanted always to tell him that I had neither need nor respect for his prayers or solicitations, that I considered him worse off than I was, immersed as he was in stupidity and hypocrisy. Indeed, when he was out of my presence I pronounced him a damned fool, and cursed my cowardice for failing to tell him so to his face. 

But when he had come the next week to greet me with his warm, ingratiating smile, I could not bring myself to believe that he was insincere, or his interest merely professional. For he would talk to me for a few moments in the vernacular of good-fellowship, with a good-humored friendliness that weakened my skepticism. He would fill my pipe and light it for me, though I knew that he was supposed to be opposed to the use of tobacco and alcohol in all of its forms. He would chat with me about baseball 271

and the progress of my Major League favorites, inquiring about my reading, and ask, more or less facetiously, what new arguments I had discovered against God and the Bible.

Small wonder, then, that in the face of such apparent reasonableness, I began gradually to doubt my superiority, and to look upon Dr. Shannon less as an adversary and more as a friend. He ceased to pray by my bedside, and 

I could not help feeling a little proud and triumphant. I loaned him copies of "The Soul of a Bishop," and Upton Sinclair's "The Profits of Religion" to read. When he returned the books to me he spoke generously and favorably of both of them-but still insisted that the greater part of humanity needed true religion and derived some benefit from it, even the church. And he considered that his greatest usefulness to the world resided in his remaining a minister.

At such moments a dim suspicion of our different temperaments assailed me. He felt something in religion. 

I did not. He felt that primordial instinct of humanity which, in this life of three parts sorrow, one part joy, hungers for respite-some promise of their unfound happiness-some escape from the hard realities of the world! I saw religion with the eyes of an embittered critic, one who had had what went under the name of religion forced down his throat since infancy, and whose interest in it now was historical, utilitarian and analytical. 

Diametrically opposed to religion was science. The battle between them was as natural as it was inevitable. Having left one side of the struggle it was very natural for me to join the other. Thus I had fallen out of the arms of religion into a faith in knowledge, had become imbued with the Socratic belief in the explicability of nature, and a trust in intelligence as a panacea. I boldly traded any illusion what- 272

ever for disillusion. I shunned defiantly myth and enchantment; and though I had at one time resolved to cultivate an increasing enjoyment of poetry, I now placed it in the category of naive and youthful interests. Where was the logic of poetry? Where the exact knowledge which would embrace the world of phenomena? Though I listened to music with increasing fascination I was inclined to regard it as merely a pleasant pastime. Intoxicated with the notion that the secret of existence could be expressed in a syllogism, I explained great music as being the purely sensual creation of super-intelligence. And whenever music became so great, so world-embracing that it disturbed my confidence in the possibility of ever expressing its meaning in cold, rational words, I decided that it was because I had not yet reached that high plane of intelligence which would enable me to do so. In brief, I had become a Platonist. 

And alas! I fear-vainly proud of my knowledge.

With Dr. Shannon religion was an instinct. He had the common touch that could not be denied. "Have your logic!" it seemed to say. "Humanity possesses something before and after logic, something that the lyric poets of the Bible came nearest to expressing . . . but dialecticians!! 

Never!... 'Dost the hawk fly by wisdom?" he would say.

So I did not win a convert to agnosticism in Dr. Shannon, but I gained a friend-a friend who called me Job and had staunch faith that I would repent. . . and that the latter part of my life "would be blessed by the Lord more than the beginning."

It was comfortable at last to think of him as a friend rather than a victim or a possible convert, and I began to flatter myself upon my liberal-mindedness. Here I, a militant agnostic was a close friend of the Methodist minister! 

That's real liberalism for you! But wasn't he also a liberal? 273

He no longer came to see me from any sense of duty. I was sure of that. Nor did he offer prayers for my salvation. The only religion we now discussed was that of human brotherhood-and that, at my instigation. What drew us together? . . . We were both interested in saving humanity. 

But more important than all this-we listened to music! 

To him Bach and Haydn were the Gods of Music; and their music, like the Psalms, expressed the universal religion. A strange mixture was Dr. Shannon-very strange. 

Born in Catholicism and brought up in a Catholic orphanage, he had run away as a youth and had been given refuge in the home of a physician, a man who was a bachelor and a Methodist. That physician must have been a most extraordinary Methodist, for he adopted the youth and brought him up without even attempting to proselyte or coerce him into the Protestant fold. 

Dr. Shannon worshipped his benefactor as a hero. Years later, after I had delved into the psychologies of Freud and Watson, I thought I understood why Dr. Shannon had gone into the Methodist ministry. And when upon the death of his benefactor he left the church, went to New York and in a few years became an internationally known journalist, I was sure of it. When he had-inevitablycome to the extreme points of the limited world of religion he still remained loyal to the man who had befriended him. Staring into the unfathomable universe from the secure and simple ground of religion's promises he, a noble and gifted man, had been eager to break away from the romance of Gods and Heavens and win for himself a new refuge, but he had seen the necessity which most of mankind has for simple illusions. 

Valete, Michael Shannon, ac plaudite. 274

CHAPTER XXV 

THE pains of arthritis were over. They had left me physically helpless though content. But the strain of so much reading began to take its toll upon my eyes. (At least we all thought it was the strain of too much reading that affected my vision; none of us had then heard of such things as arthritic cataracts.) For a while rest seemed to help them, but at length, and despite long periods of rest, they gradually and obviously grew worse. 

Suddenly a threat of blindness loomed upon the horizon, and slowly, gradually, began to uproot my contented habits and shake my happiness. When I could not read or talk to visitors a restiveness would attack and rob me of the serenity that I had struggled so hard to achieve. Then a dim suspicion came over me to the effect that perhaps I had been living, after all, in a world of books instead of that of reality-a very pleasant world indeed so long as one could remain in it. But now I was threatened with exile; for my existence in that world had depended upon my sense of sight.

At night when the sun would fade there was a sense of completeness momentarily come over the world—an interlude of meditation, a respite from doing and making, acting and acquiring, when one was satisfied to close one's eyes and view the world through the mists of memory and imagination. One was content. Let the world of sight and activity pass by! After supper when the lamps were 275

lighted I would enter into the world of my books. I had seen enough of the real world for today. 

But I could no longer feel that way. No matter how wonderful twilight was I could not stand it perpetually. I was avid to do and to see. Man needs new experiences, new sensations, new struggles to bring him to rest and contentment. I hungered to see, to feel, to exercise fully all of my senses. And now that one was being taken away from me it seemed as though one of death's lieutenants had come to rob me of part of my life.

I could not believe that such a thing could happen to me. Since I had been confined to bed I had discovered my new world, experienced some of my greatest joys. The joy which I had felt in the process-a process generally spoken of as my regeneration, and considered to have been an astounding transition from physical to mental activitygave birth to the notion that I had perhaps discovered the secret of the universe, solved the riddle of the Sphinx. But that world had been revealed to me through the instruments of other people's ideas. Whenever discouraged I had opened a book and thereby entered into that little private universe. And there I had lived and acted in the lofty atmosphere of Emerson's Essays, Whitman's "Leaves of 

Grass," William James' "Pragmatism" or Bellamy's "Looking Backward."

Mother had looked upon my reading as a pleasant diversion, a nice activity, beginning and ending in itself, for occupying my mind during hours, months and years of immobility. It was “something to do" like playing checkers or amusing oneself at the piano. Much as I had tried to impress upon her that I was gaining knowledge, that reading was merely a means toward that glorious end, her attitude toward my "convenient form of amusement" re- 276

mained unchanged. In her own life knowledge had been gained by experience precious little did one get out of books. · • · 

And I would often wonder if she were not clearly right.

For now in the increasing darkness I was driven, resistingly though inevitably, back into the labyrinths of discouragement and bitterness. There seemed to be truly nothing left in life. 

For a few minutes after her school was out Jenny would come to my room and read to me from my favorite philosopher, Nietzsche. “. . . To an intrinsically sound nature, illness may even act as a powerful stimulus to life, to an abundance of life Whatever does not kill an excellent man makes him stronger." While she read those lines I felt courage rising within me like a flood. I was defiant and happy. But not long after Jenny had left the room a smothering silence and blackness would again settle down upon me. In vain would I try to recapture the magic of: "Je fait cultiver mon jardin"-a sentence which, ever since reading "Candide," had been a miraculous talisman of inspiration and encouragement even in the lowest moments. It had lost its charm. How could I cultivate my garden now? How could I go on defying adversitychained to a bed, and blind! 2 In the midst of the gathering darkness came the news that Johnny had moved to New York to take a position in one of the large theater orchestras. He wrote me encouraging letters-letters which echoed the struggle of his own academic idealism against the encroaching materialism of the hard, vulgar occupation of professional music. 277

An artist at heart, the responsibilities of marriage, the pressure of earning a living by routine performance on his 'cello, made him just so much more defiant of the so-called "practical world." 

Also, in the turmoil of New York, Johnny found time to think of me in other connections. He sent me books and consulted medical specialists who were supposed to be interested in arthritis. And finally, while I had about reached the depths of melancholy, he sent word that he had made arrangements to bring me to New York, where I could receive the treatment of the great Doctor Luxow of Vienna, the distinguished scientist who had isolated, and discovered a serum for, the arthritic microbe. 

Once more into my world of apparently irremediable gloom and immobility there came a ray of hope. Congenitally an enthusiast I rode to New York on the wings of sanguine expectation. Mother would have reminded me that I was a very heavy and awkward piece of baggage, for she accompanied and took care of me in the very mundane drawing-room of a Pullman. Nevertheless my "spirit" soared toward liberation and light. Eight years of incarceration in a bed had not enchained it. The idea that there was hope, no matter how illogical and unsupported by evidence it was, had my heart in favor of it. 

Once I had thought that I wanted to commit suicide (a phenomenon which is always a product of reason and never of feeling), but my "heart" had been against that.

Later, when I had discovered a new life for myself in the adventure of learning, I looked back upon the thought of self-destruction as stupid and puerile, espousing the cause against youth and uncultivated instincts. But that same adventure of learning, which in the beginning had promised me Truth and Freedom, had led me in a cynical circle 278

back to the same life-destroying thought. This time that thought was cruelly supported by the knowledge I had gained, the disillusionment I had experienced. 

Indeed, I had discovered knowledge to be an extremely seductive and dangerous mistress, luring me on with the promise that nature could be explained, enticing me with the suggestion that in it alone resided the power of a universal panacea, leading me continually to new enigmas, profounder mysteries, and at length bringing me to the extreme points of its periphery from which I must stare, disillusioned and disenchanted, into the unfathomable. 

At those points knowledge coldly forsook me, leaving my hitherto despised instincts and emotions to battle with life's greatest enemy, the sense of futility. My instrument of intellection counseled: "Everything is futile! Destruction, neglect, oblivion awaits you and all your efforts!

Death is a relief which your base instincts and crude emotions alone keep you from embracing!" But again, I say, my "heart" was against it, defying reason in the spirit of revenge, and clinging to romance. I arrived in New York tenaciously hopeful... lyrically indifferent to the discouragements of logical evidence. 

Long, long before my journey to New York I had become aware-crushingly, unalleviably so-that I was now not only the black sheep of the family but also the white elephant. Inert, blind, unable to help myself in any physical way, my body weighed upon this world a useless, hopeless burden and an especially heavy burden upon the struggling backs of my mother and father, who had earned so much and won so little respite in their battle for existence. 

By no rational process with which I was familiar could I discover for myself any raison d'être. Shameful, bitter, I had denounced my very impulse to live. "Let the day 279

perish wherein I was born! ... Why died I not from the womb? . . . Wherefore is light given to him that is in misery? . . ." 

99 

Yet to New York I went, hopeful that I would see and move again! 280

CHAPTER XXVI JOHNNY met Mother and me at the Grand Central Station with an ambulance. We all rode in it to his apartment, where I was to stay for a week before going to the Central Hospital to be examined and treated by Doctor Luxow. 

Feeling Johnny's presence again did something to me. He had always given me the consoling awareness that my infirmities were unimportant, that they could be forgotten in his company, that all that mattered to him was my mind and spirit. He even conveyed the impression that I was a person of some importance. And now most of his “timeoff" from the theater and rehearsals he spent reading and talking to me. The strange noises of the city, the "elevateds," the taxis, the continual din of traffic, the suggestions of commerce, and all the material affairs of the world-all were shut out from our presence as he would sit there in the tiny room of the apartment reading to me from 

Havelock Ellis' "Dance of Life." 

Late into the night after he had returned from the theater he would put a soft needle on the Orthophonic Victrola and play records (which, with books, were his only extravagances) for my delectation. Over and over I listened to the Brahms First Symphony and was thereby transported into Paradise. Brahms and Bach and Havelock 

Ellis! How he loved them! Often as he was reading from

Ellis or listening to a Bach choral or prelude, tears would come into his eyes. I could not see them, but I knew they were there. "My God! Peter," he would exclaim, “if any- 281

body can be in the presence of such beauty and not tremble violently from the sheer joy, the tremendous triumph of it! . . . why, people must be crazy! ... they must be vegetables! . . . they're not alive! Why, that stuff conquers the universe! It conquers death! They talk about gods! 

Why, Jesus Christ! Brahms was a god if there ever was one! And so is Havelock Ellis! They make the world seem small in comparison, just as the world makes the rest of mankind appear insignificant!"

Johnny, too, was a rhapsodist. Perhaps we were both hereditarily disposed toward enthusiasm. I know I felt then pretty much as he did-let me listen to the Beethoven "Fifth" and I'd go through the tortures of hell and come out with a chuckle-and if I could have written it I too would have defied all the fates and thumbed my nose at eternity.

Such was the company that I should always be in-the joyful, life-furthering, exuberant company of the true gods of this earth. They were not intellectual, nor Ivory Tower philosophers; but vital, Promethean, fecund, glorious!

But in our journeys through those Elysian fields we were occasionally interrupted by the mundane complaints of 

Johnny's wife. A pretty girl, about four years younger than Johnny, blonde, attractive-and conscious of her dazzling attractiveness, which in her vanity she misconceived as beauty—she had eagerly sought to go on the stage shortly after Johnny had moved to New York. Her attempts had met with an early success and when I arrived she was proudly gracing the chorus of a revue on Broadway. Justly, perhaps, she resented my presence in New 

York, especially my presence in her own home. Moreover, her affection for her husband amounting to a very jealous and possessive infatuation, she was not particularly able 282

to bear his extended and nightly sessions with me imperturbably. She even despised books and hated to see a new one come into the apartment, because-she explained -Johnny was forever sticking his nose in them and never paying attention to her. "My Lord," she would grumble, "about the only time you ever realize that I'm alive is when you're in bed with me and I'm trying to get to sleep!"

Thus were the communions of amateur philosophers interrupted—interrupted by a wife who craved her husband's attentiveness . . . a glittering, avaricious girl whose interests and ambitions seemed to be as far removed from Johnny's as those of a banker from those of a scientist; whose ideals were as disparate, indeed, as those of a chorus girl and an artist. She disturbed our halcyon solitude and reminded me unpleasantly that this world had other aspects besides those which Johnny and I contemplated so loftily. 

She emanated the atmosphere of physical pleasures, the delight in doing things: dancing, traveling about the colorful city, playing baseball, walking in the Park, buying pretty clothes, making love, listening to praise and flattery, making money, showing-off, eagerly waiting and expecting people to tell you that you are beautiful—or that you are a wonderful baseball pitcher. 

I had first seen Johnny's wife, Sally, when she was sixteen, radiantly pretty, golden-haired, well-formed, alluring, with a voice that was, even then, rich and seductive. I still held that picture of her, somewhat enhanced by the enrichments of a fuller blossoming. Thus, when Johnny would finally leave my room to go to bed, my mind, having beguiled itself so happily for a few hours in the lofty reaches of philosophy and art, would ineluctably descend to the contemplation of the nocturnal joys and pleasures of Johnny's married life. 283

Alas! before and after philosophy men ate and men made love! Life was so biological! So preponderantly a matter of emotions and appetites! Why should I live with so much of life denied me? How was it possible to go on? In vain would I try to think of some paragraph from Ellis or Bertrand Russell, which would serve as a touchstone of contentment until I could get to sleep. But pictures of baseball fields and heroic contests kept floating before my eyesand visions of golden-haired, lithesome girls. Then in desperation I would call to Johnny. And when he at length came to my room I would ask him to play once more the second movement of the Brahms Second Symphony. He understood: placing the record on the Victrola, he would leave the room. Before the music had come to an end I had again been transported to another world, and it would not be long before I would calmly glide into sleep. 284

CHAPTER XXVII 

But those wonderful hours with Johnny soon came to an end-all too soon. I went to Central Hospital and was placed on exhibition before a noted doctor and sundry other specialists. After so long an absence from hospitals I was again "taken in" by its hocus-pocus of mystery, miracles and hope. Beneath all my acquired skepticism there still lingered in me a sanguine faith in the magic of medicine. Perhaps the great specialists were scientifically-minded after all. And though the ordinary practicing physician might be three parts charlatan to one part scientist, the great physician was probably another Pasteur whose predominating purpose was the advancement of knowledge. 

What a disappointment! The upshot of my conferences and consultations with not only the Viennese specialist, but many other famous physicians as well, was the dangling and sadistic assurance that something might be done for me if I only had enough money to pay for expensive treatment and equally expensive care. The chance that I might benefit from any of the régimes which they had conjured up was not promising enough to warrant their putting me through them without a better prospect of financial reward than I could give them. So the "specialists" began to avoid me and at length the hospital authorities sent me to Bellevue.

At Bellevue I became Exhibit Number Forty-five thousand-nine-hundred-and-something for the enlightenment of multitudinous internes and sundry visiting physi- 285

cians. Lecturers would expound the history and peculiarities of my case to bored and mildly curious disciples of Æsculapius. But if they ever tried any remedies upon me they were either so subtle that they escaped my attention completely, or so abstract that they would have required a Christian Scientist to appreciate them. As a matter of fact, the dark problems of my pathological condition remained as actually impenetrable to them as they were glibly solvable by their theories. The optical surgeons, intrigued by the opportunities for experiment which my arthritic cataracts offered, decided to try an operation. The result was that hemorrhages formed in my eyes and thus made the shadows before them more deeply opaque. 

At length, after my allotted six months had elapsed at Bellevue, I was discharged as an incurable and shipped as a matter of routine to City Hospital, on Welfare Island. 

Thus my hopeful journey to New York had ended in a charity hospital. 

That surely meant neglect and oblivion. There was no appeal from it. In this country of feverish activity and fierce competition wherein even a physically capable being often finds it difficult to maintain an existence and to overcome despair, what chance had I, utterly helpless, totally blind!

Johnny had done a great deal for me; but even he, after learning the verdict of the doctors, had seemed to wilt with discouragement. But not for long. "Pete," he had said on that first day of my transfer to 

City Hospital, "I can't help you financially any more just now. All that I have done has been done purely because 

I wanted to do it, without any sense of sacrifice or duty. 

You know I don't believe in altruism. Everything that every one does is primarily selfish. I helped you because I got a tremendous pleasure out of it. And right now my 286

most enlightened self-interest convinces me that the best thing to do is to save enough money to get you out of this hospital and keep you out. That'll take quite a while. If I took you to live with me now, Sally would leave me and very likely get a divorce. That would be foolish a sentimental sacrifice . . . because it wouldn't be long before you'd have to come back to this place, anyway. I admire you not because you're my brother, but because you've got a lot of courage and a very good mind . . . and because of the intellectual and artistic excursions we have shared together, and the encouragement your struggle has given me. I can't insult your courage and intelligence by making any such gesture as having you come to live with me now despite the consequences. . . . 99 Johnny was curious, and kind, and wise. There was a gentleness in his skepticism which seemed to respect the very things which he repudiated. He was never overconfident in his conclusions. His ear despised "jazz" so thoroughly that he would either suffer nauseatingly or go to sleep whenever he had to listen to it; yet he was forever defending it as the expression of the modern spirit in music. "Great musicians and great men," he would say, "have almost invariably been denounced by their contemporaries. I want to be tolerant at least."

Of course such words were the very sustenance of life for me, because in them there was the implication that at least one person in this world not only refused to consider me a burden, but even held me a kindred and invigorating spirit. That gave my life the justification which it so sorely needed during those first weeks at City Hospital. I had come into an existence so totally devoid of illumination that the handicaps of my previous plights seemed Utopian by comparison. And just as we never at any given 287

time appreciate our well-being and causes for joy until our circumstances have been reduced to conditions much worse, so I had not fully been aware of the happiness which still remained for me in the shelter of Mother's care despite my blindness and immobility. In youth we yearn for maturity, power, responsibility and fame. We know that we can never be contented without them. In maturity we sigh for peace and comfort and affection, a philosophy that will give us serenity. First I wanted to be the greatest baseball pitcher in the country. Not long after I would have been satisfied with much less fame and a little more assurance of physical comfort. Still later I asked merely to be able to move-without prowess, skill or strength. And then there had followed a period during which I would have begged and even prayed for the faculty of sight and the ability to hold a book, and counted myself the happiest man in the world had they been granted to me. At last I had come to the point of asking only one thing of life—an escape from 

City Hospital.

Man is a fascinating animal. No matter what the condition in which he lives he will always have Utopias dangling in front of him. The story of Adam and Eve is, after all, a story of one of mankind's greatest urges, poetically conceived and sung. Even in Paradise an excellent man yearns for that struggle which will bring him the sense of triumph! 

He is greedy for novelty in the midst of peace, and has a gusto for living in the midst of his wretchedness. For him there is no perfection-but nothing so imperfect that it cannot be borne. 288

CHAPTER XXVIII 

THOUGH City Hospital has its occasional comic relief, it is for the most part a sordid institution with only now and then a sparkle of contrasting illumination to light its gloomy corridors and amuse its patients. An excrementitious organ of the monstrous urban growth of New York, it is very naturally an important one. Its patients in general are made up of the derelicts of humanity which the city has so ruthlessly used and then cast aside. From the point of view of the young interne it is a handy place in which to wear off the awkward newness of his instruments and his degree; for the patients are neither hypochondriacs nor are they hypercritical. Through the eyes of the social worker the institution is a spectacle against which she must harden her heart or become the victim of a continual nightmare. To the patients the hospital often becomes a habit, like the practice of law, the profession of pedagogy, the art of saving souls or even like life itself. 

I arrived at City Hospital replete with knowledge gained from books, and possessed, beneath a temporary feeling of discouragement, of a philosophy: a half-baked, half-digested philosophy of nicely arranged premises and conclusionsthat is to say, a dialectic delight of words and ideas, a bookbound classification of the forces and purposes of the universe, together with a handy theory for turning the world into a Utopia. Having cast out God and castigated capitalists and kings, I held myself to be a free soul in the midst of physical imprisonment. Atheistic after Voltaire, self-reliant 289

after Emerson, passive and tender after the Beatitudes and Buddha, socialistic after Upton Sinclair, yet defiant and truculent after a misunderstood Nietzsche, I was very conscious of the appalling extent of my knowledge, still confident of the superiority of the mind over the body; yet hoping passionately nevertheless that something might happen to release me, and place me in an environment of scholars and philosophers. The desire to be appreciated still burned fiercely within me.

It was not long after my arrival at City Hospital before the anomalous nature of my case received a certain amount of curious attention. I was visited by the superintendent, various doctors, several nurses and numerous internes. I could scarcely have received more attention had I just been elected to high political office. They approached me with that "here-is-a-poor-fellow-who-has-suffered-incredibly-andis-still-optimistic" look; so I responded to their inquiries with appropriate morsels of the dear-sweet philosophy of the late Doctor Crane. Of course it met with considerable success. I was praised most effusively for my fortitude. It is true that no fortitude whatsoever was involved in my arrival at the hospital. Rather, it was a plain case of the inevitable. But because I smiled according to the prescribed and much advertised formula-and perhaps because I was neither illiterate nor alcoholic-I was accepted as one of those brave smile-when-everything-goes-wrong sorts of persons, who happened to have a tongue in his head. 

But man does not live by encomiums alone. In fact, there are certain hungers and several humors that reduce the importance of eulogies to a value somewhat less than nothing. And it was into one of those evil states I sank when the evening meal was announced by a strong stench from the ward serving-room. It needed more than a manu- 290

factured smile to face that meal with equanimity. A strong stomach would have been far more practical. There was liquid that was alleged to be vegetable soup-ugh!—stuff which the orderly who fed me called bread, three prunes that were just recognizable, and a mug of tea. After it was over I yearned to assuage what remained of my appetite with a few long draws on my pipe. But smoking was prohibited. Thus, in desperation, I turned to engage my neighbor in conversation. 

Though of course I could not see him, my neighbor's voice indicated that he was old, Irish, and good-natured.

He began to tell me his troubles. Addicted to alcohol, he had acquired an alcoholic ulcer on one of his legs. His kidneys and liver were defective. His abdomen and legs were swollen. He was friendless, up against it, and out of touch with his only relative. But all these troubles were as nothing to him compared to the fear he felt over the possibility of being sent to the City Home. "If I only could kape from goin' to that place!" he would wail.

Now, the City Home was a comfortable institution for the care of the old and indigent, in many respects more desirable and commodious as a place in which to live than City Hospital. So I was at a loss to understand this great woe over the thoughts of being sent there. But my solici tude gave way to amusement when I discovered that pride was at the bottom of it all-that the specter of becoming an out-and-out pauper was more dreaded than the shadow of death. This fear, I later learned, was general among the patients. So long as they were in the infirmary there was some excuse—at least they made the subtle distinctionfor their accepting the charity of the city. That they might not be subjects for medical treatment, or that City Home was a much more comfortable place in which to live, or that 291

senility was as good an excuse for accepting charity at the Home as it was at the Hospital-all these arguments had no effect upon that curious feeling, that dread fear that they have of being forced to go there. My neighbor, in common with most of the patients, was as incorrigibly proud as he was incurably hopeful. Thus I soon gave over my efforts to lay his most terrifying ghost, and settled back to ponder my new environment.

A conversation interrupted my thoughts. Some very feeble old man had asked the nurse to perform a service for him. 

She was replying to him, coldly, mercilessly. "How old are you?" she rapped out. "Seventy-eight," he replied. "Well, you're too old to be around here taking up room. 

You ought to be in the City Home."

Another old man, who had been a longshoreman for forty years, working harder in a physical way than a hundred ordinary people, and invalided to the hospital from muscular paralysis, asked the nurse to get him another cup of tea. "My God," she rapped out again in her most exasperated tone, "what's the matter with you? Why don't you get it yourself you can't expect a nurse to wait on you!"

I was experienced somewhat in physical pain, but I soon discovered that I knew almost nothing of mental cruelty. 

I had not dreamed that there were so many people in this world who could be so deliberately brutal to sad, broken derelicts who couldn't fight back.

So this was a sample of the new world in which I was going to have my being-a world in which I would probably spend a long, long time; perhaps even the rest of my life. Certainly I could never go back to live with Mother and Father. They were getting old and I had already been 292

a tremendous burden to their lives. As for Mathew, I had not heard from him for years. And Mark had now taken on the burden of supporting his wife's parents. Indeed, the economic world had yielded—and was still yielding-little more than a living to any member of the Holland family. 

The burden of my existence placed upon any one of them would have meant extreme sacrifice and at length, no doubt, a condition of insolvency.

But whether one's back is against the wall or against a cot in a charity hospital, if neglect and extinction stare him in the face, he has little inclination to occupy himself with the fruits of reason, namely: ethics, morals, or a contemplation of economic and social amenities. In the midst of adversity man does not retire to an Ivory Tower to meditate upon his uselessness to the world or the economic burden which his presence places upon it. He fights! He tugs and struggles! He has no time for precious inquiries into the vexed question of how and why or whether the game is worth the candle. He will put off those inquiries until the first taste of victory.

The instinct to fight in man preceded by a long, long time his capacity to rationalize that instinct. Indeed, even more primordial than that may be, as Nietzche has declared, the Will to Power-the will to triumph over death itself. 

Man may curse his fate and the injustices of life. Push him far enough however and such superficial-such mentalstuff as most criticisms are made of is straightway thrown overboard, and he becomes overwhelmingly conscious of the impulse to live-to fight against the forces that seek to annihilate him. I said "overwhelmingly conscious"; it is far more accurate to say that the consciousness is overwhelmed with the impulse to live.

Indeed, the period that I went through was not notably 293

a conscious state. I fought more or less unconsciously. And now I describe the experience retrospectively as a natural phenomenon of biology; an inchoate emotional struggle, rather than an intellectual one. 

Soon after I had resigned myself to immobility and blindness a centripetal force began to modulate my habits; with the result that other elements of my strength became indefinitely increased. 

I had come to City Hospital where life was so precarious that one made jokes about the dead and the moribundwhere sympathy and warmth and cheer were supplanted by contemptuous (paid for) administrations; where the walls were gray, and the stinks violent, and where the only laughter was that of the cynic. But what must the import of that world be upon one's soul? Must it desolate one of all beauty and visions of felicity? Must it leave one, as

Christian justice once left the forlorn bodies of its offenders, hanging on the gibbet-dead but not yet buried? I decided not.

Having resigned myself to the irremediable handicaps of my being, I discovered that I had the strength to cherish a little romanticism-even, indeed, to give birth to a sense of humor.

One evening a young Italian who had been brought into the hospital several days before with a serious liver condition, reached the stage of insupportable melancholy. Taking a safety razor from his bedside table, he walked quietly into the bathroom, sat down on the stool beside the tub and calmly cut his throat. He was discovered leaning over the bathtub with blood gushing from the wound. The doctors rushed him into the operating room and patched him up as well as was possible, but he died the following morning. At noon the orderly came around the ward demanding 294

"All safety razors, blades, scissors, and any sharp instruments." They were confiscated by order of the authorities. 

That night when every one else was quiet an Irishman sang out across the ward in a rich brogue: "Well, byes, I heard that some porr fellow jumped off the Quanesboro bridge this afternoon ... and now the authorities are goin' to tear the bridge down." 

I discovered that I was able to laugh. Moreover the impact of that Irishman's wit did something to me-did something very powerfully! At first, though I had laughedrather grimly-at his joke, I resented the levity of his attitude toward a poor fellow's suicide. But then the real "heart" of his remark began to dawn upon me. It wasn't that he had considered the death of the Italian particularly funny, but rather because he had been amused that the authorities should be so misguided as to presume that he or his companions would even think of committing suicide. "My God! What a joke!" his voice seemed to say. "They must be crazy if they think we'd knock ourselves off! Why,

Holy Mary! I've been through enough already to make a dozen doctors pass out, but I'm still kickin' and grinnin' no matter what happens!"

Naturally death was very common at City Hospital. The rattle of the metal cot, mounted on wheels, which was used to carry the deceased to the morgue, was a familiar sound which called for various comments. As it went clattering down the corridors or across the pavement some one would casually remark: "There goes another stiff," or "There goes another one out," or if the victim had been known to cause considerable trouble and been generally disagreeable, the 295

commentators would sigh: "There goes another nuisance."
